# PREFACE

Since the dawn of history, various extraordinary phenomena have been recorded as happening among human beings. Witnesses are not lacking in modern times to attest to the reality of such events, even in societies living under the full light of modern science. The vast majority of such evidence is unreliable, as it comes from ignorant, superstitious, or fraudulent persons. In many instances, these so-called miracles are merely imitations. But what do they imitate? It is not the sign of a candid and scientific mind to discard anything without proper investigation. Surface-level scientists, unable to explain various extraordinary mental phenomena, strive to ignore their very existence. They are, therefore, more blameworthy than those who think that their prayers are answered by a being, or beings, above the clouds, or those who believe that their petitions will make such beings change the course of the universe. The latter have the excuse of ignorance, or at least of a false system of education in their childhood which has taught them to depend upon such beings for help; this dependence has now become a part of their degenerate nature. The former—the scientists—have no such excuse.

For thousands of years, such phenomena have been investigated, studied, and categorized. The entire scope of the human religious faculty has been analyzed, and the practical result is the science of <term>Rāja Yoga</term> <note>Literally "Royal Union" or the "Royal Path"; a system of meditation and self-control aimed at realizing the ultimate reality.</note>. <term>Rāja Yoga</term> does not, in the unpardonable manner of some modern scientists, deny the existence of facts which are very difficult to explain. On the other hand, it gently, yet in no uncertain terms, tells the superstitious that miracles, answers to prayers, and the powers of faith—though true as facts—are not

<summary>The author argues that extraordinary mental phenomena should be studied scientifically rather than dismissed or attributed to supernatural beings, introducing Rāja Yoga as the practical science behind these occurrences.</summary>

<keywords>Rāja Yoga, miracles, scientific method, superstition, mental phenomena, religious faculty, prayer</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introduction to the philosophy of Yoga, specifically addressing the internal source of human power versus the belief in supernatural beings.</meta>

# RAJA YOGA

The phenomena of the mind are rendered comprehensible through the superstitious explanation of attributing them to the agency of a being, or beings, above the clouds. *Raja Yoga* <note>literally "Royal Union" or the "Royal Path" of meditation</note> declares to mankind that each being is only a conduit for the infinite ocean of knowledge and power that lies behind. It teaches that desires and wants are in man, and that the power of supply is also in man. Wherever and whenever a desire, a want, or a prayer has been fulfilled, it was out of this infinite magazine <note>original: "magazine," meaning a storehouse or treasury</note> that the supply came, and not from any supernatural being. 

The idea of supernatural beings may rouse the power of action in man to a certain extent, but it also brings spiritual decay. It brings dependence; it brings fear; it brings superstition. It degenerates into a horrible belief in the natural weakness of man. There is no "supernatural," says the *Yogi* <note>a practitioner of Yoga</note>, but there are in nature gross <note>physical or material</note> manifestations and subtle manifestations. The subtle are the causes, and the gross are the effects. The gross can be easily perceived by the senses, but the subtle cannot. The practice of *Raja Yoga* will lead to the acquisition of these more subtle perceptions.

All the orthodox systems of Indian philosophy have one goal in view: the liberation of the soul through perfection. The method is by *Yoga*. The word *Yoga* covers an immense ground, but both the *Sankhya* <note>one of the oldest schools of Indian philosophy, focusing on dualism between consciousness and matter</note> and the *Vedantist* <note>followers of Vedanta, a school based on the teachings of the Upanishads</note> schools point to *Yoga* in some form or another.

The subject of the first lectures in the present book is that form of *Yoga* known as *Raja Yoga*. The aphorisms <note>concise statements of principle</note> of *Patanjali* <note>the sage who codified the Yoga Sutras around 400 CE</note> are the highest authority and textbook on *Raja Yoga*. Other philosophers, though occasionally differing from *Patanjali* in some philosophical aspects, have, as a rule, granted his method of practice their decided consent. The first part of this book is comprised of several lectures to classes delivered by the present writer in New York. The second part is a rather free translation of the aphorisms—

<summary>The author argues that human power and knowledge come from an internal infinite source rather than supernatural beings, and introduces Raja Yoga as the practical method for soul liberation as codified by Patanjali.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Patanjali, Indian philosophy, Sankhya, Vedanta, self-reliance, subtle perception, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, transitioning from the general introduction of the work to the specific philosophical foundations of the text.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>AUTHOR’S PREFACE</header>

These are the <term>Aphorisms</term> <note>original: "Sūtras"</note> of <term>Patanjali</term> <note>An ancient Indian sage who organized the philosophy of Yoga into a series of concise statements.</note>, accompanied by a running commentary. Every effort has been made to avoid technical jargon as much as possible and to maintain a free and easy conversational style. In the first part, some simple and specific directions are provided for the student who wishes to practice. However, all such students are especially and earnestly reminded that, with very few exceptions, <term>Yoga</term> can only be safely learned through direct contact with a teacher. If these conversations succeed in awakening a desire for further information on the subject, the teacher will surely appear.

The system of Patanjali is based upon the <term>Sankhya</term> philosophy <note>One of the oldest schools of Indian philosophy, focusing on the dualism between consciousness and matter.</note>, with very few points of difference between them.

The two most important differences are: first, that Patanjali admits a <term>Personal God</term> in the form of a "first teacher," while the only God the Sankhyas admit is a nearly perfected being who is temporarily in charge of a cosmic cycle. Second, the <term>Yogis</term> <note>Practitioners of Yoga.</note> believe the mind to be equally all-pervading as the soul, or <term>Self</term> <note>original: "Puruṣa"; the pure, eternal consciousness.</note>, whereas the Sankhyas do not.

->THE AUTHOR.<-

# CHAPTER 1
## INTRODUCTION

All our knowledge is based on experience. What we call inferential knowledge—where we move from specific cases to general principles, or from the general to the specific—is rooted in experience. In the "exact sciences," truth is easily recognized because it appeals to the personal experience of every human being. Scientists do not ask for blind faith; they present results derived from their own experiences. When they ask us to accept their conclusions, they appeal to a universal human experience. Every exact science has a universal foundation common to all people, allowing us to immediately see whether the conclusions drawn from it are true or false.

The question then arises: Does religion have such a foundation? I must answer this question both "yes" and "no." As it is usually taught throughout the world, religion is said to be based on faith and belief. In most cases, it consists only of different sets of theories. This is why we see various religions in conflict with one another. These theories, in turn, are based on mere belief. One person might claim there is a great Being sitting above the clouds governing the entire universe, and they expect me to believe it simply because they said so. Similarly, I might have my own ideas that I want others to believe; yet, if they ask for a reason, I have none to give. This is why religion and metaphysical <term>philosophy</term> <note>In this context, metaphysics refers to the study of the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note> have a poor reputation today. Many educated people say, "Oh, these religions are just collections of..."

<vocab>experience, inferential knowledge, exact sciences, religion, faith, belief, metaphysical philosophy</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the idea that all knowledge, including science, is rooted in experience and questions whether religion can claim a similar universal, experiential basis rather than relying solely on blind faith.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, experience, science vs religion, faith, universal truth, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the scientific basis of religion. It completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the poor reputation of religion and metaphysical philosophy among educated people.</meta>

...theories without any standard to judge them by, with each person preaching their own pet ideas.” At the same time, I must tell you that there is a basis of universal belief in religion which governs all these different theories and all the varying ideas of different sects in different countries. When we examine their foundations, we find that they are also based upon universal experiences.

First, I will ask you to analyze the various religions of the world. You will find that these are divided into two classes: those with a book <note>Religions based on a central set of written scriptures.</note> and those without a book. Those with a book are the strongest and have the largest number of followers. Those without books have mostly died out, and the few new ones have very small followings. Yet, in all of them, we find one consensus: the truths they teach are the results of the experiences of specific individuals. 

The Christian asks you to believe in his religion—to believe in Christ and to believe in Him as the incarnation of God. He asks you to believe in a God, in a soul, and in a better state for that soul. If I ask him for reasons, he says, “No, it is my belief.” But if you go to the source <note>original: "fountain head"</note> of Christianity, you will find that it is based upon experience. Christ said He saw God; the disciples said they felt God; and so forth. 

Similarly, Buddhism is founded on Buddha’s experience. He experienced certain truths, saw them, came in contact with them, and preached them to the world. It is the same with the Hindus. In their books, the writers—who are called <term>*Rishis*</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Ṛṣi*. In Hindu tradition, these are enlightened sages or "seers" who are said to have directly perceived eternal spiritual truths.</note>, or sages—declare that they have experienced certain truths, and these they preach. 

Thus, it is clear that all the religions of the world have been built upon that one universal and unshakeable <note>original: "adamantine"</note> foundation of all our knowledge: direct experience. The teachers all saw God; they all saw their own souls, they saw their eternity, and they saw their future. They truly saw what they preached. Only...

<summary>This page argues that all major world religions—including Christianity, Buddhism, and Hinduism—are fundamentally rooted in the direct personal experiences of their founders and sages rather than mere theory.</summary>

<keywords>universal belief, direct experience, scripture, Christianity, Buddhism, Hinduism, Rishis, religious foundation, personal revelation</keywords>

# INTRODUCTORY

There is this difference, however: in most of these religions, especially in modern times, a peculiar claim is presented to us. That claim is that these experiences are impossible at the present day; they were only possible for a few men who were the first founders of the religions that subsequently bore their names. At the present time, these experiences have become obsolete, and therefore we have now to take religion on faith <note>original: "belief"</note>. 

I entirely deny this. If there has been even one case of experience in this world in any particular branch of knowledge, it absolutely follows that this experience has been possible millions of times before and will be repeated eternally. Uniformity is the rigorous law of nature; what once happened can happen always.

The teachers of the science of <term>Yoga</term> <note>A systematic practice of physical, mental, and spiritual disciplines originating in ancient India, aimed at achieving a state of perfect spiritual insight and tranquility.</note>, therefore, declare that religion is not only based upon the experiences of ancient times, but that no man can be truly religious until he has had the same perceptions himself. Yoga is the science which teaches us how to get these perceptions. It is useless to talk about religion until one has felt it. 

Why is there so much disturbance, so much fighting and quarreling in the name of God? There has been more bloodshed in the name of God than for any other cause, and the reason is that people never went to the source <note>original: "fountain head"</note>; they were content only to give a mental agreement to the customs of their forefathers and wanted others to do the same. What right has a man to say he has a soul if he does not feel it, or that there is a God if he does not see Him? If there is a God, we must see Him; if there is a soul, we must perceive it; otherwise, it is better not to believe. It is better to be an outspoken atheist than a hypocrite. 

The modern idea, on the one hand, among the "learned," is that religion and <term>metaphysics</term> <note>The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter, substance and attribute, and potentiality and actuality.</note>, and all search after a Supreme Being, are futile. On the other hand, among the semi-educated, the idea seems to be that these things really have no basis,

<summary>The author argues that religion must be based on direct, personal experience rather than blind faith in historical figures, asserting that the "science of Yoga" provides the tools for individuals to perceive spiritual truths for themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga, spiritual experience, law of uniformity, religious conflict, atheism vs. hypocrisy, metaphysics, direct perception</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that spiritual truth must be experienced personally rather than merely accepted through tradition or dogmatic words. It concludes the introduction by asserting that Rāja Yoga provides a scientific method for achieving this direct perception.</meta>

...that their only value lies in the fact that they are powerful incentives <note>original: "motive powers"</note> for doing good in the world. If people believe in a God, they may become good and moral, and thus become good citizens. We cannot blame them for holding such ideas, considering that all the instruction these people receive is simply to believe in an endless rigmarole of words without any substance behind them. They are asked to live on words alone; but can they do it? If they could, I would not have the slightest respect for human nature. Humans want truth; they want to experience truth for themselves, to grasp it, to realize it, and to feel it within their innermost heart <note>original: "heart of hearts"</note>. Only then, declare the <term>Vedas</term> <note>The most ancient Hindu scriptures, which form the foundational spiritual knowledge of India.</note>, will all doubts vanish, all darkness be scattered, and all that is crooked be made straight.

> “You children of immortality, even those who live in the highest realms, the way has been found. There is a way out of all this darkness, and that is by perceiving the One who is beyond all darkness; there is no other way.”

The science of *<term>Rāja Yoga</term>* <note>Literally the "Royal Path" of Yoga, which focuses on mastering the mind through meditation and concentration to reach spiritual liberation.</note> aims to present humanity with a practical and scientifically developed method for reaching this truth. First, every science must have its own method of investigation. If you want to become an astronomer and simply sit down and shout, “Astronomy, Astronomy!” it will never come to you. The same is true of chemistry. A specific method must be followed. You must go to the laboratory, take different substances, mix them, combine them, and experiment with them; from that process, a knowledge of chemistry will emerge. If you want to be an astronomer, you must go to an observatory, use a telescope, and study the stars and planets; then you will become an astronomer. Each science must have its own methods. I could preach thousands of sermons to you, but they would not make you religious until you actually practice the method. These are the truths shared by the sages of all countries and all ages.

<summary>This page argues that true religion is based on personal experience rather than empty words and introduces Rāja Yoga as a scientific method to achieve this spiritual realization. The author compares the practice of yoga to the experimental methods of astronomy and chemistry.</summary>
<keywords>Vedas, Rāja Yoga, spiritual experience, scientific method, truth, religious practice, immortality</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for a scientific approach to spiritual truth, transitioning from the need for personal verification to the specific methodology of observation and generalization required for the science of the mind.</meta>

<header>### INTRODUCTORY</header>

...pure and unselfish men, who had no motive but to do good for the world. They all declare that they have found a truth higher than what the senses can provide, and they challenge us to verify it. They say to you: adopt the method and practice it honestly; then, if you do not find this higher truth, you will have the right to say there is no truth in the claim. But until you have done that, it is not rational to deny the truth of these assertions. Therefore, we must work faithfully, using the prescribed methods, and light will come.

In acquiring knowledge, we make use of generalization <note>original: "generalisation"</note>, and generalization is based upon observation. We first observe facts, then we generalize, and then we draw our conclusions or principles. Knowledge of the mind—of the internal nature of humanity and of thought <note>OCR error: "though"</note>—can never be obtained until we have the power to first observe the facts occurring within us. It is very easy to observe facts in the external world, and many thousands of instruments have been invented to observe every aspect of nature; but in the internal world, we find no instrument to help us. Yet we know we must observe in order to have a real science. Without a proper analysis, any science will be hopeless <note>OCR error: "hoepless"</note>, mere theorizing. That is why all psychologists have been quarreling among themselves since the beginning of time, except for those few who discovered the means of observation.

The science of *<term>Rāja Yoga</term>* <note>original: "Rāya Yoga"; the "Royal Path" of Yoga, which focuses on mastering the mind and achieving concentration through meditation.</note> proposes, in the first place, to give people such a means of observing internal states, and the instrument is the mind itself. The power of attention, when properly guided and directed toward the internal world, will analyze the mind and illuminate facts for us. The powers of the mind are like scattered rays of light; when they are concentrated, they illuminate everything. This is the only source of knowledge that we have. Everyone uses it, both in the external and the...

<vocab>Rāja Yoga, generalization, observation, psychology, internal nature, mind</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that spiritual claims should be tested through a scientific process of internal observation and generalization, rather than being blindly accepted or rejected. It introduces Rāja Yoga as the specific science that uses the mind as an instrument to observe and analyze its own internal states.</summary>
<keywords>Rāja Yoga, internal observation, generalization, scientific method, psychology, concentration of mind, spiritual verification</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the scientific method of Rāja Yoga, specifically focusing on the transition from observing the external world to observing the internal world.</meta>

<header>### RAJA YOGA</header>

internal world. For the psychologist, the same detailed observation that a scientist <note>original: "scientific man"</note> applies to the external world must be directed toward the internal world, and this requires a great deal of practice. From childhood onward, we have been taught only to pay attention to external things and never to internal ones; consequently, most of us have nearly lost the faculty of observing our own internal mechanisms. To turn the mind inward, as it were—to stop it from wandering outside and then concentrate all its powers upon the mind itself so that it may know its own nature and analyze itself—is very hard work. Yet that <note>OCR error: "theat"</note> is the only way to achieve a truly scientific approach to this subject.

What is the use of such knowledge? In the first place, knowledge itself is its own highest reward. In the second place, it is also practical: it will take away our misery. When a person analyzes their own mind and comes face-to-face, as it were, with something that is never destroyed—something that is, by its own nature, eternally pure and perfect—they will no longer be miserable or unhappy. All misery comes from fear and from unsatisfied desire. A person will find that they never truly die, and then they will have no more fear of death. When they know they are perfect, they will no longer have vain desires. With both of these causes absent, there will be no more misery—there will be perfect bliss, even while living in this physical body.

There is only one method by which to attain this knowledge, and that is called concentration. Just as a chemist in a laboratory concentrates all the energies of the mind into one focus and throws them upon the materials being analyzed to discover their secrets, so too must the seeker of truth. An astronomer concentrates all the energies of the mind and projects them through a telescope toward the heavens; and the stars, the sun,

<summary>This page explains that Rāja Yoga requires turning the mind's powers of observation inward to analyze its own nature. This practice of concentration leads to the discovery of an eternal, perfect self, which eliminates the root causes of misery: fear and desire.</summary>
<keywords>concentration, internal observation, psychology, bliss, self-realization, immortality, fear of death, Rāja Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on concentration as the primary tool of discovery, comparing the scientist's focus on the external world to the yogi's focus on the internal mind.</meta>

<header>### INTRODUCTORY 9</header>

...and the moon, give up their secrets to him. The more I can concentrate my thoughts on the matter on which I am talking to you, the more light I can throw upon it. You are listening to me, and the more you concentrate your thoughts, the more clearly you will grasp what I have to say.

How has all this knowledge in the world been gained <note>original: "gain"</note> except through the concentration of the powers of the mind? Nature is ready to give up her secrets if we only know how to knock—how to give her the necessary blow—and the strength and force of that blow comes through concentration. There is no limit to the power of the human mind. The more concentrated it is, the more power is brought to bear on one point, and that is the secret.

It is easier to concentrate the mind on external things, as the mind naturally moves outward; but in the case of religion, <term>psychology</term> <note>In this context, the study of the soul or internal states rather than modern clinical psychology.</note>, or <term>metaphysics</term> <note>The branch of philosophy dealing with the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note>, the subject and the object are one. The object is internal; the mind itself is the object, and it is necessary to study the mind itself—mind studying mind. We know there is a power of the mind called <term>reflective</term>. As I am talking to you, I am also standing outside myself, as it were, like a second person, knowing and hearing what I am saying. You work and think at the same time, while another portion of your mind stands by and observes what you are thinking. The powers of the mind should be concentrated and turned back upon itself; and just as the darkest places reveal their secrets before the penetrating rays of the sun, so will this concentrated mind penetrate its own innermost secrets. 

Thus will we come to the basis of belief, the real genuine religion. We will perceive for ourselves whether we have souls, whether life lasts for five minutes or for eternity, and whether there is a God in the universe or none. It will all be revealed to us. This is what ***Rāja Yoga*** <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"; from Sanskrit, meaning the "Royal Path" of yoga, focusing on meditation and mental discipline.</note> proposes to teach. The goal of all its teaching is how to concentrate the mind, then how to...

We are human beings; that is sufficient. Every human being has the right and power to seek religion; every human being has the right to ask "why?" and to have their questions answered by themselves, if they only take the trouble to do so.

So far, then, we see that in the study of <term>Rāja Yoga</term> <note>Sanskrit: "Royal Union"; a path of meditation and mental discipline.</note>, no faith or belief is necessary. Believe nothing until you find it out for yourself; that is what it teaches us. Truth requires no prop to make it stand. Do you mean to say that the facts of our awakened state require any dreams or imaginings to prove them? Certainly not. This study of *Rāja Yoga* takes a long time and constant practice. A part of this practice is physical, but the main part of it is mental. As we go along, we shall find how intimately the mind is connected with the body. If we believe that the mind is simply a finer part of the body, and that the mind acts upon the body, then in the same way <note>original: "saw way"</note>, the body must act upon the mind. 

If the body is sick, the mind becomes sick also. If the body is healthy, the mind remains healthy and strong. When one is angry, the mind becomes disturbed; at the same time, when the mind is disturbed, the body also becomes disturbed. With the majority of mankind, the mind is entirely under the control of the body; the mind is very little developed. The vast majority of humanity—if you will kindly excuse me—is very little removed from the animals. Not only that, but in many instances, their power of control is very little higher than that of the lower animals. We have very little command over our minds. Therefore, to bring that command about—to gain that control over both body and mind—we must take certain physical [steps]...

...helps, and when the body is sufficiently controlled, we can attempt to master the mind. By mastering the mind, we will be able to bring it under our control, make it work as we like, and compel it to concentrate its powers as we desire.

According to the <term>Royal Practitioner</term> <note>original: "Rāja Yogī"; a practitioner of the "Royal Path" of yoga focused on meditation and mind control.</note>, this entire external world is simply the physical <note>original: "gross"</note> form of the internal, or subtle world. The finer form is always the cause, and the physical form is the effect. Therefore, the external world is the effect, and the internal world is the cause. In the same way, external forces are simply the physical parts of the internal forces, which are more refined. One who has discovered and learned how to manage these internal forces will bring all of nature under their control. The <term>Yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga.</note> sets themselves no less a task than to master the entire universe and to control all of nature. They want to reach the point where what we call "nature's laws" will have no influence over them and where they will be able to transcend them all. They will be the master of all nature, both internal and external. The progress and civilization of the human race is simply the result of controlling this nature.

Different cultures vary in their methods. Just as in the same society some individuals want to control external nature and others want to control internal nature, so among different peoples, some want to control the external and some the internal. Some say that by controlling internal nature, we control everything; others say that by controlling external nature, we control everything. Carried to the extreme, both are right, because there is actually no difference between the "internal" and the "external." It is an artificial <note>original: "fictitious"</note> limitation that does not truly exist. Both the "externalists" and the "internalists" are destined to meet at the same point when both reach the limits of their knowledge. Just as the physical scientist <note>original: "physician"; here referring to a practitioner of physical science or medicine.</note>, when he pushes his knowledge to its limits, finds it merging into <term>metaphysics</term> <note>The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note>, so the metaphysician will find that...

<meta>This page continues the explanation of how a practitioner (Yogi) views the world, arguing that the distinction between mind and matter is temporary and that mastering internal forces allows for control over the external world.</meta>

# RAJA YOGA

What he calls mind and matter are only apparent distinctions, which will eventually have to vanish forever.

The goal and aim of all science is to find a single unit—that "One" from which this whole diversity <note>original: "manifold"; referring to the many different forms in the physical world.</note> is being manufactured; that "One" existing as many. <term>Royal Yoga</term> <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"; the "Royal Path" of yoga, which focuses on mastering the mind and internal nature through meditation.</note> proposes to start from the internal world, to study internal nature, and, through that, to control the whole—both internal and external. This is a very ancient attempt. India has been its special stronghold, but it was also attempted by other nations. 

In Western countries, it is thought to be mysticism. People who wanted to practice it were either burned or killed as witches and sorcerers. In India, for various reasons, it fell into the hands of people who destroyed 90 percent of the knowledge; of the portion that remained, they tried to keep it a great secret. In modern times, many so-called teachers have appeared who are worse than those in India, because the Indian teachers at least knew something, while these modern exponents do not.

Anything that is secret or mysterious in these systems of <term>Yoga</term> <note>A system of physical, mental, and spiritual practices originating in ancient India.</note> should be rejected at once. The best guide in life is strength. In religion, as in everything else, discard everything that weakens you; have nothing to do with it. All "mystery-mongering" weakens the human brain. Through such secrecy, this science of Yoga has been nearly <note>original: "well nigh"</note> destroyed, but it is really one of the grandest of sciences. From the time it was discovered—more than 4,000 years ago—it was perfectly described, formulated, and preached in India. It is a striking fact that the more modern the commentator, the greater the mistakes they make. The more ancient the writer on the subject, the more rational they are. Thus, it fell into the hands of a few people who made it a secret instead of letting the full light of day and reason fall upon it; they did this so that they might keep the power for themselves.

# INTRODUCTORY

To begin with, there is no mystery in what I teach. What little I know, I will tell you. So far as I can reason it out, I will do so; but whatever I do not know, I will simply tell you that it is what the scriptures <note>original: "books"; referring to the ancient Vedic and Yogic texts.</note> say. It is wrong to believe blindly. You must exercise your own reason and judgment; you must put these ideas into practice and see whether the results happen or not. Just as you would approach any other material science, you should take up this science for study in exactly the same manner. There is neither mystery nor danger in it. So far as it is true, it ought to be preached in the public streets in broad daylight. Any attempt to make these things mysterious is very dangerous.

Before proceeding further, I will explain a little of the <term>Sankhya</term> <note>original: "Sānkhya"; one of the oldest schools of Indian philosophy, which views the universe as a duality between Purusha (consciousness) and Prakriti (nature/matter).</note> Philosophy, upon which the whole of <term>Royal Yoga</term> <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"; the path of yoga focused on meditation and mastery of the mind.</note> is based. According to this philosophy, perception comes through instruments—for example, the eyes. The eyes carry the sensation to the organs <note>Refers to the sensory centers in the brain rather than just the physical eye.</note>, the organs to the mind, the mind to the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "determinative faculty"; in Sanskrit, 'Buddhi', the part of the mind that decides and reacts.</note>, and from this the <term>Purusha</term> <note>original: "Puruṣa"; the soul or pure consciousness, which is the true observer.</note> (the soul) receives it and gives the "order" back, as it were, returning through all these stages. In this way, sensations are received. 

With the exception of the Soul, all of these are material; however, the mind is made of much finer material than the external instruments. That material of which the mind is composed becomes denser <note>original: "grosser"</note> and becomes what is called the <term>Subtle Elements</term> <note>original: "Tanmātras"; the refined essences of sound, touch, sight, taste, and smell that precede physical matter.</note>. It becomes even denser and forms the external, physical world. That is the psychology of the Sankhya. Therefore, between the intellect and the physical matter outside, there is only a difference in degree. The Soul is the only thing that is immaterial. The mind is an instrument in the hands of the soul, so to speak, through which the soul perceives external objects. This mind is constantly changing and wavering, and it can attach itself either to several organs, to only one, or to none at all. For instance, if I listen to a clock with great attention, I will not, perhaps, see

In the same way, the mind can be attached to all the sensory organs simultaneously. This mind has the reflexive power of looking back into its own depths. This reflexive power is what the <term>Yogi</term> <note>original: "Yogī"; a practitioner of yoga.</note> wants to attain; by concentrating the powers of the mind and turning them inward, he seeks to know what is happening inside. There is no question of mere belief here; this is the analysis of certain philosophers. Modern physiologists tell you that the eyes are not the actual organs of vision, but that the true organs are located in the nerve centers of the brain—and the same is true for all the senses. They also tell you that these centers are formed of the same material as the brain itself. The <term>Sankhyas</term> <note>original: "Sānkhyas"; followers of one of the oldest schools of Indian philosophy.</note> will tell you the same thing, though one is a statement from a physical perspective and the other from a psychological perspective; yet both are describing the same reality. Beyond this, we must demonstrate the truth for ourselves.

The <term>Yogi</term> proposes to attain a refined state of perception in which he can observe all these internal processes. There must be a mental perception of all the different states of being. We shall perceive how a sensation travels, how the mind receives it, how it moves to the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "determinative faculty"; in Sanskrit, 'Buddhi', the part of the mind that decides and reacts.</note>, and how the intellect presents it to the <term>Soul</term> <note>original: "Puruśa"; the pure consciousness or true Self.</note>. Just as every science requires certain preparations and has its own specific method—and we can never understand that science until we follow its method—the same is true for <term>Royal Yoga</term> <note>original: "Rāja Yoga".</note>.

Certain regulations regarding food are necessary; we must consume the food that produces the purest mind. If you go into a <term>menagerie</term> <note>original: "menagerie"; a collection of wild animals kept in captivity for exhibition; a zoo.</note>, you will see this demonstrated at once. You see the elephants—huge animals, but calm and gentle. Yet, if you go toward the cages of the lions and tigers, you will find them restless, showing how much difference has been produced by their diet. All the forces that are working in this

### INTRODUCTORY

...the body has been produced from food; we see that every day. If you begin to fast, first your body will grow weak and your physical strength will suffer; then, after a few days, your mental strength will suffer as well. First, your memory will fail. Then a point comes when you are unable to think, much less follow any complex course of reasoning. Therefore, we must be careful about what kind of food we eat at the beginning of our practice. Once we have gained enough strength and our practice is well advanced, we do not need to be so cautious in this regard. While a plant is growing, it must be protected by a hedge lest it be injured; but when it becomes a tree, the hedges are taken away, for it is strong enough to withstand all assaults.

A <term>Yogi</term> <note>original: "Yogī"; a practitioner of yoga or one seeking spiritual union.</note> must avoid the two extremes of luxury and <term>austerity</term> <note>original: "austerity"; severe self-discipline or the avoidance of all comforts.</note>. He must not fast or torture his own flesh. The <term>Gita</term> <note>original: "Gītā"; referring to the Bhagavad Gita, a sacred Hindu scripture.</note> says that he who does so cannot be a <term>Yogi</term>. Neither the person who stays awake too long, nor the one who sleeps too much, nor the one who works too much, nor the one who does no work at all—none of these can be <term>Yogis</term>.

## CHAPTER II
### THE FIRST STEPS

<term>Rāja Yoga</term> <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"; often called the "Royal Path" of yoga, focusing on meditation and mastery of the mind.</note> is divided into eight steps. The first is <term>Yama</term> <note>original: "Yama"; ethical restraints or universal moral commandments.</note>—non-violence, truthfulness, non-stealing, self-restraint <note>original: "continence"</note>, and the refusal to accept gifts. Next is <term>Niyama</term> <note>original: "Niyama"; personal observances or rules for self-discipline.</note>—cleanliness, contentment, self-discipline <note>original: "mortification"</note>, study, and surrendering oneself to God. Then comes <term>Asana</term> <note>original: "Āsana"</note>, or posture; <term>Pranayama</term> <note>original: "Prāṇāyāma"</note>, or controlling the body’s vital forces; <term>Pratyahara</term> <note>original: "Pratyāhāra"</note>, or turning the mind inward; <term>Dharana</term> <note>original: "Dhāranā"</note>, or concentration; <term>Dhyana</term> <note>original: "Dhyānā"</note>, or meditation; and <term>Samadhi</term> <note>original: "Samādhi"</note>, or the state of super-consciousness. 

As we can see, <term>Yama</term> and <term>Niyama</term> are moral trainings; without these as a foundation, no practice of <term>Yoga</term> will succeed. As these practices become established, the <term>Yogi</term> <note>original: "Yogī"</note> will begin to realize the fruits of his practice; without them, it will never bear fruit. A <term>Yogi</term> must not think of harming anyone through thought, word, or deed; this applies not only to humans, but to all animals. Mercy should not be for humans alone, but should go beyond and embrace the entire world.

The next step is <term>Asana</term>, or posture. A series of exercises, both physical and mental, must be performed every day until certain higher states of being are reached. Therefore, it is quite necessary that we find a posture in which we can remain for a long time. The posture that is easiest for each individual is the one they should use. For one person, it may be very easy to think in a certain posture, but this same position may be very difficult for another. We will find later on that in the study of these psychological matters, a great deal of activity will occur within the body. Nerve currents will have to be redirected and given a new channel. New types of vibrations will begin, and the person's entire physical and mental makeup <note>original: "constitution"</note> will be remodeled, as it—

# THE FIRST STEPS

...were <note>This continues from the previous page's discussion on posture (Asana).</note>. But the main part of the action will occur along the spinal column, so the one thing necessary for the posture is to hold the spinal column free by sitting erect and keeping the three parts—the chest, neck, and head—in a straight line. Let the whole weight of the body be supported by the ribs, and then you will have an easy, natural posture with a straight spine. You will naturally see that you cannot think high thoughts with a sunken chest. 

This portion of **Yoga** is somewhat similar to **Hatha Yoga** <note>original: "Hatha Yoga"; a branch of yoga focused on physical postures and health.</note>, which deals entirely with the physical body; the aim of the latter is to make the physical body very strong. We are not concerned with that here, because those practices are very difficult, cannot be learned in a day, and, ultimately, do not lead to any spiritual growth. You will find many of these practices in the work of **Delsarte** <note>François Delsarte (1811–1871) was a French teacher who created a system of movement and expression popular in the late 19th century.</note> and other teachers, such as placing the body in different postures, but their object is physical, not psychological. There is not one muscle in the body over which a person cannot establish perfect control. The heart can be made to stop or continue at one's bidding; in the same way, every part of the organism can be made to work according to one’s will.

The result of this branch of **Yoga** is to make people live a long time; health is the primary idea and the one goal of the <term>Hatha Yogi</term> <note>original: "Hatha Yogī"; a practitioner of Hatha Yoga.</note>. He is determined not to fall sick, and he never does. He lives a long life; a hundred years is nothing to him; he is still quite young and fresh when he is 150, without a single hair turning gray. But that is all. A <term>Banyan tree</term> <note>The Banyan tree (*Ficus benghalensis*) is an Indian fig tree known for its vast size and extreme longevity.</note> sometimes lives 5,000 years, but it is still only a Banyan tree and nothing more. Similarly, if a man merely lives a long time, he is only a healthy animal. 

One or two ordinary lessons from the <term>Hatha Yogis</term> are very useful, however. For instance, some of you will find it beneficial for headaches to drink cold water through the nose as soon as you wake up; for the rest of the day, your brain will feel clear and cool, and you will never catch a cold. It...

->**RAJA YOGA**<-

...is very easy to do; put your nose into the water and perform a pumping action in the throat. <note>This refers to *Jala Neti*, a traditional yogic technique for cleansing the nasal passages with water.</note>

After a person has learned to maintain a firm, upright posture, they must perform a practice called "the purifying of the nerves," according to certain schools of thought. This part has been rejected by some as not belonging to <term>Raja Yoga</term> <note>The "Royal Path" of yoga, focused on meditation and mental mastery.</note>, but because such a great authority as the commentator **Shankaracharya** <note>Adi Shankara (c. 788–820 CE), a highly influential Indian philosopher who consolidated the doctrine of Advaita Vedanta.</note> advises it, I think it is appropriate to mention it. I will quote his own directions from his commentary on the **Svetasvatara Upanishad** <note>One of the primary ancient Sanskrit texts of Hindu philosophy.</note>: 

> "The mind whose impurities have been cleared away by **breath control** <note>original: "Prāṇāyāma"</note> becomes fixed in **the Absolute** <note>original: "Brahman"; the ultimate, infinite reality in Hindu philosophy.</note>; therefore, breath control is recommended. First, the nerves are to be purified, and then comes the power to practice breath control. Closing the right nostril with the thumb, inhale air through the left nostril according to your capacity; then, without any delay, exhale the air through the right nostril while closing the left one. Again, inhaling through the right nostril, exhale through the left, according to your capacity. By practicing this three or five times at four intervals during the day—before dawn, at midday, in the evening, and at midnight—purity of the nerves is attained within fifteen days or a month. Then the practice of breath control begins."

Practice is absolutely necessary. You may sit down and listen to me for hours every day, but if you do not practice, you will not move one step further. Everything depends on practice. We never truly understand these things until we experience them; we must see and feel them for ourselves. Simply listening to explanations and theories will not suffice. 

There are several obstructions to practice. The first obstruction is an unhealthy body; if the body is not in a fit state, the practice will be hindered. Therefore, we must take care of what we eat and drink, and what we do. Always use a mental effort—similar to what is commonly called "Christian Science" <note>A reference to the 19th-century spiritual movement that emphasized mental healing and the power of thought over physical illness.</note>—to keep the body strong. That is all; nothing...

# THE FIRST STEPS

...further regarding the body. We must not forget that health is only a means to an end. If health were the ultimate goal, we would be no different from animals; animals rarely become unhealthy.

The second obstruction is doubt; we always feel uncertain about things we cannot see. A person cannot live on words alone, no matter how hard they may try. Therefore, doubt arises as to whether there is any truth in these teachings or not; even the best of us will doubt sometimes. However, with practice, within a few days, a small glimpse will appear—enough to give you encouragement and hope. As one commentator on **Yoga** <note>A spiritual and physical discipline aimed at joining the individual soul with the universal consciousness.</note> philosophy says: “When even one proof is realized, however small it may be, it will give us faith in the entire teaching of Yoga.” 

For instance, after the first few months of training and teaching, you will begin to find that you can read another person’s thoughts; they will appear to you in the form of pictures. Perhaps you will hear something happening at a great distance when you concentrate your mind and attempt to do so. These glimpses will come—just a little bit at first—but they are enough to give you faith, strength, and hope. For example, if you concentrate your thoughts on the tip of your nose, within a few days you will begin to smell a most beautiful fragrance. That will be enough to show you that there are certain **mental perceptions** <note>original: "percepitons"</note> that can be made obvious without physical contact with objects. 

But we must always remember that these experiences are only the means; the aim, end, and goal of all this training is the **liberation of the soul**. Absolute control over nature—and nothing short of it—must be the goal. We must be the masters, not nature. Neither the body nor the mind must be our master; nor must we forget that the body belongs to the mind, and I am not a servant to the body.

A god and a demon went to learn about the **Self** <note>original: "Atman"; the true, eternal essence of a person.</note> from a great **sage** <note>A profoundly wise person or spiritual teacher.</note>. They studied with him for a long time, and at last the sage told them: “You yourself are the being you seek...” <note>The text here begins a famous parable from the Chandogya Upanishad regarding the realization of "Tat Tvam Asi" or "Thou Art That."</note>

seeking.” Both of them thought that their bodies were the Self. “We have got everything,” they said, and both of them returned to their people, and said, “We have learned everything that is to be learned; eat, drink, and be merry; we are the Self; there is nothing beyond us.” The nature of the demon was ignorant and clouded, so he never inquired any further, but was perfectly satisfied with the idea that he was God, and that by "the Self" was meant the body. 

But the god had a purer nature. He at first committed the mistake of thinking, “I, this body, am <term>Brahman</term> <note>The ultimate reality or supreme cosmic power in Hindu philosophy.</note>, so I must keep it strong and in health, and well-dressed, and give it all sorts of bodily enjoyments.” But, in a few days, he found out that this could not be the meaning of the sage, their master; there must be something higher. So he came back and said, “Sir, did you teach me that this body is the Self? If so, I see all bodies die; the Self cannot die.” The sage said, “Find it out; **you are That** <note>original: "thou art That" (Tat Tvam Asi). A famous Sanskrit aphorism from the Upanishads meaning the individual soul is identical to the ultimate reality.</note>.” 

Then the god thought that the vital forces which work the body were what the sage meant. But, after a time, he found that if he ate, these vital forces remained strong, but, if he starved, they became weak. The god then went back to the sage and said, “Sir, do you mean that the vital forces are the Self?” The sage said, “Find out for yourself; **you are That**.” 

The god returned once more, and thought that it was the mind; perhaps that is the Self. But in a few days he reflected that thoughts are so various; now good, now bad; the mind is too changeable to be the Self. He went back to the sage and said, “Sir, I do not think that the mind is the Self; did you mean that?” “No,” replied the sage, “**you are That**; find out for yourself.” 

The god went back, and, at last, found that he was the Self, beyond all thought; One, without birth or death, whom the sword cannot pierce, or the fire burn, whom the air cannot dry, or the water melt, the beginningless and birthless, the immovable, the intangible, the omniscient, the...

<summary>This page uses a parable to explain that the "Self" is not the physical body, the energy of the body, or the changing mind, but an eternal, indestructible spirit. It highlights the difference between an ignorant perspective that identifies only with the material and a spiritual perspective that seeks the ultimate reality.</summary>

This world has a great many people with these demonic <term>demoniac</term> <note>In this context, it refers to a nature focused entirely on material and sensory pleasures rather than spiritual truth.</note> natures, but there are some gods as well. If one proposes to teach any science to increase the power of sensory enjoyment, he finds multitudes ready for it. If one undertakes to show mankind the supreme goal, they care nothing for it. Very few have the power to grasp the highest truth, and fewer still have the patience to attain it; but a few also know that even if the body could be preserved for a thousand years, the result will be the same in the end. When the forces that hold it together dissipate, the body must fall. No man was ever born who could stop his body from changing for even one moment. "Body" is simply the name of a series of changes. “As in a river the masses of water are changing before you every moment, and new masses are coming, yet taking a similar form, so it is with this body.” Yet the body must be kept strong and healthy; it is the best instrument we have.

This human body is the greatest body in the universe, and a human being is the greatest being. Man is higher than all animals, and even higher than all angels; no one is greater than man. Even the **celestial beings** <note>original: "Devas" — gods or shining ones in Hindu cosmology who live in a state of high pleasure but are not yet liberated from the cycle of rebirth.</note> will have to come down again and attain salvation through a human body. Man alone attains perfection—not even the gods. According to the Jews and Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note>, God created man, and after creating man He asked the angels to come and salute him, and all did except **Iblis** <note>The name of the primary devil in Islamic tradition, who refused to bow to Adam.</note>; so God cursed him and he became Satan. Behind this allegory is the great truth that this human birth is the greatest birth we can have. The lower creation, the animal, is dull and made mostly out of **inertia** <note>original: "Tamas" — one of the three qualities (gunas) in Hindu philosophy, representing darkness, stability, or ignorance.</note>. Animals cannot have any high thoughts; nor can the angels, or celestial beings, attain direct freedom without human birth. In human society, in the same...

# RAJA YOGA

In the same way, too much wealth or too much poverty is a great obstacle to the higher development of the soul. It is from the middle classes that the great leaders of the world come. In this state of life, the social and physical forces are very equally adjusted and balanced.

Returning to our subject, we come next to ***Prānāyāma*** <term>Pranayama</term> <note>Sanskrit: *prāna* (vital energy) + *āyāma* (control). It refers to the practice of regulating the breath to control the flow of energy in the body.</note>, or controlling the breathing. What does that have to do with concentrating the powers of the mind? Breath is like the **fly-wheel** <note>In mechanics, a heavy wheel that regulates a machine's speed and accumulates power. Here, the author suggests that breath is the primary physical motion that drives more subtle internal energies.</note> of this machine. In a large engine, you see the fly-wheel move first, and that motion is then conveyed to finer and finer machinery, until the most delicate and precise mechanism in the machine is moving in sync. This breath is that fly-wheel, supplying and regulating the motive power to everything in this body.

There once was a minister to a great king. He fell into disgrace, and the king, as a punishment, ordered him to be imprisoned at the top of a very high tower. This was done, and the minister was left there to die. He had a faithful wife, however, and at night she came to the tower and called to her husband at the top to ask what she could do to help him. 

He told her to return to the tower the following night and bring with her a long rope, a stout twine, a piece of pack-thread, a silken thread, a beetle, and a little honey. Wondering why he needed these, the good wife obeyed her husband and brought him the desired articles. The husband directed her to attach the silken thread firmly to the beetle, then to smear its horns with a drop of honey, and set it free on the wall of the tower with its head pointing up. 

She followed all these instructions, and the beetle started on its long journey. Smelling the honey ahead of it, the beetle slowly crept onwards and upwards in the hope of reaching it, until at last it reached the top of the tower. There, the minister caught the beetle and took hold of the silken thread. He told his wife to tie the other end of the silk to the pack-thread, and after he had drawn it up...

<page-num>23</page-num>

# THE FIRST STEPS

...carrying up the packing thread, he repeated the procedure with the stout twine, and finally with the rope. Then the rest was easy. The minister descended from the tower by means of the rope and made his escape. In this body of ours, the movement of the breath is the "silken thread." By laying hold of that and learning to control it, we grasp the packing thread of the nerve currents; from these, we reach the stout twine of our thoughts, and finally the rope of ***Prāṇa*** <term>Prana</term> <note>Sanskrit: the vital life-force or energy that permeates the universe.</note>. By controlling this, we achieve freedom.

We do not know anything about our own bodies; indeed, we cannot know them through external means alone. At best, we can take a corpse and dissect it, and there are some who can dissect a living animal to see what is inside the body. Still, that has nothing to do with understanding our own living bodies. We know very little about them. Why is this? Because our attention is not discriminating <note>original: "discriminating," meaning sharp or observant enough to distinguish fine details</note> enough to catch the very fine movements that are occurring within. 

We can know of these internal movements only as the mind, so to speak, enters the body and becomes more subtle. To achieve that subtle perception, we have to begin with coarser <note>original: "grosser," referring to physical or material things that are easily perceived by the senses</note> perceptions. We must take hold of that which is setting the whole engine in motion, and that is the ***Prāṇa***, the most obvious manifestation of which is the breath. Then, along with the breath, we will slowly "enter" the body. This will enable us to discover the subtle forces—to see how the nerve currents are moving all over the body. As soon as we perceive them and learn to feel them, we shall begin to gain control over them and over the physical body. 

The mind is also set in motion by these nerve currents; therefore, we shall finally reach a state where we have perfect control over both the body and the mind, making both of them our servants. Knowledge is power, and we must obtain this power. So, we must begin at the beginning with ***Prāṇāyāma*** <term>Pranayama</term> <note>Sanskrit: the regulation of breath to control the flow of vital energy.</note>, the restraining of the ***Prāṇa***. This ***Prāṇāyāma*** is a broad subject and will take several lessons to illustrate thoroughly. We will take it part by part.

->**RAJA YOGA**<-

We will gradually see the reasons for each exercise and which forces in the body are set in motion. All these insights will come to us, but they require constant practice; the proof will come through that practice. No amount of reasoning I can provide will be proof to you until you have demonstrated it for yourselves. As soon as you begin to feel these currents in motion throughout your body, your doubts will vanish, but this requires diligent practice every day. You must practice at least twice daily, and the best times are toward the morning and the evening. When night passes into day, and day into night, the atmosphere passes through a state of relative calmness. These times—early morning and early evening—are the two points of natural stillness. Your body will have a similar tendency to become calm at those times. We should take advantage of that natural condition and begin our practice then. Make it a rule not to eat until you have practiced; if you do this, the sheer force of hunger will help you overcome your laziness. In India, they teach children never to eat until they have practiced and worshipped, and it becomes natural to them after a while; a boy will not feel hungry until he has bathed and completed his practice.

Those of you who can afford it will do better to have a room dedicated solely to this practice. Do not sleep in that room; it must be kept holy <note>original: "holy," meaning sacred or set apart for spiritual use</note>. You must not enter the room until you have bathed and are perfectly clean in both body and mind. Keep flowers in that room always; they are the best surroundings for a ***Yogī*** <term>Yogi</term> <note>Sanskrit: a person who practices yoga or follows the path of yoga</note>. Also, display pictures that are pleasing. Burn incense morning and evening. Allow no quarreling, anger, or unholy thoughts in that room. Only allow people to enter who share your mindset. Then, gradually <note>original: "by and by"</note>, a sacred atmosphere will develop in the room. When you are feeling miserable, sorrowful, or doubtful, or when your mind is disturbed, the mere act of entering that room...

<header>THE FIRST STEPS</header>

...will make you calmer. This was the original purpose of the temple and the church, and in some temples and churches you will find it even now, but in the majority of them the very idea has been lost. The concept is that by maintaining holy vibrations there, the place becomes and remains <term>illumined</term> <note>spiritually enlightened or filled with divine light</note>. Those who cannot afford to have a room set apart for this purpose can practice anywhere they like. 

Sit in an upright posture, and the first thing to do is to send a current of holy thought to all creation; mentally repeat: “May all beings be happy; may all beings be peaceful; may all beings be blissful.” Do this while facing the East, South, North, and West. The more you do that, the better you will feel. You will eventually find that the easiest way to make yourselves healthy is to see that others are healthy, and the easiest way to make yourselves happy is to see that others are happy. 

After doing that, those who believe in God should pray—not for money, not for health, nor for heaven; pray for knowledge and light. Every other prayer is selfish. Then, the next thing to do is to think of your own body and ensure that it is strong and healthy; it is the best instrument you have. Think of it as being as strong as <term>adamant</term> <note>original: "adamant," a legendary stone of impenetrable hardness, often used to describe something unbreakable</note>, and believe that with the help of this body you will cross this **ocean of life** <note>a common metaphor for the cycle of worldly existence and its many trials</note>. 

Freedom is never to be reached by the weak; throw away all weakness. Tell your body that it is strong; tell your mind that it is strong, and have unbounded faith and hope in yourself.

<page-num>26</page-num>
# CHAPTER III. PRANA <term>Prāna: The vital life-force or energy that permeates the universe.</term>

*Pranayama* <note>original: "PRĀNĀYĀMA"; a yogic practice often translated as "breath control."</note> is not, as many think, simply a matter of breathing; breathing, indeed, has very little to do with it, if anything. Breathing is only one of the many exercises through which we reach the true *Pranayama*. *Pranayama* means the control of *Prana*. According to the philosophers of India, the entire universe is composed of two materials, one of which they call *Akasha* <term>Ākāśa: The most subtle, ethereal sky or substance; the "ether."</term>. It is the omnipresent, all-penetrating existence. Everything that has form, and everything that is the result of combined elements, is developed out of this *Akasha*. It is the *Akasha* that becomes the air, the liquids, and the solids. It is the *Akasha* that becomes the plants, every form that we see, everything that can be sensed, and everything that exists. By itself, it cannot be perceived; it is so subtle that it is beyond all ordinary perception. It can only be seen when it has become tangible <note>original: "gross"</note> and has taken form. At the beginning of creation, there is only this *Akasha*. At the end of the cycle, the solids, the liquids, and the gases all melt back into the *Akasha* again, and the next creation proceeds out of this *Akasha* in the same way.

By what power is this *Akasha* brought into being <note>original: "manifested"</note> as this universe? By the power of *Prana*. Just as *Akasha* is the infinite, omnipresent material of this universe, so is this *Prana* the infinite, omnipresent manifesting power of this universe. At the beginning and at the end of a cycle, everything becomes *Akasha*, and all the forces in the universe resolve back into the *Prana*. In the next cycle, everything that we call energy or force is developed out of this *Prana*. It is the *Prana* that is...

<vocab>Pranayama (control of vital energy), Prana (vital energy), Akasha (primary substance/ether), India, philosophers, creation, cycle, manifestation, energy, force</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the two fundamental principles of the universe in Indian philosophy: Akasha (the primordial matter) and Prana (the primordial energy). It explains that Pranayama is the practice of controlling this universal energy, rather than just simple breathing exercises.</summary>
<keywords>Prana, Akasha, Pranayama, Indian Philosophy, Cosmology, Vital Energy, Ether, Creation Cycles</keywords>

<page-num>27</page-num>

# PRANA

...manifesting as motion. It is *Prana* <term>Prana: The vital life-force or psychic energy.</term> that manifests as gravitation and as magnetism. It is *Prana* that manifests as the actions of the body, as nerve currents, and as the power of thought. From thought down to the lowest physical force, everything is simply a manifestation of *Prana*. The total of all forces in the universe, whether mental or physical, when resolved back into its original state, is called *Prana*. 

"When there was neither something nor nothing <note>original: "neither aught nor naught"</note>, when darkness was covering darkness, what existed then? That *Akasha* existed without motion." <note>This is a reference to the Nasadiya Sukta, an ancient Vedic hymn about the creation of the universe.</note> At that stage, the physical motion of the *Prana* had stopped, but it existed nonetheless. We know through modern science that the total energy in the universe is unchangeable. The sum total of energy in the universe remains the same throughout; however, at the end of a cycle, these energies quiet down and become potential <term>potential: stored energy that is not yet in use.</term>. At the beginning of the next cycle, they spark into action, strike upon the *Akasha*, and evolve out of the *Akasha* into various forms. As the *Akasha* changes, this *Prana* also changes into all these different manifestations of energy. The knowledge and control of this *Prana* is what is truly meant by *Pranayama* <note>original: "PRĀṆĀYĀMA"; the practice of controlling the vital forces.</note>.

This opens the door to almost unlimited power for us. Suppose, for instance, a person understood the *Prana* perfectly and could control it; what power on earth would not be theirs? They would be able to move the sun and stars out of their places and control everything in the universe, from the smallest atoms to the largest suns, because they would control the *Prana* itself. This is the ultimate goal of *Pranayama*. When the *Yogi* <term>Yogi: A practitioner of yoga who has achieved a high level of spiritual mastery.</term> becomes perfect, there will be nothing in nature outside of their control. If they command the gods <note>In this context, "gods" refers to the Devas, or celestial beings/higher natural forces.</note> to come, they will come at their bidding; if they ask the spirits of the dead to come, they will come at their bidding. All the forces of nature will obey them as their servants, and when the ignorant see these powers of the *Yogi*, they call them...

<page-num>28</page-num>

# RAJA YOGA

...control miracles. One characteristic of the Hindu mind is that it always searches for the ultimate generalization <note>original: "generalisation"</note>, leaving the details to be worked out later. The question is raised in the *Vedas* <term>Vedas: The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism, written in ancient Sanskrit.</term>, "What is that, by knowing which we shall know everything?" Thus, all books and all philosophies that have been written have only been intended to prove *that by knowing which, everything else is known.* 

If a person wants to know this universe bit by bit, they must know every individual grain of sand, which would require infinite time; it is impossible to know them all. Then how can knowledge exist? How is it possible for a person to be all-knowing through specific details <note>original: "particulars"</note>? The practitioners of yoga <note>original: "Yogīs"</note> say that behind these specific manifestations, there is a generalization. Behind all individual ideas stands a generalized, abstract principle; grasp it, and you have grasped everything. 

Just as this whole universe has been generalized in the *Vedas* into that One Absolute Existence, whoever has grasped that Existence has grasped the whole universe. In the same way, all forces have been generalized into this vital energy <note>original: "Prāṇa"</note>, and whoever has grasped that energy has grasped all the forces of the universe, whether mental or physical. Whoever has controlled this vital energy has controlled their own mind and all the minds that exist. Whoever has controlled this energy has controlled their body and all the bodies that exist, because this vital energy is the generalized manifestation of force.

How to control the vital energy is the single focus of energy control <note>original: "Prāṇāyāma"</note>. All these trainings and exercises are for that one end, and each person must begin where they stand; they must learn how to control the things that are nearest to them. This body is the nearest thing to us—nearer than anything else in the universe—and this mind is the nearest of all. The vital energy <note>original: "Prāṇa"</note> which is operating this mind and body is the closest to us of all the energy in the universe. This little wave of vital energy, which represents our own energies, both mental and physical, is the nearest wave to us...

<summary>This page explains the Hindu philosophical pursuit of "ultimate generalizations," arguing that by mastering Prana (vital life-force), one gains control over all mental and physical forces in the universe.</summary>

# PRĀṆA

...of all that infinite ocean of *Prāṇa* <term>Prāṇa: The vital energy or life force that pervades the universe.</term>. If we can succeed in controlling that little wave, then alone can we hope to control the entirety of *Prāṇa*. Perfection is gained by the yogi <note>original: "Yogī"</note> who has done this, and no power is his master any longer. He has become almost almighty and almost all-knowing. We see groups <note>original: "sects"</note> in every country who have attempted this control of *Prāṇa*. In this country, there are mind-healers, faith-healers, spiritualists, Christian Scientists, hypnotists, and so on. If we analyze these different groups, we find that the background of each is this control of the *Prāṇa*, whether they know it or not <note>original: "now"</note>. If you boil all their theories down, the residue <note>original: "residuum"</note> will be the same. They are manipulating the same force, though they do so unknowingly. They have stumbled upon the discovery of a force without knowing its nature, but they are unconsciously using the same powers that a yogi uses, which come from *Prāṇa*.

This *Prāṇa* is the vital force in every being, and the finest and highest action of *Prāṇa* is thought. Yet this thought, as we see, is not everything. There is also a kind of thought we call instinct, or unconscious thought—the lowest level <note>original: "plane"</note> of action. If a mosquito stings us, our hand strikes it automatically and instinctively without thinking. This is one expression of thought. All reflex actions of the body belong to this level of thought. 

There is then a still higher level of thought: the conscious. I reason, I judge, I think, and I weigh the pros and cons of certain things; yet even that is not everything. We know that reason is limited. There is only a certain extent to which reason can go; it cannot reach beyond that. The circle within which reason operates is very, very limited indeed. Yet, at the same time, we find facts rush into this circle. Like the arrival of comets, certain things enter this circle, and it is certain they come from outside these limits, even though our reason cannot go beyond them. The causes of the phenomena protruding

->RAJA YOGA<-

...themselves in this small limit are outside of this limit. The reason and the intellect cannot reach them, but, says the yogi <note>original: "Yogī"</note>, that is not all. The mind can exist on a still higher level: the super-conscious. When the mind has attained the state called *Samādhi* <term>Samādhi: A state of intense concentration and meditative union where the individual consciousness merges with the universal.</term>—perfect concentration or super-consciousness—it goes beyond the limits of reason and comes face to face with facts that no instinct or reason can ever know. All these workings <note>original: "manipulations"</note> of the body's subtle forces, the different expressions of *Prāṇa* <term>Prāṇa: The vital energy or life force that pervades the universe.</term>, can give a push to the mind if they are trained. The mind then rises higher and becomes super-conscious, acting from that elevated level.

In this universe, there is one continuous mass on every level <note>original: "plane"</note> of existence. Physically, this universe is a single unit; there is no real difference between the sun and you. A scientist will tell you it is only a fiction to say otherwise. There is no real difference between the table and me; the table is one point in the mass of matter, and I am another point. Each form represents, as it were, one whirlpool in the infinite ocean of matter, and these forms are not permanent. 

Just as millions of whirlpools may exist in a rushing stream, where the water in each is fresh every moment—turning round and round for a few seconds and then passing out the other end—the whole universe is one constantly changing mass of matter in which we are little whirlpools. A mass of matter enters them, spins around for a few years as the body of a human, changes, and is then whirled out in the form of, perhaps, an animal. From there, it rushes around to enter another whirlpool a few years later, called a lump of mineral. It is a state of constant change. Not one body is permanent. There is no such thing as "my body" or "your body," except in name. It is one huge mass of matter. One point is called the moon, another the sun, another a person, another the earth, another a plant, another a...

# PRĀṆA

...mineral. Not one is constant, but everything is changing; matter is eternally forming <note>original: "concreting"</note> and disintegrating. So it is with the mind. Matter is represented by the ether <term>Ether: In 19th-century science, a hypothesized medium that filled all space and transmitted light and heat.</term>; when the action of *Prāṇa* <term>Prāṇa: The vital energy or life force that pervades the universe.</term> is most subtle, this very ether—in its finer state of vibration—will represent the mind, and there it will still be one unbroken mass. If you can reach simply that subtle vibration, you will see and feel that the whole universe is composed of these subtle vibrations.

Sometimes certain drugs have the power to take us, as it were, beyond our senses and bring us to that condition. Many of you may remember the celebrated experiment of Sir Humphry Davy <note>original: "Sir Humphrey Davy"; a pioneering English chemist (1778–1829).</note> when the laughing gas <note>Nitrous oxide.</note> overpowered him. During the lecture, he remained motionless and dazed <note>original: "stupefied"</note>. Afterward, he said that the whole universe was made up of ideas. For the time being, the gross vibrations had ceased, and only the subtle vibrations, which he called the mind, were present to him. He could only see the subtle vibrations around him; everything had become thought. The whole universe was an ocean of thought, and he and everyone else had become little whirlpools of thought.

Thus, even in the universe of thought, we find this unity. At last, when we reach the Self <note>The Atman, or the individual's eternal soul which is identical to the universal reality.</note>, we know that the Self can only be One. Beyond motion, there is only the One. Even in manifest motion, there is only a unity. These facts can no more be denied, as modern science has demonstrated them. Modern physics has also demonstrated that the sum total of energy is the same throughout the universe. It has also been proved that this sum total of energy exists in two forms. It becomes potential—toned down and calmed—and next, it emerges manifested as all these various forces. Again, it goes back to the quiet state, and again it manifests. Thus, it goes on evolving and involving <term>Involving: Here used as "involution," the process where the universe or energy returns to a latent, potential, or seed state.</term> through eternity. The control of this *Prāṇa*, as stated before, is what is called *Prāṇāyāma* <term>Prāṇāyāma: The practice of breath control and the regulation of vital energy.</term>.
