It is not that breath produces the motion; on the contrary, the motion produces the breath. This motion draws in the air through a pump-like action.

The life force <note>original: "Prāṇa"</note> moves the lungs, and that motion of the lungs draws in the air. Therefore, this practice is not just breathing, but controlling that muscular power which moves the lungs. This is the same muscular power that travels through the nerves to the muscles and then to the lungs, making them move in a specific way. This is the vital energy <note>original: "Prāṇa"</note> which we must learn to control in this practice.

As you go higher, the atmosphere becomes thinner and more subtle. Or look at the ocean; as you go deeper and deeper, the density of the water increases. Those animals that live at the bottom of the sea can never come to the surface, or they would be crushed <note>original: "broken into pieces"</note> by the change in pressure.

Think of the entire universe as an ocean of ether <note>a theoretical substance once thought to fill all space</note>, vibrating under the influence of vital energy, consisting of layer after layer of varying degrees of vibration. Think of the whole system as a single circle, the center of which is perfection. The farther you move from the center, the slower the vibrations become. **Matter** is the outermost crust, next comes the **mind**, and the **spirit** is at the center.

# CHAPTER IV. THE PSYCHIC VITAL ENERGY <note>original: *PRĀṆA*</note>

According to the practitioners of Yoga <note>original: *Yogīs*</note>, there are two nerve currents in the spinal column, called the solar current <note>original: *Pingalā*</note> and the lunar current <note>original: *Iḍā*</note>, and there is a hollow canal called the central channel <note>original: *Suṣumnā*</note> running through the spinal cord. At the lower end of the hollow canal is what the practitioners call the "Lotus of the Coiled Energy <note>original: *Kuṇḍalinī*</note>." They describe it as triangular in shape, in which—in the symbolic language of Yoga—there is a power called the Coiled Energy <note>original: *Kuṇḍalinī*</note> folded up. 

When that Coiled Energy awakens, it tries to force a passage through this hollow canal. As it rises step-by-step, it is as if layer after layer of the mind opens up; the practitioner <note>original: *Yogī*</note> then experiences various visions and wonderful powers. When it reaches the brain, the practitioner is perfectly detached from both the body and the mind; the soul finds itself free. 

We know that the spinal cord is structured in a unique manner. If we look at the figure eight placed horizontally (∞), there are two parts, and these two parts are connected in the middle. Suppose you stack several of these eights one on top of the other; that would represent the structure of the spinal cord. The left side is the lunar current <note>original: *Iḍā*</note>, the right is the solar current <note>original: *Pingalā*</note>, and the hollow canal running through the center of the spinal cord is the central channel <note>original: *Suṣumnā*</note>. 

Where the spinal cord ends in the lower back vertebrae <note>original: lumbar vertebrae</note>, a fine fiber extends downward; the canal continues even within that fiber, though it is much thinner. The canal is closed at the bottom, which is located near the network of nerves at the base of the spine <note>original: sacral plexus</note>—which, according to modern physiology, is triangular in shape. The various nerve centers <note>original: plexuses</note> that have their headquarters in the spinal cord correspond very well to the different "lotuses" of the practitioner of Yoga.

It seeks to bridge ancient Yoga concepts with modern physiological structures like the spinal cord and the sacral plexus.</summary>

The practitioner of Yoga <note>original: *Yogī*</note> conceives of several energy centers, beginning with the "Root-Support" center <note>original: *Mūlādhāra*</note>, the basic one, and ending with the "Thousand-Petalled Lotus" <note>original: *Sahasrāra*</note> in the brain. If we take these different nerve clusters <note>original: plexuses</note> as representing these circles, the idea of the practitioner can be understood very easily in the language of modern physiology. We know there are two types of actions in these nerve currents: one inward-carrying <note>original: afferent</note>, the other outward-carrying <note>original: efferent</note>; one sensory and the other motor; one moving toward the center <note>original: centripetal</note>, and the other moving away from the center <note>original: centrifugal</note>. One carries sensations to the brain, and the other carries signals from the brain to the outer body. In the long run, these vibrations are all connected with the brain. 

We have to remember several other facts in order to clear the way for the explanation which is to come. At the brain, this spinal cord ends in a sort of bulb, in the medulla, which is not attached to the bone but floats in a fluid in the brain. Because of this, if there is a blow to the head, the force of that blow will be dispersed in the fluid and will not hurt the bulb. This will be an important fact as we go on. Secondly, we also have to know that, of all the centers, we must particularly remember three: the Root-Support center <note>original: *Mūlādhāra*</note> (the basis), the Thousand-Petalled Lotus <note>original: *Sahasrāra*</note> of the brain, and the "Own-Base" center <note>original: *Svādhiṣṭhāna*</note> (located next above the Root-Support). Next, we will take one fact from physics. We all hear of electricity and various other forces connected with it. What electricity is, no one knows; but, as far as it is known, it is a type of motion.

There are various other motions in the universe; what is the difference between them and electricity? Suppose this table moves—meaning that the molecules which compose this table are moving in different directions. If they are all made to move in the same direction, it will be electricity. Electric motion occurs when the molecules all move in the same direction. If all the air molecules in a room are made to move in the same direction, it would create a gigantic battery of electricity...

<summary>The author draws parallels between the spiritual anatomy of Yoga—specifically the energy centers like the Mūlādhāra and Sahasrāra—and modern physiological structures like the medulla and nerve currents. # THE PSYCHIC PRĀṆA

There is another point from physiology we must remember: the center that regulates the respiratory system (the breathing system) has a controlling influence over the system of nerve currents. This controlling center of the respiratory system is located in the spinal column, opposite the thorax <note>the chest region</note>. This center regulates the respiratory organs and also exercises some control over the secondary nerve centers.

Now we shall see why breathing is practiced. In the first place, rhythmical breathing causes all the molecules in the body to align in the same direction. When the mind transforms into will, the nerve currents change into a motion similar to electricity, because it has been proven that nerves show polarity under the action of electric currents. This shows that when the will evolves into nerve currents, it is transformed into something like electricity. When all the motions of the body have become perfectly rhythmical, the body has, as it were, become a gigantic battery of will. This tremendous will is exactly what the practitioner <term>Yogī</term> wants. This is, therefore, a physiological explanation of the breathing exercise. It tends to bring a rhythmic action to the body and helps us, through the respiratory center, to control the other centers. The aim of breath control <term>Prāṇāyāma</term> here is to rouse the coiled-up power in the root center <term>Mūlādhāra</term>, called the <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term>.

Everything that we see, imagine, or dream, we must perceive in space. This is ordinary space, called the <term>Mahākāśa</term>, or "great space." When a practitioner <term>Yogī</term> reads the thoughts of other men or perceives objects beyond the reach of the senses, he sees them in another sort of space called the <term>Chittākāśa</term>, the "mental space." When perception has become objectless and the soul shines in its own nature, it is called the <term>Chidākāśa</term>, or "knowledge space." When the <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term> is aroused and enters the central canal <term>Suṣumnā</term>, all perceptions occur in the mental space. When it has reached the end of that canal...

Using the analogy of electricity, we find that humans can only send a current along a wire, but nature requires no wires to send her immense currents. This proves that the wire is not truly necessary, but rather our inability to do without it compels us to use it.

Similarly, all the sensations and movements of the body are sent into and out of the brain through these "wires" of nerve fibers. The columns of sensory and motor fibers in the spinal cord are what the practitioners <term>Yogīs</term> call the left and right channels <term>Idā and Pingalā</term>. They are the main paths through which the incoming <note>afferent</note> and outgoing <note>efferent</note> currents travel. 

But why should the mind not send its signals without any wire, or react without any wires? We see that this is already being done in nature. The practitioner <term>Yogī</term> says if you can do that, you have escaped the bondage of matter. How can this be done? If you can make the current pass through the central channel <term>Suṣumnā</term>—the canal in the middle of the spinal column—you have solved the problem. The mind has created this network of the nervous system and must now break through it, so that no physical wires will be required to work through. Only then will all knowledge come to us, and we will no longer be enslaved by the body. This is why it is so important that you should gain control of the central channel <term>Suṣumnā</term>. If you can send the mental current through that hollow canal without any nerve fibers to act as wires, the practitioner <term>Yogī</term> says you have solved the riddle, and he also says it can be done.

In ordinary people, this central channel <term>Suṣumnā</term> is closed at the lower end; no activity passes through it. The practitioner <term>Yogī</term> proposes a practice by which it can be opened and the nerve currents made to travel through it. When a sensation is carried to a nerve center, the center reacts. In the case of automatic <note>involuntary</note> centers, this reaction is followed by motion. In the case of conscious centers, it is followed first by perception, and...

But what is the source of the action that triggers even this milder form of similar vibrations in the brain? It is certainly not from the original primary sensations. Therefore, it must be that the sensations are coiled up somewhere, and by their acting, they produce the mild reaction which we call dream perception.

# CHAPTER V.
## THE CONTROL OF PSYCHIC PRĀṆA <term>the vital life force as it relates to the mind and spirit</term>.

We must now deal with the exercises in *Prāṇāyāma* <term>the practice of controlling the breath to manage life energy</term>. We have seen that the first step, according to the *Yogīs* <term>practitioners of Yoga</term>, is to control the motion of the lungs. Our goal is to feel the subtle <note>original: "finer"</note> movements that are occurring within the body. Our minds have become focused on external things and have lost sight of the subtle movements inside. If we can begin to feel them, we can begin to control them. These nerve currents flow throughout the entire body, bringing life and vitality to every muscle, yet we do not feel them. The *Yogī* says we can learn to do so. How? By taking up and controlling all those movements of the *Prāṇa* <term>the universal life energy or vital force</term>, beginning with the motion of the lungs. When we have done that for a sufficient length of time, we shall also be able to control the more subtle <note>original: "finer"</note> movements.

We now come to the exercises in *Prāṇāyāma*. Sit upright; the body must be kept straight. The spinal cord, although it is inside the vertebral column <note>original: "vertabral column"</note>, is not attached to it. If you sit crookedly, you disturb this spinal cord, so let it be free. Any time that you sit crookedly and try to meditate, you are doing yourself an injury. The three parts of the body—the chest, the neck, and the head—must always be held straight, in one line. You will find that with a little practice, this will become as natural to you as breathing. The second requirement is to gain control of the nerves. We have seen that the nerve center that controls the respiratory organs has a sort of controlling effect on the other nerves, and rhythmical breathing is therefore necessary. The breathing that we generally use should not be called breathing at all; it is very irregular. Furthermore, there are some natural differences in breathing between men and women.

It emphasizes the necessity of maintaining a straight posture and rhythmical breathing during meditation.</summary>

# THE CONTROL OF PSYCHIC PRĀṆA <term>the vital life force as it relates to the mind and spirit</term>

The first lesson is simply to breathe in a rhythmic, measured way—in and out. This will harmonize the system. When you have practiced this for some time, you would do well to accompany your breathing with the mental repetition of a word, such as "Om" <term>a sacred sound and spiritual symbol in Indian religions</term> or any other sacred word. Let the word flow in and out with the breath, rhythmically and harmoniously, and you will feel your entire body become rhythmic. Then you will learn what true rest is. Compared to this, sleep is not really restful. Once this rest comes, even the most exhausted nerves will be calmed, and you will find that you have never truly rested before. In India, we use specific symbolic words instead of counting "one, two, three, four." This is why I advise you to join the mental repetition of "Om" or another sacred word to the *Prāṇāyāma* <term>the practice of breath control</term>.

The first effect of this practice will be a change in the face; harsh lines will disappear, and as the mind becomes calm, that serenity <note>original: "calm though calmness," likely a transcription error for "calm thought, calmness"</note> will show on the face. Next, a beautiful voice will develop. I have never seen a *Yogī* <term>a practitioner of Yoga</term> with a harsh or raspy voice. These signs will appear after a few months of practice. After practicing this initial breathing exercise for a few days, you should move on to a more advanced one. Slowly fill the lungs with breath through the *Iḍā* <term>the channel ending in the left nostril</term>, and at the same time, concentrate your mind on the nerve current. You are, as it were, sending the nerve current down the spinal column and striking firmly against that last nerve center <term>plexus</term>—the "basic lotus" <note>the Muladhara chakra</note>. This center is triangular in shape and is the seat of the *Kuṇḍalinī* <term>the latent spiritual energy located at the base of the spine</term>. Then, hold the current there for some time. Imagine that you are slowly drawing that nerve current up with the breath through the other side, then slowly exhale it through the right nostril. You will find this a little difficult to practice. The easiest way is to block the right nostril with your thumb and slowly draw in the breath through the left; then close both nostrils with your thumb and forefinger and imagine that you are sending that current down and striking the base of the *Suṣumṇā* <term>the central energy channel within the spine</term>; then take the...

At the same time, visualize the triangle <note>referring to the symbolic representation of the energy center at the base of the spine mentioned previously</note> and concentrate your mind on that center. The next breathing exercise involves slowly inhaling, immediately exhaling slowly, and then holding the breath out—using the same counts as before. The only difference is that in the first exercise, the breath was held inside, and in the second, it is held outside. This second method is the easier one. You must not practice the exercise where you hold the breath in the lungs too much. Do it only four times in the morning and four times in the evening. As you progress, you can slowly increase the duration and the number of repetitions. You will find that you have the strength to do so and that you take pleasure in it. Therefore, very carefully and cautiously increase the count to six seconds instead of four as you feel you have the capacity. It may be harmful if you practice it inconsistently.

Of these three processes—the purification of the nerves, holding the breath in, and holding the breath out—the first and the last are neither difficult nor dangerous. The more you practice the first one, the calmer you will become. Just think of the word “*Om*” <term>a sacred sound and spiritual symbol in Indian religions</term>, and you can practice even while you are sitting at your work. You will be much better for it. One day, if you practice diligently, the *Kuṇḍalinī* <term>the latent spiritual energy located at the base of the spine</term> will be awakened. For those who practice once or twice a day, a sense of calmness in the body and mind will develop, along with a beautiful voice; only for those who can progress further with this practice will this...

# THE CONTROL OF PSYCHIC PRĀṆA

Once the *Kuṇḍalinī* <term>the latent spiritual energy located at the base of the spine</term> is awakened, your entire nature will begin to change, and the book of knowledge will be opened. You will no longer need to consult books for wisdom; your own mind will have become your book, containing infinite knowledge. I have already spoken of the *Iḍā* and *Pingalā* <term>subtle energy channels located on the left and right sides of the spinal column</term> currents, which flow through either side of the spinal column, as well as the *Suṣumnā* <term>the central channel through which spiritual energy rises</term>, the passage through the center of the spinal cord. These three are present in every animal; any creature with a spinal column has these three lines of action. However, the *Yogīs* <term>practitioners of Yoga</term> claim that in ordinary humans, the *Suṣumnā* is closed. Its activity is not apparent there, while in the other two channels it is evident, carrying power to different parts of the body.

Only the *Yogī* has an open *Suṣumnā*. When this *Suṣumnā* current opens and thought begins to rise through it, we move beyond the senses. Our minds become super-sensory and superconscious; we transcend even the intellect, reaching a state that reasoning cannot attain. Opening that *Suṣumnā* is the primary goal of the *Yogī*. According to their teachings, various centers of distribution—or "lotuses," as they are called in more symbolic language—are arranged along this *Suṣumnā*. 

The lowest one is located at the bottom end of the spinal cord and is called *Mūlādhāra* <term>the root chakra</term>. The next is called *Svādhiṣṭhāna*, followed by *Maṇipūra*, *Anāhata*, *Viśuddha*, and *Ajna*. The last one, located in the brain, is the *Sahasrāra*, or "the thousand-petaled" <note>original: "Sahasrāra," meaning the crown chakra at the top of the head</note>. For now, we only need to consider two of these centers: the lowest, the *Mūlādhāra*, and the highest, the *Sahasrāra*. The lowest center is where all energy is stored; that energy must be drawn up from there and brought to the final center, the brain. The *Yogīs* claim that of all the energies the human body contains, the highest is what they call *Ojas* <term>the refined energy of vitality and spiritual power</term>. This *Ojas* is stored in the brain, and the more *Ojas* a person has in their head, the more powerful they are, the more...

-> **RAJA YOGA** <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"; the "Royal Path" of yoga focused on meditation and mental mastery</note> <-

...intellectual and spiritually strong a person is, the more they will possess this vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note>. This is the work of vital energy. One person may use beautiful language and express profound thoughts, yet they fail to impress others; another person may use neither beautiful language nor deep thoughts, yet their words are captivating. That is the power of vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note> manifesting. Every action coming from such a person will be impactful.

Now, within all of humanity, there is a greater or lesser amount of this vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note> stored up. All the forces working within the body, in their highest form, become this energy. You must remember that it is simply a matter of transformation. The same force that works externally as electricity or magnetism is converted into an inner force; the same forces that function as muscular energy are transformed into vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note>. 

The practitioners of yoga <note>original: "Yogīs"</note> say that the part of human energy expressed through sexual functions and sexual thoughts can be checked and controlled, and then easily transformed into vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note>. Because the lowest energy center <note>the Mūlādhāra chakra</term> guides these functions, the practitioner of yoga pays particular attention to that center. He attempts to take all of this sexual energy and convert it into vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note>. 

Only a chaste man or woman can cause this vital energy <note>original: "Ojas"</note> to rise and be stored in the brain. This is why chastity has always been considered the highest virtue; man feels that if he is unchaste, his spirituality fades, and he loses mental vigor and strong moral stamina. This is why all the religious orders in the world that have produced spiritual giants have always insisted on intense chastity. This is the reason monks exist, choosing to give up marriage. There must be perfect chastity in thought, word, and deed. Without it, the practice of Royal Yoga <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"</note> is dangerous and may even lead to insanity. If people practice Royal Yoga while living an impure life, how can they expect to become true practitioners of yoga <note>original: "Yogīs"</note>?

***

**Vocabulary**
*   **Vital Energy** <note>original: "Ojas"</note>: The refined energy within the body that fuels spiritual and intellectual power.
*   **Practitioners of Yoga** <note>original: "Yogīs"</note>: Those who practice the disciplines of yoga.
*   **Royal Yoga** <note>original: "Rāja Yoga"</note>: The branch of yoga focused on controlling the mind and achieving enlightenment through meditation.
*   **Chastity**: The practice of refraining from sexual activity; in this context, it refers to the conservation of energy for spiritual use.
*   **Transformation**: The process of changing one form of energy (like physical or sexual energy) into a higher spiritual form.
*   **Spiritual Giants**: Great teachers or saints who have achieved high levels of spiritual realization.
*   **Mental Vigor**: The strength and clarity of the mind.
*   **Sexual Energy**: The creative biological force that yogis believe can be redirected to fuel the brain.

### CHAPTER VI. WITHDRAWAL OF THE SENSES AND CONCENTRATION <note>original: "PRATYĀHĀRA AND DHĀRAṆĀ"</note>

The next step is called withdrawal of the senses <note>original: "Pratyāhāra"</note>. What is this? You know how perceptions occur. First of all, there are the external instruments (the physical sense organs), then the internal organs (the sensory nerves), acting in the body through the brain centers, and finally there is the mind. When these all come together and attach themselves to some external object, then we perceive that thing. At the same time, it is very difficult to concentrate the mind and attach it to only one organ; the mind is currently a slave to its environment.

We hear "be good," "be good," and "be good" taught all over the world. There is hardly a child born in any country who has not been told "do not steal" or "do not tell a lie," but nobody tells the child how he can avoid doing so. Talking alone will never achieve it. Why should he not become a thief? We do not teach him how *not* to steal; we simply tell him "do not steal." Only when we teach him to control his mind do we really help him. 

All actions, internal and external, occur when the mind joins itself to certain centers, and these centers are called the organs <note>the author refers here to the "Indriyas," the subtle sensory instruments in the nervous system rather than the physical ears or eyes</note>. Whether we want it to or not, the mind is drawn to connect itself to these centers. This is why people perform foolish deeds and feel misery—actions they would not take if the mind were under control. 

What would be the result of controlling the mind? It would then not join itself to the centers of perception, and naturally, our feelings and our will would be under our control. It is clear so far. Is it possible? It is perfectly possible. You see it in modern times; the faith-healers teach people to deny misery, pain, and evil. Their philosophy is somewhat indirect, but it is a part of Yoga into which they have somehow stumbled. In...

# RAJA YOGA

In those cases where they succeed in helping a person cast off suffering by denying its existence, they have actually taught a part of sensory withdrawal <note>original: "Pratyāhāra"</note>. This is because they have made the student’s mind strong enough to refuse to accept the records of the senses. In a similar way, hypnotists use suggestion to excite a type of unhealthy <note>original: "morbid"</note> sensory withdrawal in the patient for a time. This so-called hypnotic suggestion can only work on a diseased body and a clouded mind. Until the practitioner—through a fixed gaze or other means—has succeeded in putting the subject's mind into a passive, unhealthy state, his suggestions never work.

Now, the control over the brain centers that is established in a hypnotic patient, or a patient of faith-healing, is completely unacceptable because it leads to eventual ruin. It is not truly controlling the brain centers through the power of one’s own will; instead, it is like stunning the patient’s mind for a time with sudden blows delivered by someone else’s will. It is not like using reins and physical strength to guide the wild run of a fiery team of horses; rather, it is like asking someone else to deliver heavy blows to the horses' heads to stun them into temporary gentleness. Through each of these processes, the person being operated upon loses a part of their mental energy. Eventually, instead of gaining the power of perfect control, the mind becomes a shapeless, powerless mass, and the patient's only destination is the psychiatric hospital <note>original: "lunatic asylum"</note>.

Every attempt at control that is not voluntary—that is, not achieved with the controller’s own mind—is not only disastrous, but it also fails to achieve its purpose. The goal of every soul is freedom and mastery: freedom from the slavery of matter and thought, and mastery over both external and internal nature. Instead of leading toward that goal, every "will-current" from another person—in whatever form it reaches us, whether as direct control of my organs or by forcing me to control them while in an unhealthy state—only...

...fastens <note>original: "rivets"</note> one more link to the already heavy chain of bondage formed by past thoughts and past superstitions. Therefore, be careful how you allow yourselves to be influenced <note>original: "acted upon"</note> by others. Be careful how you unknowingly lead another to ruin. It is true that some people succeed in doing good for many for a while by giving their natural tendencies <note>original: "propensities"</note> a new direction. But at the same time, they bring ruin to millions through the unconscious hypnotic suggestions they spread, triggering in men and women that unhealthy <note>original: "morbid"</note>, passive, hypnotic state which eventually leaves them almost soulless. Therefore, anyone who asks another to believe blindly, or who drags humanity behind them by controlling others with their own superior will, is a person who harms humanity—even if they did not intend to do so.

Therefore, use your own minds. Control your own body and mind yourselves. Remember that unless you are in a weakened <note>original: "diseased"</note> state, no external <note>original: "extraneous"</note> will can influence you. Avoid everyone, no matter how great and good they may seem, who asks you to believe blindly. All over the world, there have been religious sects characterized by dancing, jumping, and shouting <note>original: "howling"</note> that spread like infections when they begin to sing, dance, and preach; these also fall into this category. They exercise a peculiar control for a short time over sensitive people, but unfortunately, in the long run, they often cause whole races to decline <note>original: "degenerate"</note>. 

Indeed, it is healthier for an individual or a race to remain "wicked" than to be made seemingly good by such unhealthy external control. One’s heart sinks to think of the amount of injury done to humanity by such irresponsible, yet well-meaning religious fanatics. They little realize that the minds which reach a sudden spiritual awakening <note>original: "upheaval"</note> under their suggestions—through music and prayers—are simply making themselves passive, unhealthy, and powerless. They are opening themselves up to any other suggestion, no matter how evil it may be. These ignorant, deluded people little dream that while they are congratulating themselves upon—

Through their miraculous power to transform human hearts—a power they believe was poured upon them by some Being above the clouds—they are actually sowing the seeds of future decay, of crime, of insanity <note>original: "lunacy"</note>, and of death. Therefore, beware of everything that takes away your freedom. Know that it is dangerous, and avoid it by all the means in your power. He who has succeeded in attaching or detaching his mind to or from the sensory centers at will has succeeded in *<term>Pratyāhāra</term>*, which means “gathering towards” <note>In yoga, this refers to the withdrawal of the senses from external objects.</note>, checking the outgoing powers of the mind, and freeing it from the slavery <note>original: "thraldom"</note> of the senses. When we can do this, we truly possess a character; then alone shall we have taken a long step toward freedom. Before that, we are mere machines.

How hard it is to control the mind! It has been well compared to a crazed monkey. There was a monkey, restless by its own nature, as all monkeys are. As if that were not enough, someone made him drink freely of wine, so that he became still more restless. Then a scorpion stung him. When a man is stung by a scorpion, he jumps about for a whole day, so the poor monkey found his condition worse than ever. To complete his misery, a demon entered into him. What language can describe the uncontrollable restlessness of that monkey? The human mind is like that monkey; incessantly active by its own nature, then it becomes drunk with the wine of desire, thus increasing its turbulence. After desire takes possession, comes the sting of the scorpion of jealousy of others whose desires are fulfilled, and last of all the demon of pride takes possession of the mind, making it think itself all-important. How hard it is to control such a mind!

The first lesson, then, is to sit for some time and let the mind run on. The mind is bubbling up all the time. It is like that monkey jumping about. Let the monkey jump as much...

### PRATYĀHĀRA AND DHĀRAṆĀ

as he can; you simply wait and watch. "Knowledge is power," says the proverb, and that is true. Until you know what the mind is doing, you cannot control it. Give it the full length of its reins; many hideous thoughts may enter it; you will be astonished that it was possible for you to think such thoughts. But you will find that each day the mind’s vagaries become less and less violent, and that each day it becomes calmer. In the first few months, you will find that the mind has a thousand thoughts; later, you will find that it is reduced to perhaps seven hundred; and after a few more months, it will have fewer and fewer, until at last it is under perfect control. But we must patiently practice every day. 

As soon as the steam <note>OCR reads "stream," likely referring to the power source for an engine.</note> is turned on, the engine must run, and as soon as things are before us, we must perceive; so a man, to prove that he is not a machine, must demonstrate that he is under the control of nothing. This control of the mind, and not allowing it to join itself to the sensory centers, is *<term>Pratyāhāra</term>*. How is this practiced? It is a long task, not to be done in a day. Only after a patient, continuous struggle for years can we succeed.

The next lesson depends on this. After you have practiced *<term>Pratyāhāra</term>* for a time, take the next step, *<term>Dhāraṇā</term>*, which is holding the mind to certain points. What is meant by holding the mind to certain points? It means forcing the mind to feel certain parts of the body to the exclusion of others. For instance, try to feel only the hand, to the exclusion of other parts of the body. When the *<term>Chitta</term>*, or mind-stuff, is confined and limited to a certain place, this is called *<term>Dhāraṇā</term>*. This *<term>Dhāraṇā</term>* is of various sorts, and along with it, it is better to have a little play of the imagination. For instance, the mind should be made to think of one point in the heart. That is very difficult; an easier way is to imagine a lotus there. That lotus is full of light—an effulgent <note>shining brightly; radiant</note> light. Put

The <term>Yogī</term> must always practice. He should try to live alone; the companionship of different sorts of people distracts his mind. He should not speak much, because speaking distracts the mind; nor should he work much, because too much work distracts the mind. The mind cannot be controlled after a whole day’s hard work. One with such determination becomes a <term>Yogī</term>. Such is the power of good that even the least amount done will bring a great amount of benefit. It will not hurt anyone, but will benefit everyone. First of all, it will tone down nervous excitement, bring calmness, and enable us to see things more clearly. The temperament will improve, and the health will be better. Sound health will be one of the first signs, along with a beautiful voice. Defects in the voice will be changed. This will be among the first of the many effects that will come. 

Those who practice hard will see many other signs. Sometimes there will be sounds, like a peal of bells heard at a distance, blending together and falling on the ear as one continuous sound. Sometimes things will be seen, such as little specks of light floating and becoming bigger and bigger; when these things occur, know that you are progressing very fast. 

Those who want to be <term>Yogīs</term> and practice very hard must take some care with their diet at first. Those who want to make very rapid progress will find it an advantage if they can live on milk and cereals alone for some months. But for those who want only a little practice for an everyday, business-oriented life, let them simply not eat too much; otherwise, they may eat whatever they please.

For those who want to make faster progress and to practice intensely, a strict diet is absolutely necessary. As the physical constitution becomes finer and finer, at first you will find

You will find that when you are beginning to concentrate, the dropping of a pin will seem like a thunderbolt crashing through your brain. The organs become more refined, and the perceptions become more acute. These are the stages through which we must pass, and all those who persevere will succeed. Give up all arguing and other distractions. Is there anything of value in this dry intellectual jargon? It only throws the mind off its balance and disturbs it. These things must be experienced through realization. Will talking do that? Therefore, give up all vain talk. Read only those books written by people who have achieved realization.

Be like the pearl oyster. There is a beautiful Indian fable that if it rains when the star <term>Svātī</term> is rising, and a drop of rain falls into an oyster, that drop will become a pearl. The oysters know this, so they come to the surface when that star shines and wait to catch the precious raindrop. When one falls into the shell, the oyster quickly closes it and dives down to the bottom of the sea, patiently developing the drop into a pearl there. We should be like that. First hear, then understand, and then—leaving all distractions—shut our minds to outside influences and devote ourselves to developing the truth within us. There is a danger of wasting our energies by taking up an idea only for its novelty and then abandoning it for another that is newer. Take one thing up and do it; see it through to the end, and do not give it up before you have reached that end. He who can become consumed by an idea, he alone will see the light. Those who only take a nibble here and there will never achieve anything. They may titillate their nerves for a moment, but it will end there. They will be slaves in the hands of nature and will never get beyond the senses.

Those who truly want to be <term>*Yogīs*</term> must give up, once and for all, this habit of nibbling at things. Take up one idea. Let the brain, the body, muscles, nerves, and every part of your body be full of that idea, and just leave every other idea alone. This is the way to success, and this is the way great spiritual giants are produced. Others are mere talking machines. If we truly want to be blessed and make others blessed, we must go deeper; as a first step, do not disturb the mind and do not associate with people whose ideas are disturbing. All of you know that certain people, certain places, and certain foods repel you. Avoid them; and those who want to reach the highest state must avoid all company, whether good or bad. Practice hard; whether you live or die does not matter. You have to plunge in and work without thinking of the result. If you are brave enough, in six months you will be a perfect <term>*Yogī*</term>. But for others—those who take up just a bit of it, a little of everything—they get no higher. It is of no use simply to take a course of lessons. Those who are full of <term>*Tamas*</term>, the ignorant and the dull, whose minds never focus on any idea and who only crave for something to entertain them—religion and philosophy are simply forms of entertainment to them. They come to religion as if to an amusement and get only that little bit of entertainment. These are the people who lack perseverance. They hear a talk, think it is very nice, and then go home and forget all about it. To succeed, you must have tremendous perseverance and a tremendous will. “I will drink the ocean,” says the persevering soul. “By my will, mountains will crumble.” Have that kind of energy, that kind of will; work hard, and you will reach the goal.

# CHAPTER VII.
# <term>DHYANA</term> AND <term>SAMĀDHI</term>.

We have finished a brief review of the different steps in <term>Rāja Yoga</term>, except for the more subtle ones: the training in concentration, which is the aim and the goal to which <term>Rāja Yoga</term> will lead us. We see, as human beings, that all our knowledge which is called rational is related to consciousness. I am conscious of this table, I am conscious of your presence, and so forth; this makes me know that you are here, that the table is here, and that the things I see, feel, and hear are here. At the same time, there is a very large part of my existence of which I am not conscious—all the various organs inside the body, the different parts of the brain, and the brain itself; no one is conscious of these things.

When I eat food, I do it consciously; when I digest it, I do it unconsciously; when the food is turned into blood, it is done unconsciously; when all the different parts of my body are made out of the blood, it is done unconsciously. And yet, it is I who am doing this; there cannot be twenty people in one body. How do I know that I do it, and no one else? It may be argued that my business is only in eating and digesting the food, and that the construction of the body out of that food is done for me by someone else. That cannot be, because it can be demonstrated that almost every action of which we are currently unconscious can be brought back to the level of consciousness. The heart is beating apparently without our control; none of us here can control the heart; it goes on its own way. But through practice, people can bring even the heart under control, until it will beat at will, slowly, or

<summary>This page introduces the final stages of Raja Yoga, focusing on the distinction between conscious actions and the unconscious biological processes of the body. It argues that these unconscious processes are still performed by the individual and can eventually be brought under conscious control through dedicated practice.</summary>
<keywords>Rāja Yoga, Dhyana, Samādhi, consciousness, concentration, unconscious mind, self-control, physiology</keywords>

RAJA YOGA

---

quickly, or almost stop. Nearly every part of the body can be brought under control. What does this show? It shows that these things which are beneath consciousness are also worked by us, but we are doing them unconsciously. We have, then, two planes in which the human mind works. First is the conscious plane; that is to say, the sort of work which is always accompanied by the feeling of egoism. The part of mind-work which is unaccompanied by the feeling of egoism is unconscious work, and the part which is accompanied by the feeling of egoism is conscious work. In lower animals, this unconscious work is called instinct. In higher animals—and in the highest of animals, man—the second part, which is accompanied by the feeling of egoism, prevails and is called conscious work.

But it does not end here. There is an even higher plane upon which the mind can work. It can go beyond consciousness. Just as unconscious work is beneath consciousness, so there is another type of work which is above consciousness and which, also, is not accompanied by the feeling of egoism. The feeling of egoism exists only on the middle plane. When the mind is above or below that line, there is no feeling of "I," and yet the mind works. When the mind goes beyond this line of self-consciousness, it is called *Samādhi*, or super-consciousness. It is above consciousness. How, for instance, do we know that a man in *Samādhi* has not fallen below his consciousness—that he has not degenerated instead of going higher? In both cases, the actions are unaccompanied by egoism. The answer is that by the effects and results of the work, we know that which is below and that which is above. When a man goes into a deep sleep, he enters a plane beneath consciousness. He works the body all the time; he breathes and moves the body, perhaps, in his sleep, without any accompanying feeling of ego; he is

unconscious, and when he returns from his sleep, he is the same man who went into it. The sum total of the knowledge which he had before he went to sleep remains the same; it has not increased at all. No enlightenment has come. But if a man enters *Samādhi*—if he enters it as a fool—he comes out as a sage.

What makes the difference? From one state, a man comes out as the very same man who went in, but from another state, the man becomes enlightened: a sage, a prophet, a saint, his whole character changed, his life changed and illumined. These are the two effects. Now, since the effects are different, the causes must be different. As this illumination, with which a man returns from *Samādhi*, is much higher than anything that can be obtained from unconsciousness, or much higher than can be obtained by reasoning in a conscious state, it must therefore be super-consciousness, and *Samādhi* is called the super-conscious state.

This, in short, is the idea of *Samādhi*. What is its application? The application is here. The field of reason, or of the conscious workings of the mind, is narrow and limited. There is a small circle within which human reason must move. It cannot go beyond it. Every attempt to go beyond is impossible, yet it is beyond this circle of reason that everything humanity holds most dear lies. All these questions—whether there is an immortal soul, whether there is a God, whether there is any supreme intelligence guiding this universe—are beyond the field of reason. Reason can never answer these questions. What does reason say? It says, “I am agnostic; I know neither yes nor no.” Yet these questions are important to us. Without a proper answer to them, human life will be impossible. All our ethical theories, all our moral attitudes, and all that is good and great in human nature have been molded upon answers that have

<meta>This page continues the argument that the most vital human questions regarding the soul, God, and morality lie beyond the reach of reason, and that without these answers, ethical systems like utilitarianism lack a solid foundation.</meta>

<header>RAJA YOGA</header>

...come from beyond that circle. It is very important, therefore, that we should have answers to these questions; without such answers, human life will be impossible. If life is only a brief five-minute thing, if the universe is only a “fortuitous combination of atoms,” then why should I do good to another? Why should there be mercy, justice, or fellow feeling? The best thing for this world would be to "make hay while the sun shines," each man for himself. If there is no hope, why should I love my brother and not cut his throat? If there is nothing beyond, if there is no freedom but only rigorous, dead laws, I should only try to make myself happy here. You will find people saying nowadays that they have utilitarian grounds as the basis of all morality. What is this basis? Procuring the greatest amount of happiness for the greatest number. Why should I do this? Why should I not produce the greatest unhappiness for the greatest number, if that serves my purpose? How will utilitarians answer this question? How do you know what is right or what is wrong? I am impelled by my desire for happiness and I fulfill it, and it is in my nature; I know nothing beyond. I have these desires and must fulfill them; why should you complain? Where do all these truths about human life, about morality, about the immortal soul, about God, about love and sympathy, about being good, and, above all, about being unselfish, come from?

All ethics, all human action, and all human thought rest upon this one idea of unselfishness; the whole concept of human life can be put into that one word: unselfishness. Why should we be unselfish? Where is the necessity, the force, or the power of my being unselfish? Why should I be? You call yourself a rational man, a utilitarian, but if you do not show me a reason, I say you are irrational. Show me the reason why I should not be selfish, why I should not be like a brute,

Because Mr. and Mrs. So-and-so say so does not carry weight with me. What is the utility of my being unselfish? My utility is to be selfish, if utility means the greatest amount of happiness. I may get the greatest amount of happiness by cheating and robbing others. What is the answer? The utilitarian can never give it. The answer is that this world is only one drop in an infinite ocean, one link in an infinite chain. Where did those who preached unselfishness, and taught it to the human race, get this idea? We know it is not instinctive; the animals, which have instinct, do not know it. Neither is it reason; does reason know anything about these ideas? Where did they come from?

We find, in studying history, one fact held in common by all the great teachers of religion the world has ever had; they all claim to have received these truths from beyond, though many of them did not know what they were receiving. For instance, one would say that an angel came down in the form of a human being, with wings, and said to him, "Hear, O man, this is the message." Another says that a *Deva*, a bright being, appeared to him. Another says he dreamed that his ancestor came and told him all these things. He did not know anything beyond that. But this thing is common, that all claim either that they saw angels, or heard the voice of God, or saw some wonderful vision. All claim that this knowledge came to them from beyond, not through their reasoning power. What does the science of *Yoga* teach? It teaches that they were right in claiming that this knowledge came to them from beyond reasoning, but that it came from within themselves.

The *Yogī* teaches that the mind itself has a higher state of existence, beyond reason, a super-conscious state, and when

When the mind reaches that higher state, then this knowledge, beyond reasoning, comes to a man—metaphysical knowledge, beyond all physical knowledge. Metaphysical and transcendental knowledge comes to that man, and this state of going beyond reason, transcending ordinary human nature, may sometimes come by chance to a man who does not understand its science; he, as it were, stumbles into it. When he stumbles into it, he generally interprets it as coming from the outside. So this explains why an inspiration, or this transcendental knowledge, may be the same in different countries, but in one country it will seem to come through an angel, in another through a *Deva*, and in another through God. What does it mean? It means that the mind brought the knowledge by its own nature, and that the discovery of the knowledge was interpreted according to the beliefs and education of the person through whom it came. The real fact is that these various men, as it were, stumbled into this super-conscious state.

The *Yogī* says there is a great danger in stumbling into this state. In many cases there is the danger of the brain being destroyed, and, as a rule, you will find that all those men—however great they were—who have stumbled into this super-conscious state without understanding it, grope in the dark and generally have some quaint superstition along with their knowledge. They open themselves to hallucination. Mohammed claimed that the Angel Gabriel came to him in a cave one day and took him on the heavenly horse, Harak <note>Harak: Likely a reference to Al-Buraq, the winged steed in Islamic tradition.</note>, and he visited the heavens. But, despite all that, Mohammed spoke some wonderful truths. If you read the Qur’an, you find the most wonderful truths mixed with these superstitions. How will you explain it? That man was inspired, no doubt, but that inspiration was, as it were, stumbled upon. He was not a trained *Yogī*, and did not know

So we see, in studying the lives of all these great teachers, that there was this danger. Yet we find, at the same time, that they were all inspired. Somehow or other they reached this super-conscious state; however, whenever a prophet entered that state by simple force of emotion—just by heightening his emotional nature—he brought back some truths from that state, but also some fanaticism and some superstition which harmed the world as much as the greatness of the teaching did good. To find any logic in this mass of inconsistency we call human life, we must transcend our reason, but we must do it scientifically, slowly, and through regular practice, and we must cast off all superstition. We must approach it just like any other science; we must have reason to lay our foundation, we must follow reason as far as it leads, and when reason fails, reason itself will show us the way to the highest plane. So, whenever we hear someone say, “I am inspired,” and then speak the most irrational nonsense, simply reject it. Why? Because these three states of the mind—instinct, reason, and super-consciousness (or the unconscious, conscious, and super-conscious states)—belong to one and the same mind. There are not three minds in one person, but one develops into the other. Instinct develops into reason, and reason into transcendental consciousness; therefore, one never contradicts the other. So, whenever you encounter wild statements that contradict human reason and common sense, reject them without any fear, because true inspiration will never contradict, but will fulfill. Just as you

...find the great prophets saying, “I do not come to destroy but to fulfill,” so this inspiration always comes to fulfill reason and is in direct harmony with reason; and whenever it contradicts reason, you must know that it is not inspiration.

All the different steps in Yoga are intended to bring us scientifically to the super-conscious state, or *Samādhi*. Furthermore, it is a most vital point to understand that inspiration is as much in every man’s nature as it was in the ancient prophets. These prophets were not unique; they were just the same as you or I. They were great *Yogīs*. They had gained this super-consciousness, and you and I can gain the same. They were not peculiar people. The very fact that one man ever reached that state proves that it is possible for every man to do so. Not only is it possible, but every man must, eventually, reach that state, and that is religion. Experience is the only teacher we have. We may talk and reason all our lives without ever understanding a word of truth until we experience it ourselves. You cannot hope to make a man a surgeon by simply giving him a few books. You cannot satisfy my curiosity to see a country by showing me a map; I must have actual experience. Maps can only create a little curiosity in us to gain more perfect knowledge. Beyond that, they have no value whatever. All clinging to books only degenerates the human mind. Was there ever a more horrible blasphemy than to say that all the knowledge of God is confined in this or that book? How dare men call God infinite, and yet try to compress Him within the covers of a little book! Millions of people have been killed because they did not believe what the books say—because they would not see all the knowledge of God within the covers of a book. Of course, this killing and murdering has passed, but the world is still tremendously bound by a belief in books.

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>DHYANA AND SAMADHI</header>

In order to reach the super-conscious state in a scientific manner, we must pass through these various steps that I have been teaching you in *Rāja Yoga*. After *Pratyāhāra* and *Dhāraṇā*, which I taught you in the last lecture, we come to *Dhyāna*, or meditation. When the mind has been trained to remain fixed on a certain internal or external location, it gains the power of flowing, as it were, in an unbroken current towards that point. This state is called *Dhyāna*. When this power of *Dhyāna* has been intensified so much that it is able to reject the external part of perception and remain meditating only on the internal part—the meaning—that state is called *Samādhi*. The three—*Dhāraṇā*, *Dhyāna*, and *Samādhi*—together are called *Samyama*. That is, if the mind can first concentrate upon an object, and then is able to continue in that concentration for a length of time, and then, by continued concentration, to dwell only on the internal part of the perception of which the object was the effect, everything comes under the control of such a mind.

This meditative state is the highest state of existence. As long as there is desire, no real happiness can come. It is only the contemplative, witness-like study of objects that brings us to real enjoyment and happiness. The animal finds its happiness in the senses, the human in the intellect, and God in spiritual contemplation. It is only to the soul that has attained this contemplative state that the world has really become beautiful. To the person who desires nothing and does not involve himself with them, the manifold changes of nature are one panorama of beauty and sublimity.

These ideas must be understood in *Dhyāna*, or meditation. We hear a sound. First, there is the external vibration; second, the nerve motion that carries it to the mind; third, the reaction from the mind, along with which flashes the knowledge of the object which was the external

In the language of physiology, these are called the ethereal vibration, the motion in the nerve and brain, and the mental reaction. Now these, though they are distinct processes, have become mixed up in such a fashion as to become quite indistinct. In fact, we cannot now perceive any of these causes; we only perceive the effect of these three, an effect which we call the external object. Every act of perception includes these three, and there is no reason why we should not be able to distinguish between them.

When, through the previous preparations, the mind becomes strong and controlled, and the power of finer perception has been attained, then the mind should be employed in meditation. This meditation must begin with gross objects and slowly rise to finer ones, then to finer and finer still, until it has become objectless. The mind should first be employed in perceiving the external causes of sensations, then the internal motions, and finally the reaction of the mind. When it has succeeded in perceiving the external causes of sensations by themselves, it will acquire the power of perceiving all fine material existence, all fine bodies and forms. When it succeeds in perceiving the motions inside by themselves, it will gain control over all mental waves, in itself or in others, even before they have translated themselves into physical forces; and when he is able to perceive the mental reaction by itself, the *Yogī* will acquire the knowledge of everything, as every sensible object and every thought is the result of this reaction. Then he will have seen, as it were, the very foundations of his mind, and it will be under his perfect control. Different powers will come to the *Yogī*, and if he yields to the temptations of any one of these, the road to

And the *Yogī* will find himself as he is and as he always was: the essence of knowledge, the immortal, and the all-pervading.

*Samādhi* is the property of every human being—indeed, of every animal. From the lowest animal to the highest angelic being, at some time or another, each one will have to reach that state; then, and then alone, will religion truly begin for him. And all this time, what are we doing? We are only struggling toward that stage; there is no difference between us and those who have no religion, because we have had no experience. What is concentration good for, except to bring us to this experience? Each of the steps to attain this *Samādhi* has been reasoned out, properly adjusted, and scientifically organized; when faithfully practiced, they will surely lead us to the desired end. Then all sorrows and all miseries will cease; the seeds of actions will be burned, and the soul will be free forever.

## CHAPTER VIII.
### RĀJA YOGA IN BRIEF.

This is a summary of *Rāja Yoga* freely translated from the *Kurma Purāna*.
The fire of *Yoga* burns the cage of sin that surrounds a person. Knowledge becomes purified, and *Nirvāna* is directly obtained. From *Yoga* comes knowledge; knowledge, in turn, helps the *Yogī*. The Lord is pleased with whoever combines both *Yoga* and knowledge. Know those who practice *Mahāyoga*—whether once, twice, or three times a day, or constantly—to be gods. *Yoga* is divided into two parts. One is called *Abhāva*, and the other *Mahāyoga*. Where one’s self is meditated upon as zero and devoid of quality, that is called *Abhāva*; through this, the *Yogī* realizes his Self. That in which one sees the Self as full of bliss, free from all impurities, and one with God, is called *Mahāyoga*. The other forms of *Yoga* that we read and hear about do not compare even slightly to this great *Brahmayoga*, in which the *Yogī* finds himself and the whole universe to be God Himself. This is the highest of all *Yogas*.

These are the steps in *Rāja Yoga*: *Yama*, *Niyama*, *Āsana*, *Prānāyāma*, *Pratyāhāra*, *Dhāranā*, *Dhyāna*, and *Samādhi*. Of these, non-injury to anyone, truthfulness, non-covetousness, chastity, and not receiving gifts from others are called *Yama*; it purifies the mind, the *Chitta*. Always avoiding the production of pain in any living being, through thought, word, and deed, is what is called *Ahimsa*, or non-injury. There is no virtue higher than this non-injury. There is no happiness higher than what a person obtains through this attitude of non-offensiveness toward all creation. By truth we attain to

<summary>The author provides a summary of Rāja Yoga from the Kurma Purāna, distinguishing between Abhāva and Mahāyoga while identifying the eight steps of Yoga, beginning with the moral restraints of Yama.</summary>
<keywords>Rāja Yoga, Kurma Purāna, Mahāyoga, Abhāva, Yama, Ahimsa, Chitta, spiritual purification</keywords>

->RĀJA YOGA IN BRIEF 73<-

work. Through truth everything is attained; in truth everything is established. Relating facts as they are; this is truth. Not taking others' goods by stealth or by force is called <term>*Asteyam*</term>, non-covetousness. Chastity in thought, word, and deed, always, and in all conditions, is what is called <term>*Brahmacharya*</term>. Not receiving any present from anybody, even when one is suffering terribly, is what is called <term>*Aparigraha*</term>. When a person receives a gift from another person, the theory is that their heart becomes impure; they become low, they lose their independence, and they become bound and attached. 

The following are helps to success in <term>*Yoga*</term>: <term>*Niyama*</term>, regular habits and observances; <term>*Tapas*</term>, austerity; <term>*Srādhyāya*</term>, study; <term>*Santela*</term> <note>Likely a variant or transcription of *Santosha*, meaning contentment.</note>, contentment; <term>*Saucham*</term>, purity; <term>*Iśvara pranidhāna*</term>, worshipping God. Fasting, or in other ways controlling the body, is called the physical <term>*Tapas*</term>.

Repeating the <term>*Vedas*</term> and other <term>*Mantrams*</term>, by which the <term>*Sattva*</term> material in the body is purified, is called study, <term>*Srādhyāya*</term>. There are three sorts of repetitions of these <term>*Mantrams*</term>. One is called the verbal, another semi-verbal, and the third mental. The verbal or audible is the lowest, and the inaudible is the highest of all. The repetition which is so loud that anyone can hear it is the verbal; the next one is where only the organs begin to vibrate, but no sound is heard—another person sitting near cannot hear what is being said. That in which there is no sound, only mental repetition of the <term>*Mantram*</term> while simultaneously thinking of its meaning, is called the "mental muttering" and is the highest. 

The sages have said that there are two sorts of purification: external and internal. The purification for the body is by water, earth, or other materials; this is external purification, such as bathing, etc. Purification of the mind by truth and by all the other virtues is what is called internal purification. Both are necessary. It is not sufficient that a person should be internally pure and

externally dirty. When both are not attainable, internal purity is better, but no one will be a <term>Yogī</term> until he has both. Worship is performed through praise, memory, and devotion to God.

We have spoken about <term>Yama</term> and <term>Niyama</term>; next comes <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>. <term>Prāṇa</term> means the vital forces in one’s own body, and <term>Yama</term> means controlling them. There are three types of <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>: the simple, the middle, and the high. The whole of <term>Prāṇāyāma</term> is divided into two parts: one is called filling, and the other is called emptying. When you begin with twelve seconds, it is the lowest <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>; when you begin with twenty-four seconds, it is the middle <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>; and the best <term>Prāṇāyāma</term> is that which begins with thirty-six seconds. That <term>Prāṇāyāma</term> which first produces perspiration, then vibration of the body, and finally rising from the seat and the joining of the man’s soul with great bliss is the very highest <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>. There is a <term>Mantram</term> called the <term>Gāyatri</term>. It is a very holy verse of the <term>Vedas</term>: “We meditate on the glory of that Being who has produced this universe; may He enlighten our minds.” Then <term>Om</term> is joined to it at the beginning and end. In one <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>, repeat three <term>Gāyatris</term>. In all books, they speak of <term>Prāṇāyāma</term> being divided into <term>Rechaka</term> (rejecting or exhaling), <term>Pūraka</term> (inhaling), and <term>Kumbhaka</term> (restraining or stationary). The <term>Indriyas</term>, the organs of the senses, act outwardly and come into contact with external objects. Bringing them under the control of the will is what is called <term>Pratyāhāra</term>; "gathering towards oneself" is the literal translation.

Fixing the mind on the lotus of the heart, or on the center of the head, is what is called <term>Dhāraṇā</term>. This involves remaining in one place, making one place the base where the waves of the mind rise up without being touched by other waves—when all other waves have stopped—and only one wave remains.

...rises in the mind, that is called *<term>Dhyāna</term>*, meditation. When no basis is necessary, when the whole of the mind has become one wave, “one-formedness,” it is called *<term>Samādhi</term>*. Stripped of all help from locations and centers, only the meaning of the thing is present. If the mind can be fixed on one center for twelve seconds it will be a *<term>Dhāraṇā</term>*, twelve such *<term>Dhāraṇās</term>* will be a *<term>Dhyāna</term>*, and twelve such *<term>Dhyānas</term>* will be a *<term>Samādhi</term>*. 

The next is *<term>Āsana</term>* (posture). The only thing to understand is to hold the body straight, leaving the body free, with the chest, shoulders, and head in a line. Where there is fire, or in water, or on ground which is strewn with dry leaves, or where there are wild animals, where four streets meet, or where there is too much noise, or too much fear, or too many anthills, or where there are many wicked persons—Yoga must not be practiced in such places. This applies more particularly to India. When the body feels very lazy do not practice, or when the mind is very miserable and sorrowful, or when the body is ill. Go <note>OCR reads "God"</note> to a place which is well hidden, and where people do not come to disturb you. As soon as you do not want people to know what you are doing, all the curiosity in the world will be awakened; but, if you go into the street and want people to know what you are doing, they will not care. Do not choose dirty places. Rather choose beautiful scenery, or a room in your own house which is beautiful. When you practice, first salute all the ancient *<term>Yogīs</term>*, and your own *<term>Gurū</term>*, and God, and then begin.

*<term>Dhyāna</term>* is spoken of, and a few examples are given of what to meditate upon. Sit straight, and look at the tip of your nose. Later on we will come to know how that concentrates the mind, how by controlling the two optic nerves one advances a long way towards the control of the arc of reaction, and so to the control of the will. # RAJA YOGA 76

...a few examples of meditation. Imagine a lotus upon the top of the head, several inches up, with virtue as its center and the stalk representing knowledge. The eight petals of the lotus are the eight powers of the *<term>Yogī</term>*. Inside, the stamens and pistils represent renunciation. If the *<term>Yogī</term>* refuses these external powers, he will reach salvation. So, the eight petals of the lotus are the eight powers, but the internal stamens and pistils are extreme renunciation—the renunciation of all of these. Inside of that lotus, think of the Golden One, the Almighty, the Intangible, He whose name is *<term>Om</term>*, the Inexpressible, surrounded with radiant light. Meditate on that. 

Another meditation is given. Think of a space in your heart <note>OCR reads "heard"</note>, and in the midst of that space, think that a flame is burning. Think of that flame as your own soul, and inside that flame is another space, radiant, and that is the Soul of your soul, God. Meditate upon that in the heart. Chastity, non-injury, pardoning everyone—even the greatest enemy—truth, and faith in the Lord; these are all different *<term>Vṛittis</term>*. Do not be afraid if you are not perfect in all of these; work, and the others will come. He who has given up all attachment, all fear, and all anger; he whose whole soul has gone to the Lord; he who has taken refuge in the Lord; whose heart has become purified—with whatever desire he comes to the Lord, He will grant it to him. Therefore, worship Him through knowledge, or worship Him through love, or worship Him through renunciation.

He who is the same in praise or in blame, with a silent, thoughtful mind <note>OCR reads ",ind"</note>, blessed with whatever little comes his way, homeless—he who has no home, for the whole world is his home—steady in his ideas, such a person becomes a *<term>Yogī</term>*.

There was a great god-sage called *<term>Nārada</term>*. Just as there are sages among mankind, great *<term>Yogīs</term>*, so there are great *<term>Yogīs</term>* among the gods. *<term>Nārada</term>* was a good *<term>Yogī</term>*, and very great. He traveled everywhere, and one day he was passing through a forest and saw a man who had been meditating until the white ants had built a huge mound around his body; he had been sitting in that position for so long. He said to *<term>Nārada</term>*, "Where are you going?" *<term>Nārada</term>* replied, "I am going to heaven." "Then ask God when He will be merciful to me; when I will attain freedom." Further on, *<term>Nārada</term>* saw another man. He was jumping about, singing, and dancing, and said, "Oh, *<term>Nārada</term>*, where are you going?" His voice and his gestures were wild. *<term>Nārada</term>* said, "I am going to heaven." "Then ask when I will be free." So *<term>Nārada</term>* went on. In the course of time, he came again by the same road, and there was the man who had been meditating until the ant-hills <note>OCR reads "anti-hills"</note> had grown around him. He said, "Oh, *<term>Nārada</term>*, did you ask the Lord about me?" "Oh, yes." "What did He say?" "The Lord told me that you would attain freedom in four more births." Then the man began to weep and wail, and said, "I have meditated until an ant-hill has been raised around me, and I have four more births yet!" *<term>Nārada</term>* went to the other man. "Did you ask my question?" "Oh, yes. Do you see this tamarind tree? I have to tell you that as many leaves as

### INTRODUCTION.

Before going into the *Yoga* Aphorisms, I will try to discuss one great question upon which the whole theory of religion rests for the *Yogis*. It seems to be the consensus of opinion of the great minds of the world, and it has been nearly demonstrated by researchers of physical nature, that we are the outcome and manifestation of an absolute condition behind our present relative condition, and that we are moving forward to return to that absolute again. This being granted, the question is: which is better—the absolute or this state? There is no lack of people who think that this manifested state is the highest state of man. Thinkers of great caliber are of the opinion that we are manifested specimens of undifferentiated being, and that this differentiated state is higher than the absolute. Because there can be no qualities in the absolute, they imagine that it must be insensate, dull, and lifeless; they believe that only this life can be enjoyed, and therefore we must cling to it.

First of all, we want to inquire into other solutions for the meaning of life. There was an old solution suggesting that after death, a person remained the same—that all their good qualities, without their evil ones, remained forever. Stated logically, this means that man’s goal is the world; this world, carried a stage higher and with its evils eliminated, is the state they call heaven. This theory, on the face of it, is absurd and childish, because it cannot be. There cannot be good without evil, or evil without good. To live in a world where everything is good and there is no evil is what Sanskrit logicians call a “dream in the air.” Another theory in modern times has been presented by several schools: that man’s destiny is to always keep improving, always struggling toward a goal, and never reaching it,

<summary>This introduction explores the philosophical debate between the "absolute" state and our current "manifested" existence. The author critiques the concept of a heaven devoid of evil, arguing that good and evil are logically inseparable in the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Aphorisms, Yogis, the absolute, manifestation, heaven, good and evil, Sanskrit logicians, undifferentiated being</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of existence, arguing against the theory of infinite linear progression and instead proposing that all life and force move in a circular return to their source.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>RAJA YOGA</header>

the goal. This statement, though seemingly very pleasant, is also absurd because there is no such thing as motion in a straight line. Every motion is circular. If you could pick up a stone and throw it into space, and then live long enough, that stone would return exactly to your hand. A straight line, projected infinitely, must end in a circle. Therefore, this idea that the destiny of humanity is a progression ever forward and forward, never stopping, is absurd.

Although it is outside the main subject, I might remark that this idea explains the ethical theory of why you must not hate and why you must love. Just as in the case of electricity or any other force, the modern theory is that the power leaves the dynamo and completes the circle back to the dynamo. The same applies to all forces in nature; they must return to their source. Therefore, do not hate anyone, because that force of hatred which leaves you must, in the long run, return to you. If you love, that love will return to you, completing the circuit. It is as certain as can be that every bit of hatred that leaves the heart of a person returns to them with full force; nothing can stop it, and every impulse of love returns to them.

On other practical grounds, we see that the theory of eternal progression is indefensible, for destruction is the goal of everything earthly. All our struggles, hopes, fears, and joys—what will they lead to? We will all end in death. Nothing is so certain as this. Where, then, is this motion in a straight line? This infinite progression? It is only moving outward to a distance and again returning to the center from which it started. See how the sun, moon, and stars are produced from nebulae; then they dissolve and return to nebulae. The same is happening everywhere. The plant takes material from the earth, dissolves, and gives it back. Every form in this world is

<summary>The author argues that all motion in the universe is circular rather than linear, meaning that every force—including human emotions like love and hate—must eventually return to its source. This concept refutes the idea of eternal linear progression, noting that all physical forms eventually dissolve and return to their original state.</summary>

<keywords>circular motion, karma, ethics, eternal progression, nebulae, return to source, reincarnation, conservation of force</keywords>

taken out of corresponding atoms and goes back to those atoms.

It cannot be that the same law acts differently in different places. Law is uniform. Nothing is more certain than that. If this is the law of nature, so it is with thought; it will dissolve and come back to its origin; whether we want it or not, we shall have to return to the origin, which is called God or the Absolute. We all came from God, and we are all bound to go to God, call that God by any name you like; call Him God, or the Absolute, or Nature, or by any hundred names you like, the fact remains the same. "From whom all this universe comes out, in whom all that is born lives, and to whom all returns." This is one fact that is certain. Nature works on the same plan; what is being worked out in one sphere is being worked out in millions of spheres. What you see with the planets will be the same with this earth, with men, and with the stars. The huge wave is a mighty compound of small waves, perhaps millions of them; the life of the whole world is a compound of millions of little lives, and the death of the whole world is the compound of the deaths of those millions of little beings.

Now the question arises: is going back to God the higher state, or is it not? The philosophers of the <term>Yoga</term> school answer emphatically that it is. They say that man’s present state is a degeneration; there is no one religion on the face of the earth which says that man is an improvement. The idea <note>The word "idea" is repeated in the original printed text.</note> is that his beginning is perfect and pure, that he degenerates until he cannot degenerate further, and that there must come a time when he shoots upward again to complete the circle; the circle must be there. However low he goes, he must ultimately take the upward bend again and go back to the original source, which is God. Man comes from God in the beginning; in the middle he becomes man,
