and in the end he goes back to God. This is the method of putting it in the dualistic form. In the monistic form, you say that man is God and goes back to Him again. If our present state is the higher one, then why is there so much horror and misery, and why is there an end to it? If this is the higher state, why does it end? That which corrupts and degenerates cannot be the highest state. Why should it be so diabolical, so unsatisfying? It is only excusable because, through it, we are taking a higher path; we have to pass through it in order to become regenerated again. Put a seed into the ground and it disintegrates, dissolves after a time, and out of that dissolution comes the splendid tree. Every seed must degenerate to become the stately tree. So it follows that the sooner we get out of this state we call "man," the better for us. Is it by committing suicide that we get out of this state? Not at all. That would make it all the worse. Torturing ourselves, or condemning the world, is not the way to get out. We have to pass through the "Slough of Despond" <note>A state of extreme despair or a deep bog of spiritual discouragement, famously referenced in John Bunyan’s "The Pilgrim's Progress."</note>, and the sooner we are through, the better. But it must always be remembered that this is not the highest state.

The really difficult part to understand is that this state, the Absolute, which has been called the highest, is not, as some fear, that of the zoophyte <note>A plant-like animal, such as a coral or sea anemone, once thought to be a link between plants and animals.</note>, or of the stone. That would be a dangerous thing to think. According to these thinkers, there are only two states of existence: one of the stone and the other of thought. What right have they to limit existence to these two? Is there not something infinitely superior to thought? The vibrations of light, when they are very low, we do not see; when they become a little more intense, they become light to us; when they become still more intense, we do not see them; it is dark to us. Is the darkness in the end the same as in the beginning? Certainly not; it is the difference between the two poles. Is the thoughtlessness of the

<meta>This page continues the argument that the state beyond thought is not a regression to an inanimate state like a stone, but an advancement toward a higher, divine existence.</meta>

<header>YOGA APHORISMS: INTRODUCTION</header>

Is the state of a stone the same as the thoughtlessness of God? Certainly not. God does not think; He does not reason; why should He? Is anything unknown to Him, that He should need to reason? The stone cannot reason; God does not. Such is the difference. These philosophers think it is awful if we go beyond thought; they find nothing beyond thought.

There are much higher states of existence beyond reasoning. It is actually beyond the intellect that the first stage of religious life is to be found. When you step beyond thought and intellect and all reasoning, then you have made the first step towards God; and that is the beginning of life. This that is commonly called life is but an embryo state.

The next question will be, what proof is there that this state beyond thought and reasoning is the highest state? In the first place, all the great men of the world—men much greater than those who only talk, men who moved the world, men who never thought of any selfish ends whatever—have declared that this life is but a small stage on the way, and that the Infinite lies beyond. In the second place, they not only say so, but they lay it open to everyone; they leave their methods behind, and all can follow in their steps. In the third place, there is no other way left. There is no other explanation. Taking for granted that there is no higher state, why are we going through this circle all the time? What reason can explain the world? The sensory world will be the limit of our knowledge if we cannot go further, and if we must not ask for anything more. This is what is called agnosticism. But what reason is there to believe only in the testimony of the senses? I would call that man a true agnostic who would stand still in the street and die. If reason is everything, it leaves us no place to stand on this side of nihilism. If a man is agnostic about everything except money, fame, and reputation, he is only a fraud. Kant has proved beyond all doubt that we cannot penetrate beyond the tremendous dead

# CHAPTER I.
## CONCENTRATION: ITS SPIRITUAL USES

### 1. Now concentration is explained.
### 2. Yoga is restraining the mind-stuff (*Chitta*) from taking various forms (*Vṛttis*)

A good deal of explanation is necessary here. We have to understand what *Chitta* is, and what these *Vṛttis* are. I have this eye. Eyes do not see. Take away the brain center which is in the head, and the eyes will still be there, the retinas will be complete, and the picture will be there as well, yet the eyes will not see. So the eyes are only a secondary instrument, not the organ of vision. The organ of vision is in the nerve center of the brain. The two eyes alone will not be sufficient. Sometimes a person is asleep with their eyes open. The light is there and the picture is there, but a third thing is necessary; the mind must be joined to the organ. Carriages roll down a street and you do not hear them. Why? Because your mind has not attached itself to the organ of hearing. First there is the instrument, then there is the organ, and third, the mind's attachment to these two. The mind takes the impression farther in and presents it to the determinative faculty—*Buddhi*—which reacts. Along with this reaction flashes the idea of egoism. Then this mixture of action and reaction is presented to the *Puruṣa*, the real Soul, who perceives an object in this mixture. The organs (*Indriyas*), together with the mind (*Manas*), the determinative faculty (*Buddhi*) and egoism (*Ahamkāra*), form the group called the *Antahkaraṇa* (the internal

<summary>This page defines Yoga as the restraint of the mind-stuff (Chitta) from taking various forms (Vṛttis) and explains the physiological and psychological process of perception. It details how the eyes, brain centers, and the mind must work together with the soul (Puruṣa) to perceive an object.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga, Chitta, Vṛttis, perception, Buddhi, Puruṣa, Indriyas, Manas, Ahamkāra, Antahkaraṇa, mind-stuff, egoism</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the internal instrument (Antahkaraṇa) and defines the relationship between the mind, external stimuli, and the soul.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

->RAJA YOGA<-

instrument). They are only various processes in the mind-stuff called *Chitta*. The waves of thought in the *Chitta* are called *Vṛtti* (literally translated as "the whirlpool"). What is thought? Thought is a force, just like gravity or repulsion. It is absorbed from nature's infinite storehouse of force; the instrument called *Chitta* takes hold of that force, and when it is expressed at the other end, it is called thought. This force is supplied to us through food, and from that food the body obtains the power of movement, etc. The body releases other, finer forces in what we call thought. Naturally, we see that the mind is not intelligent by itself; yet it appears to be intelligent. Why? Because the intelligent soul is behind it. You are the only sentient being; the mind is only the instrument through which you perceive the external world. 

Take this book; as a "book," it does not exist outside of us. What exists externally is unknown and unknowable. It is the external suggestion that strikes the mind, and the mind produces a reaction. If a stone is thrown into water, the water reacts against it in the form of waves. The real universe provides the occasion for the mind's reaction. The form of a book, an elephant, or a person is not outside us; all we know is our mental reaction to the external suggestion. Matter is the "permanent possibility of sensation," as <term>John Stuart Mill</term> said. Only the suggestion exists outside. 

Take an oyster, for example. You know how pearls are made. A grain of sand or some other object gets inside and begins to irritate it; the oyster then coats the sand with a type of enamel, and this creates the pearl. This whole universe is our own "enamel," so to speak, and the real universe is the grain of sand. The average person will never understand this because, when he tries to, he projects his own enamel and sees only that. Now we understand what is meant by these *Vṛttis*. The real person exists behind the mind, and the mind is the instrument in his hands,

<summary>This page explains that thought is a force processed by the Chitta (mind-stuff) and that our perception of the world is actually a mental reaction to external stimuli. Using the analogy of an oyster creating a pearl, it describes how the mind projects forms onto an unknown external reality.</summary>

<keywords>Chitta, Vṛtti, perception, thought-force, John Stuart Mill, soul, mental reaction, nature of matter</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Chitta (mind-stuff) and Vṛttis (mental waves), transitioning from the definition of thought-force to the analogy of a lake and the three qualities of the mind.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>YOGA APHORISMS: CONCENTRATION I</header>

And it is his intelligence that is percolating through it. It is only when you stand behind it that it becomes intelligent. When a person gives it up, it falls to pieces and is nothing. So you understand what is meant by *Chitta*. It is the mind-stuff, and *Vṛttis* are the waves and ripples rising in it when external causes impinge on it. These *Vṛttis* are our whole universe.

We cannot see the bottom of the lake because its surface is covered with ripples. It is only possible for us to catch a glimpse of the bottom when the ripples have subsided and the water is calm. If the water is muddy, the bottom will not be seen; if the water is constantly agitated, the bottom will not be seen. If the water is clear and there are no waves, we shall see the bottom. That bottom of the lake is our own true Self; the lake is the *Chitta*, and the waves are the *Vṛttis*. 

Again, this mind exists in three states. One is darkness, which is called *Tamas*, just as in animals and the ignorant; it only acts to injure others. No other idea enters that state of mind. Then there is the active state of mind, *Rajas*, whose chief motives are power and enjoyment: “I will be powerful and rule others.” Then, at last, when the waves cease and the water of the lake becomes clear, there is the state called *Sattva*: serenity and calmness. It is not inactive, but rather intensely active. To be calm is the greatest manifestation of power. It is easy to be active. Let go of the reins, and the horses will drag you down. Anyone can do that, but he who can stop the plunging horses is the strong man. Which requires the greater strength: letting go or restraining? The calm man is not a man who is dull. You must not mistake *Sattva* for dullness or laziness. The calm man is the one who has restraint over these waves. Activity is the manifestation of the lower strength; calmness is the manifestation of the superior strength.

<vocab>Chitta, Vṛttis, Tamas, Rajas, Sattva, Yoga Aphorisms, mind-stuff, true Self</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the concept of Chitta as "mind-stuff" using the analogy of a lake, where ripples (Vṛttis) obscure our true Self. It defines the three states of mind: Tamas (darkness/ignorance), Rajas (activity/power), and Sattva (serenity/restraint).</summary>
<keywords>Chitta, Vṛttis, Tamas, Rajas, Sattva, true Self, Raja Yoga, mental states, self-restraint</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the *Chitta* (mind-stuff) and the process of calming the mental waves (*Vṛttis*) to reveal the true Self, comparing the mind to a lake that must be still to see the bottom.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>RAJA YOGA</header>

This *Chitta* is always trying to return to its natural pure state, but the organs draw it out. To restrain it, to check this outward tendency, and to start it on the return journey to that essence of intelligence is the first step in *Yoga*, because only in this way can the *Chitta* get onto its proper course.

Although this *Chitta* exists in every animal, from the lowest to the highest, it is only in the human form that we find intellect. Until the mind-stuff can take the form of intellect, it is not possible for it to return through all these steps and liberate the soul. Immediate salvation is impossible for the cow and the dog, even though they have minds, because their *Chitta* cannot yet take that form which we call intellect.

*Chitta* manifests itself in all these different forms—scattering, darkening, weakening, and concentrating. These are the four states in which the mind-stuff manifests itself. First, a scattered form is activity. Its tendency is to manifest in the form of pleasure or pain. Then, the dull form is darkness, the only tendency of which is to injure others. The commentator says the first form is natural to the *Devas* (the angels), and the second is the demonic form. The *Ekāgra*, the concentrated form of the *Chitta*, is what brings us to *Samadhi*.

->3. At that time (the time of concentration) the seer (the *Puraṣa*) rests in his own (unmodified) state.<-

As soon as the waves have stopped and the lake has become quiet, we see the ground below the water. It is the same with the mind; when it is calm, we see what our own nature is; we do not confuse ourselves with the mind but remain our own true selves.

<summary>This page explains that Yoga involves redirecting the Chitta inward to its pure state, a process requiring human intellect. ### 4. At other times (other than that of concentration), the seer is identified with the modifications.

For instance, I am in a state of sorrow; someone blames me; this is a modification, a *Vrtti*, and I identify myself with it, and the result is misery.

### 5. There are five classes of modification, painful and not painful.
### 6. (These are) right knowledge, indiscrimination, verbal delusion, sleep, and memory.
### 7. Direct perception, inference, and competent evidence are proofs.

When two of our perceptions do not contradict each other, we call it proof. If I hear something that contradicts something already perceived, I begin to fight it out and do not believe it. There are also three kinds of proof. Direct perception, *Pratyaksham*—whatever we see and feel—is proof, provided there has been nothing to delude the senses. I see the world; that is sufficient proof that it exists. Secondly, *Anumāna*, or inference; you see a sign, and from that sign, you come to the thing signified. Thirdly, *Āptavākyam*, the direct perception of the *Yogi*, of those who have seen the truth. We are all of us struggling toward knowledge, but you and I have to struggle hard and reach knowledge through a long, tedious process of reasoning. However, the *Yogi*, the pure one, has gone beyond all this. Before his mind, the past, the present, and the future are alike—one book for him to read. He does not require to go through all this tedious process, and his words are proofs because he sees knowledge in himself; he is the Omniscient One. These, for instance, are the authors

<summary>This page explains how the soul identifies with mental modifications (Vrttis) outside of concentration and defines the five types of modifications, specifically detailing the three kinds of valid proof: direct perception, inference, and the testimony of a Yogi.</summary>
<keywords>Patanjali, Yoga Aphorisms, Vrtti, Pratyaksham, Anumāna, Āptavākyam, Yogi, direct perception, inference, mental modifications</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of competent evidence as proof, specifically defining the *Āpta*—a reliable authority who has direct perception of spiritual truth.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
->RAJA YOGA<-

of the Sacred Scriptures; therefore, the Scriptures are proof, and if any such persons are living now, their words will be proof. Other philosophers go into long discussions about this <term>*Āpta*</term>, and they ask: "What is the proof that this is truth?" The proof is that they see it; because whatever I see is proof, and whatever you see is proof, provided it does not contradict any past knowledge. There is knowledge beyond the senses, and whenever it does not contradict reason and past human experience, that knowledge is proof. Any madman may come into this room and say that he sees angels around him, but that would not be proof. In the first place, it must be true knowledge; secondly, it must not contradict knowledge of the past; and thirdly, it must depend upon the character of the man. I hear it said that the character of the man is not as important as what he may say—that we must first hear what he says. This may be true in other matters; a man may be wicked and yet make an astronomical discovery, but in religion it is different, because no impure person will ever have the power to reach the truths of religion. 

Therefore, we have first of all to see that the man who declares himself to be an <term>*Āpta*</term> is a perfectly unselfish and holy person; secondly, that he has reached beyond the senses; and thirdly, that what he says does not contradict the past knowledge of humanity. Any new discovery of truth does not contradict past truth but fits into it. And fourthly, that truth must have a possibility of verification. If a man says, "I have seen a vision," and tells me that I have no right to see it, I do not believe him. Everyone must have the power to see it for himself. No one who sells his knowledge is an <term>*Āpta*</term>. All these conditions must be fulfilled: you must first see that the man is pure and that he has no selfish motive—that he has no thirst for gain or fame. Secondly, he must show that he is super-conscious. Thirdly, he must give us something that

...we cannot get from our senses, and which is for the benefit of the world. And we must see that it does not contradict other truths; if it contradicts other scientific truths, reject it at once. Fourthly, the person should never be unique; he should only represent what all people can attain. The three types of proof are, then: direct sense perception, inference, and the words of an <term>*Āpta*</term>. I cannot translate this word into English. It is not the word "inspired," because that implies something coming from the outside, while this comes from within himself. The literal meaning is "attained."

### 8. Indiscrimination is false knowledge not established in real nature.

The next class of <term>*Vṛttis*</term> that arises is mistaking one thing for another, such as when a piece of mother-of-pearl is mistaken for a piece of silver.

### 9. Verbal delusion follows from words having no (corresponding) reality.

There is another class of <term>*Vṛttis*</term> called <term>*Vikalpa*</term>. A word is spoken, and we do not wait to consider its meaning; we jump to a conclusion immediately. It is a sign of weakness of the <term>*Chitta*</term>. Now you can understand the theory of restraint. The weaker the person, the less restraint they have. Always consider yourselves in that way. When you are about to be angry or miserable, reason it out: how is it that some news that has come to you is throwing your mind into <term>*Vṛttis*</term>?

### 10. Sleep is a <term>*Vṛtti*</term> which embraces the feeling of voidness.

The next class of <term>*Vṛttis*</term> is called sleep and dream. When we awake, we know that we have been sleeping; we can only have a memory of perception. That which we do not perceive...

...we can never have any memory of. Every reaction is a wave in the lake. Now, if the mind had no waves during sleep, it would have no perceptions, positive or negative, and therefore we would not remember them. The very reason we remember sleep is that there was a certain class of waves in the mind during sleep. Memory is another class of <term>*Vṛttis*</term>, which is called <term>*Smṛti*</term>.

### 11. Memory is when the (<term>*Vṛttis*</term> of) perceived subjects do not slip away (and return to consciousness through impressions).

Memory can be caused by the previous three types of modifications. For instance, you hear a word. That word is like a stone thrown into the lake of the <term>*Chitta*</term>; it causes a ripple, and that ripple rouses a series of ripples; this is memory. It is the same in sleep. When the peculiar kind of ripple called sleep throws the <term>*Chitta*</term> into a ripple of memory, it is called a dream. A dream is another form of the ripple which is called memory in the waking state.

### 12. Their control is through practice and non-attachment.

To have this non-attachment, the mind must be clear, good, and rational. Why should we practice? Because each action is like the pulsations quivering over the surface of the lake. The vibration dies out, and what is left? The <term>*Saṃskāras*</term>, the impressions. When a large number of these impressions are left on the mind, they coalesce and become a habit. It is said, "Habit is second nature"; it is first nature also, and the whole nature of man; everything that we are is the result of habit. That gives us consolation because, if it is only habit, we can make and unmake it at any time. The <term>*Saṃskāra*</term> is left by

...these vibrations passing out of our mind, each one of them leaving its result. Our character is the sum total of these marks, and depending on which particular wave prevails, one adopts that tone. If good prevails, one becomes good; if wickedness prevails, one becomes wicked; if joyfulness prevails, one becomes happy. The only remedy for bad habits is counter-habits; all the bad habits that have left their impressions are to be controlled by good habits. Continue doing good and thinking holy thoughts continuously; that is the only way to suppress base impressions. Never say any man is hopeless, because he only represents a character—a bundle of habits—and these can be countered by new and better ones. Character is repeated habits, and repeated habits alone can reform character.

->13. Continuous struggle to keep them (the<-
-><term>*Vṛttis*</term>) perfectly restrained is practice.<-

What is this practice? It is the attempt to restrain the mind in the <term>*Chitta*</term> form, to prevent it from turning into waves.

->14. Its ground becomes firm by long, constant<-
->efforts with great love (for the end to be<-
->attained).<-

Restraint does not come in one day, but by long-continued practice.

power of battling against these, and holding the mind in check. Renunciation of these two motives is what we want. I am passing through a street, and a man comes and takes my watch. That is my own experience. I see it myself, and it immediately throws my *Chitta* into a wave, taking the form of anger. Do not allow that to come. If you cannot prevent that, you are nothing; if you can, you have *Vairāgyam*. Similarly, the experience of the worldly-minded teaches us that sensory enjoyments are the highest ideal. These are tremendous temptations. To deny them, and not allow the mind to come into a wave form with regard to them, is renunciation; to control the twofold motive powers arising from my own experience and from the experience of others, and thus prevent the *Chitta* from being governed by them, is *Vairāgyam*. These should be controlled by me, and not I by them. This sort of mental strength is called renunciation. This *Vairāgyam* is the only way to freedom.

->16. That extreme non-attachment, giving up<-
->even the qualities, shows (the real nature<-
->of) the *Puruṣa*.<-

It is the highest manifestation of power when it takes away even our attraction toward the qualities. We first have to understand what the *Puruṣa*, the Self, is, and what the qualities are. According to *Yoga* philosophy, the whole of nature consists of three qualities; one is called *Tamas*, another *Rajas*, and the third *Sattva*. These three qualities manifest themselves in the physical world as attraction, repulsion, and control. Everything that is in nature, all these manifestations, are combinations and recombinations of these three forces. This nature has been divided into various categories by the *Sānkhyas*; the Self of man is beyond all

this, beyond nature, is effulgent by its very nature. It is pure and perfect. Whatever intelligence we see in nature is but the reflection of this Self upon nature. Nature itself is insentient. You must remember that the word "nature" also includes the mind; the mind is in nature; thought is in nature; from thought, down to the most solid form of matter, everything is in nature—it is all the manifestation of nature. This nature has covered the Self of the human being, and when nature removes the covering, the Self becomes unveiled and appears in its own glory. This non-attachment, as it is described in Aphorism 15 (as being the control of nature), is the greatest help toward manifesting the Self. The next aphorism defines *Samādhi*, perfect concentration, which is the goal of the *Yogī*.

->17. The concentration called right know-<-
->ledge is that which is followed by reasoning,<-
->discrimination, bliss, and unqualified ego.<-

This *Samādhi* is divided into two varieties. One is called *Samprajñāta*, and the other *Asamprajñāta*. The *Samprajñāta* is of four varieties. In this *Samādhi* come all the powers of controlling nature. The first variety is called *Savitarka*, when the mind meditates upon an object repeatedly, isolating it from other objects. There are two sorts of objects for meditation: the categories of nature and the *Puruṣa*. Again, the categories are of two varieties; the twenty-four categories are insentient, and the one sentient category is the *Puruṣa*. When the mind thinks of the elements of nature by considering their beginning and their end, this is one sort of *Savitarka*. These words require explanation. This part of Yoga is based entirely on *Sānkhya* Philosophy, which I have already discussed with you. As you will remember, egoism and

This is called *Avyaktam*, the unmanifested state of nature before creation, and to which, after the end of a cycle, the whole of nature returns, only to emerge again after another period. Beyond that is the *Puruṣa*, the essence of intelligence. There is no liberation in obtaining powers. It is a worldly search for enjoyment in this life; all search for enjoyment is vain; this is the old, old lesson which humanity finds it so hard to learn. When one does learn it, they leave the universe and become free. The possession of what are called occult powers only intensifies the world and, in the end, intensifies suffering. Though, as a scientist, *Patanjali* is bound to point out the possibilities of this science, he never misses an opportunity to warn us against these powers. Knowledge is power, and as soon as we begin to know a thing we gain power over it; likewise, when the mind begins to meditate on the different elements, it gains power over them. That sort of meditation where the external, gross elements are the objects is called *Savitarka*. *Tarka* means question; *Savitarka* means "with questioning." It involves questioning the elements, as it were, so that they may give up their truths and their powers to the person who meditates upon them. Again, in the very same meditation, when one struggles to take the elements out of time and space and think of them as they are, it is called *Nirvitarka*, "without questioning." When the meditation goes a step higher and takes the *Tanmātras* <note>the subtle elements or fine particles</note> as its object, and thinks of them as within time and space, it is called *Savichāra*, "with discrimination," and when the same meditation goes beyond time and space and thinks of the fine elements as they are, it

...the object, and whenever a thought comes, to strike it down, allowing no thought to come into the mind, thus making it an entire vacuum. When we can really do this, in that moment we shall attain liberation. When persons without training and preparation try to make their minds vacant, they are likely to succeed only in covering themselves with *Tamas*, the material of ignorance, which makes the mind dull and stupid and leads them to think that they are making a vacuum of the mind. To be able to really do that is a manifestation of the greatest strength, of the highest control. When this state, *Asamprajnāta* (super-consciousness), is reached, the *Samādhi* becomes seedless. What is meant by that? In that sort of concentration where there is consciousness, where the mind has succeeded only in quelling the waves in the *Chitta* and holding them down, they are still there in the form of tendencies, and these tendencies (or seeds) will become waves again when the time comes. But when you have destroyed all these tendencies, almost destroyed the mind, then it has become seedless; there are no more seeds in the mind out of which to manufacture this plant of life again and again, this ceaseless round of birth and death. You may ask, what state would that be in which we should have no knowledge? What we call knowledge is a lower state than the one beyond knowledge. You must always bear in mind that the extremes look very much the same. The low vibration of light is darkness, and the very high vibration of light is darkness also, but one is real darkness and the other is really intense light; yet their appearance is the same. So, ignorance is the lowest state, knowledge is the middle state, and beyond knowledge is a still higher state. Knowledge itself is a manufactured something, a combination; it is not reality. What will be the result of constant practice of this higher concentration? All old tendencies of restlessness, and

dullness, will be destroyed, as well as the tendencies of goodness too. It is just the same as with the metals that are used with gold to take off the dirt and alloy. When the ore is smelted down, the dross is burnt along with the alloy. So this constant controlling power will stop the previous bad tendencies, and, eventually, the good ones also. Those good and evil tendencies will suppress each other, and there will remain the Soul, in all its glorious splendor, untrammeled by either good or bad, and that Soul is omnipresent, omnipotent, and omniscient. By giving up all powers it has become omnipotent; by giving up all life it is beyond mortality—it has become life itself. Then the Soul will know It neither had birth nor death, neither need of heaven nor of earth. It will know that It neither came nor went; it was nature which was moving, and that movement was reflected upon the Soul. The form of the light is moving; it is reflected and cast by the camera upon the wall, and the wall foolishly thinks it is moving. So with all of us: it is the *Chitta* constantly moving, manipulating itself into various forms, and we think that we are these various forms. All these delusions will vanish. When that free Soul will command—not pray or beg, but command—then whatever It desires will be immediately fulfilled; whatever It wants It will be able to do.

According to the *Sānkhya* Philosophy there is no God. It says that there cannot be any God of this universe, because if there were He must be a Soul, and a Soul must be one of two things: either bound or free. How can the soul that is bound by nature, or controlled by nature, create? It is itself a slave. On the other hand, what business has the soul that is free to create and manipulate all these things? It has no desires, so it cannot have any need to create. Secondly, it says the theory of God is an unnecessary one; nature explains all. What is the use of any God? But *Kapila*

teaches that there are many souls who, through nearly attaining perfection, fall short because they cannot perfectly renounce all powers. Their minds merge into nature for a time, only to re-emerge as its masters. We shall all become such gods, and according to the *Sānkhyas*, the "God" spoken of in the *Vedas* actually refers to one of these free souls. Beyond them, there is no eternally free and blessed Creator of the universe. On the other hand, the *Yogīs* say, "Not so; there is a God. There is one Soul separate from all other souls, and He is the eternal Master of all creation, the Ever Free, the Teacher of all teachers." The *Yogīs* admit that those the *Sānkhyas* called "merged in nature" also exist. They are *Yogīs* who have fallen short of perfection, and although they are temporarily barred from attaining the ultimate goal, they remain as rulers of parts of the universe.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;22. They again differ according to whether the means are mild, medium, or supreme.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;23. Or by devotion to *Iśvara*.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;24. *Iśvara* (the Supreme Ruler) is a special *Puruṣa*, untouched by misery, the results of actions, or desires.

We must again remember that this *Patanjali Yoga Philosophy* is based upon that of the *Sāṅkhyas*, except that in the latter there is no place for God, while with the *Yogīs* God has a place. The *Yogīs*, however, avoid many ideas about God, such as the act of creation. God as the Creator of the Universe is not what is meant by the *Iśvara* of the *Yogīs*, although, according to the *Vedas*, *Iśvara* is the Creator of the universe. Seeing that the universe is harmonious, it must be the manifestation of one will. The *Yogīs* and *Sāṅkhyas* both avoid the question of creation. The *Yogīs* want to establish a God, but they carefully avoid this question; they do not raise it at all. Yet you will find that they arrive at God in a peculiar fashion of their own. They say:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;25. In Him, that all-knowingness which in others is only a seed becomes infinite.

The mind must always travel between two extremes. You can think of limited space, but the very idea of that also gives you the concept of unlimited space. Close your eyes and think of a little space, and at the same time that you perceive the little circle, you have a circle around it of unlimited dimensions. It is the same with time. Try to think of a second; with the same act of perception, you will have to think of time which is unlimited. It is the same with knowledge. Knowledge is only a seed in man, but you will have to think of infinite knowledge.

It also presents a logical argument for God's omniscience based on the mind's ability to conceive of the infinite through the limited.</summary>

around it, so that the very nature of your makeup shows us that there is unlimited knowledge, and the *Yogīs* call that unlimited knowledge God.

## 26. He is the Teacher of even the ancient teachers, for He is not limited by time.

It is true that all knowledge is within ourselves, but this must be called forth by another knowledge. Although the capacity to know is inside us, it must be drawn out, and that drawing out of knowledge can only be obtained, a *Yogī* maintains, through another knowledge. Dead, unconscious matter never draws out knowledge. It is the action of knowledge that brings out knowledge. Knowing beings must be with us to call forth what is in us, and so these teachers were always necessary. The world was never without them, and no knowledge can come without them. God is the Teacher of all teachers because these teachers, however great they may have been—whether gods or angels—were all bound and limited by time, whereas God is not limited by time. These are the two unique distinctions made by the *Yogīs*. The first is that in thinking of the limited, the mind must also think of the unlimited, and if one part of the perception is true, the other must be as well, because their value as perceptions of the mind is equal. The very fact that man has a little knowledge shows that God has unlimited knowledge. If I am to accept one, why not the other? Reason forces me to accept both or reject both. If I believe that there is a man with a little knowledge, I must also admit that there is someone behind him with unlimited knowledge. The second deduction is that no knowledge can come without a teacher. It is true, as modern philosophers say, that there is something in man which evolves out of him; all knowledge is in man, but

<summary>This page explains that because knowledge must be called forth by a knowing being and because human teachers are limited by time, God exists as the eternal Teacher and the source of infinite knowledge. It argues that the existence of limited knowledge logically necessitates the existence of its unlimited counterpart.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Yogīs, God, Teacher, infinite knowledge, perception, logic, time, evolution of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument for the existence of God (*Iśvara*) as the eternal Teacher, established by the necessity of a non-limited source for all knowledge.</meta>

<header>YOGA APHORISMS: CONCENTRATION I</header>
<page-num>105</page-num>

---

Certain environments are necessary to call it out. We cannot find any knowledge without a teacher; whether there are human teachers, divine teachers, or angelic teachers, they are all limited. Who was the teacher before them? We are forced to admit, as a final conclusion, one Teacher who is not limited by time; and that one Teacher of infinite knowledge, without beginning or end, is called God.

### 27. His manifesting word is *Om.*

Every idea that you have in the mind has a counterpart in a word; the word and the thought are inseparable. The external part of the thought is what we call the word, and the internal part is what we call thought. No one can, through analysis, separate thought from the word. The idea that language was created by humans—specific people sitting together and deciding on words—has been proved wrong. For as long as things have existed, there have been words and language. What is the connection between an idea and a word? Although we see that there must always be a word associated with a thought, it is not necessary for the same thought to require the same word. The thought may be the same in twenty different countries, yet the language is different. We must have a word to express each thought, but these words do not necessarily need to have the same sound. Sounds will vary among different nations. Our commentator says, "Although the relation between thought and word is perfectly natural, it does not mean a rigid connection between a specific sound and a specific idea." These sounds vary, yet the relationship between the sounds and the thoughts is natural. The connection between thoughts and sounds is valid only if there is a real connection between the thing signified and the symbol; until then, that symbol will never come into general use. A symbol manifests the thing signified, and if the

The thing signified already exists, and if, through experience, we know that the symbol has expressed that thing many times, then we are sure that there is a real relation between them. Even if the things are not present, there will be thousands who will know them by their symbols. There must be a natural connection between the symbol and the thing signified; then, when that symbol is pronounced, it recalls the thing signified. The commentator says the manifesting word of God is *Om*. Why does he emphasize this? There are hundreds of words for God. One thought is connected with a thousand words; the idea of God is connected with hundreds of words, and each one stands as a symbol for God. Very well. But there must be a generalization among all these words, some substratum, some common ground for all these symbols; and that symbol which is the common symbol will be the best, and will truly be the symbol of all. In making a sound we use the larynx and the palate as a sounding board. Is there any material sound of which all other sounds must be manifestations—one which is the most natural sound? *Om (Aum)* is such a sound, the basis of all sounds. The first letter, A, is the root sound, the key, pronounced without touching any part of the tongue or palate; M represents the last sound in the series, being produced by the closed lips; and the U rolls from the very root to the end of the sounding board of the mouth. Thus, *Om* represents the whole phenomenon of sound production. As such, it must be the natural symbol, the matrix of all the varying sounds. It denotes the whole range and possibility of all the words that can be made. Apart from these speculations, we see that all the different religious ideas in India are centered around this word *Om*; all the various religious ideas of the *Vedas* have gathered themselves around this word *Om*. What has that to do with America and England, or any

Monists, Dualists, Mono-Dualists, Separatists, and even Atheists adopted this *Om*. *Om* has become the singular symbol for the religious aspirations of the vast majority of human beings. Take, for instance, the English word "God." It conveys only a limited function, and if you go beyond that, you have to add adjectives to make it a Personal, Impersonal, or Absolute God. It is the same with the words for God in every other language; their meaning is very limited. This word *Om*, however, encompasses all these various meanings. As such, it should be accepted by everyone.

->28. The repetition of this (Om) and meditating on its meaning (is the way).<-

Why should there be repetition? We have not forgotten the theory of *Samskāras*—that the sum total of impressions lives in the mind. Impressions live in the mind as a sum total; they become increasingly latent but remain there, and as soon as they receive the right stimulus, they emerge. Molecular vibration will never cease. When this universe is destroyed, all the massive vibrations disappear—the sun, moon, stars, and earth will melt away—but the vibrations must remain in the atoms. Each atom will perform the same function as the great worlds do. Similarly, the vibrations of this *Chitta* will subside but will continue like molecular vibrations, and when they receive an impulse, they will emerge again. We can now understand what is meant by repetition. It is the greatest stimulus that can be given to the spiritual *Samskāras*. "One moment of company with the holy acts as a ship to cross this ocean of life." Such is the

->**31. Grief, mental distress, trembling of the body, and irregular breathing accompany the failure to maintain concentration.**<-

Repetition of *Om* and self-surrender to the Lord will strengthen the mind and bring fresh energy. These nervous tremors will happen to almost everyone. Do not mind them at all, but keep on practicing. Practice will cure them and make the seat <note>The physical posture or 'asana' for meditation.</note> firm.

->**32. To remedy this, the practice of focusing on a single subject should be undertaken.**<-

Training the mind to take the form of one object for some time will destroy these obstacles. This is general advice. In the following aphorisms, it will be expanded upon and specified. Since one practice cannot suit everyone, various methods will be proposed, and everyone will find through actual experience what helps them most.

### 33. Friendship, mercy, gladness, and indifference, when applied respectively toward those who are happy, unhappy, good, and evil, pacify the *Chitta*.

We must maintain these four types of ideas. We must have friendship for all; we must be merciful toward those who are in misery; when people are happy, we ought to be happy; and to the wicked, we must be indifferent. This applies to all subjects that come before us. If the subject is a good one, we shall feel friendly toward it; if the subject of thought is one that is miserable, we must be merciful toward that subject. If it is good, we must be glad; if it is evil, we must be indifferent. These attitudes of the mind toward the different subjects that come before it will make the mind peaceful. Most of our difficulties in our daily lives come from being unable to hold our minds in this way. For instance, if a man does evil to us, instantly we want to react with evil, and every reaction of evil shows that we are not able to hold the *Chitta* <note>The mind-stuff or mental substance.</note> down; it comes out in waves toward the object, and we lose our power. Every reaction in the form of hatred or evil is a great loss to the mind, and every evil thought, deed of hatred, or any thought of reaction, if it is controlled, will be credited in our favor. It is not that we lose by thus restraining ourselves; we are gaining infinitely more than we suspect. Each time we suppress hatred or a feeling of anger, it is so much good energy stored up in our favor; that piece of energy will be converted into higher powers.

### 34. By throwing out and restraining the Breath.

The word used is *Prāna*. *Prāṇa* is not exactly breath. It is the name for the energy that is in the universe. Whatever you see in the universe, whatever moves or works, or has

Life is a manifestation of this *Prāṇa*. The sum total of the energy displayed in the universe is called *Prāṇa*. Before a cycle begins, this *Prāṇa* remains in an almost motionless state, and when the cycle begins, this *Prāṇa* starts to manifest itself. It is this *Prāṇa* that is manifested as motion, as the nervous motion in human beings or animals, and this same *Prāṇa* manifests as thought, and so on. The whole universe is a combination of *Prāṇa* and *Ākāśa*; so is the human body. From *Ākāśa*, you get the different materials that you feel and see, and from *Prāṇa* come all the various forces. Now, this exhaling and restraining of the *Prāṇa* is what is called *Prāṇāyāma*.

*Patanjali*, the father of the *Yoga* Philosophy, does not give many specific directions regarding *Prāṇāyāma*, but later on, other *Yogīs* discovered various things about this *Prāṇāyāma* and turned it into a great science. For *Patanjali*, it is one of many ways, but he does not place much emphasis on it. He means that you simply exhale the air, draw it in, and hold it for some time—that is all—and by that, the mind will become a little calmer. But later on, you will find that a specific science called *Prāṇāyāma* evolved out of this. We will hear a little of what those later *Yogīs* have to say. I have told you some of this before, but a little repetition will help to fix it in your minds. 

First, you must remember that this *Prāṇa* is not the breath. Rather, that which causes the motion of the breath—that which is the vitality of the breath—is the *Prāṇa*. Again, the word *Prāṇa* is used for all the senses; they are all called *Prāṇa*, and the mind is called *Prāṇa*. Thus, we see that *Prāṇa* is the name of a certain force. And yet we cannot call it force, because force is only its manifestation. It is that which manifests itself as force and everything else in the way of motion. The *Chitta*, the mind-stuff, is the engine which draws in the *Prāṇa* from the surroundings, and

If there were no grey matter, there would be no memory, because memory means going over these old streets, retracing a thought, as it were. A little of the Infinite is projected into consciousness, and that we call our world. So there is an Infinite beyond, and religion has to deal with both: with the little lump we call our world, and with the Infinite beyond. Any religion which deals alone with either one of these two will be defective. It must deal with both. Consciousness—in the context of time, space, and causation—is quite familiar to us because we already exist within it, and ideas about this world have been with us since time immemorial. This is why you find that, in the practice of *Yoga*, ordinary people are initially pushed out of their familiar groove. In order to lessen these disturbances as much as possible, all these methods were devised by *Patanjali*, so that we may practice whichever one is best suited to us.

->35. Those forms of concentration that bring extraordinary sense perceptions cause the mind to persevere.<-

This naturally occurs with *Dhāraṇa* (concentration). The *Yogīs* say that if the mind becomes concentrated on the tip of the nose, one begins to smell wonderful perfumes after a few days. If it becomes concentrated at the root of the tongue, one begins to hear sounds; if on the tip of the tongue, one begins to taste wonderful flavors; if on the middle of the tongue, one feels as if he were coming into contact with something. If one concentrates his mind on the palate, he begins to see peculiar things. If a person whose mind is disturbed wants to take up these practices of *Yoga*, yet doubts their truth, his doubts will be set at rest when these experiences come to him after a little practice, and he will persevere.

This is another sort of concentration. ->37. Or (by meditation on) the heart that has<-
->given up all attachment to sense objects.<-

Take some holy person, some great person whom you revere, or some saint whom you know to be perfectly detached, and think of his heart. That heart has become detached; meditate on that heart, and it will calm the mind. If you cannot do that, there is the next way.

->38. Or by meditating on the knowledge that<-
->comes in sleep.<-

Sometimes a man dreams that he has seen angels coming to him and talking to him, that he is in an ecstatic condition, or that he has heard music floating through the air. He is in a blissful condition in that dream, and when he awakes it makes a deep impression on him. Think of that dream as real, and meditate upon it. If you cannot do that, meditate on any holy thing that pleases you.

->39. Or by meditation on anything that appeals<-
->to one as good.<-

This does not mean any wicked subject, but anything good that you like—any place that you like best, any scenery that you like best, any idea that you like best, or anything that will concentrate the mind.

->40. The *Yogi's* mind, meditating in this way, becomes<-
->unobstructed from the atomic to the Infinite.<-

41. The *<term>Yogī</term>* whose *<term>Vṛttis</term>* have thus become powerless (controlled) obtains in the receiver, receiving, and received (the self, the mind, and external objects), concentratedness and sameness, like a crystal (in the presence of differently colored objects).

What results from this constant meditation? We must remember how, in a previous aphorism, *Patanjali* went into the various states of meditation: how the first will be the gross objects and the second the fine objects, and from them the advance is toward even finer objects of meditation. We must also remember how, in all these meditations—which are only of the first degree and not very high ones—we achieve the result that we can meditate as easily on fine objects as on grosser ones. Here the *<term>Yogī</term>* sees three things: the receiver, the received, and the receiving, which correspond to the Soul, the object, and the mind. Three objects of meditation are given to us. First, the gross things, such as bodies or material objects; second, fine things, such as the mind, the *<term>Chitta</term>*; and third, the *<term>Puruṣa</term>* qualified—not the *<term>Puruṣa</term>* itself, but egoism. By practice, the *<term>Yogī</term>* becomes established in all these meditations. Whenever he meditates, he can keep out all other thoughts; he becomes identified with that on which he meditates. When he meditates, he is like a piece of crystal; in the presence of flowers, the crystal becomes almost identified with the flowers. If the flower is red, the crystal looks red; if the flower is blue, the crystal looks blue.

->42. Sound, meaning, and resulting knowledge, being mixed together, are called *<term>Samādhi</term>* with reasoning.<-

Sound here means vibration; meaning refers to the nerve currents which conduct it; and knowledge is the reaction. <term>Patanjali</term> calls all the various meditations we have had so far *Savitarka* (meditations with reasoning). Later on, he will give us higher and higher *Dhyānas*. In those called "with reasoning," we maintain the duality of subject and object, which results from the mixture of word, meaning, and knowledge. First, there is the external vibration—the word; this, carried inward by the sense currents, is the meaning. After that, there occurs a reactionary wave in the *<term>Chitta</term>*, which is knowledge; but the mixture of these three makes up what we call knowledge. In all the meditations up to this point, we get this mixture as the object of meditation. The next *<term>Samādhi</term>* is higher.

->43. The *<term>Samādhi</term>* called "without reasoning" occurs when the memory is purified, or devoid of qualities, expressing only the meaning (of the meditated object).<-

It is by the practice of meditation on these three that we reach the state where these three do not mix. We can get rid of them. We will first try to understand what these three are. Here is the *<term>Chitta</term>*; you will always remember the simile of the lake (the mind-stuff) and the vibration (the word or sound) as a pulsation coming over it. You have that calm lake within you, and I pronounce a word: "cow." As soon as it enters through your ears, there is a wave produced in your *<term>Chitta</term>* along with it. So, that wave represents the idea of the cow—the form or the meaning, as we call it. That apparent

<summary>This page defines the first two stages of Samādhi, explaining how sound, meaning, and mental reaction combine to form our standard knowledge and how higher meditation seeks to separate these elements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the internal mental processes during meditation, specifically focusing on the transition from Savitarka (with reasoning) to Nirvitarka (without reasoning) and the classification of objects of concentration from gross to fine.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>RAJA YOGA</header>

The cow that you know is really that wave in the mind-stuff, and it arises as a reaction to internal and external sound vibrations; when the sound ceases, the wave dies away. That wave can never exist without a word. You may ask how it is when we only think of the cow and do not hear a sound. You are making that sound yourself. You are saying “cow” faintly in your mind, and with that comes a wave. There cannot be any wave without this impulse of sound; when it does not come from the outside, it comes from the inside. When the sound dies, the wave dies. What remains? The result of the reaction, and that is knowledge. These three are so closely combined in our mind that we cannot separate them. When the sound comes, the senses vibrate, and the wave rises in reaction; they follow one another so closely that there is no distinguishing one from the other. When this meditation has been practiced for a long time, memory—the receptacle of all impressions—becomes purified, and we are able to distinguish them clearly from one another. This is called “<term>Nirvitarka</term>,” or concentration without reasoning.

> 44. By this process, the concentrations with discrimination and without discrimination, whose objects are finer, are also explained.

A process similar to the preceding one is applied again, only the objects to be taken up in the former meditations were gross; in these, they are fine.

> 45. The finer objects end with the *<term>Pradhāna</term>*.

Gross objects are only the elements and everything manufactured out of them. The fine objects begin with the

*<term>Tanmatras</term>* or fine particles. The *<term>Puruṣa</term>* (the Soul) alone is excepted from this definition.

->46. These concentrations are with seed.<-

These do not destroy the seeds of past actions, and thus cannot give liberation; however, what they bring to the *<term>Yogī</term>* is stated in the following aphorisms.

The idea is that we must get our knowledge of ordinary objects by direct perception, by inference from that, and from the testimony of competent people. By "competent people," the *<term>Yogīs</term>* always mean the *<term>Rishīs</term>*, or the Seers of the thoughts recorded in the Scriptures—the *<term>Vedas</term>*. According to them, the only proof of the Scriptures

<page-num>120</page-num>

is that they were the testimony of competent persons, yet they say the Scriptures cannot take us to realization. We can read all the *<term>Vedas</term>*, and yet will not realize anything, but when we practice their teachings, then we attain that state which realizes what the Scriptures say, which penetrates where reason cannot go, and where the testimony of others cannot avail. This is what is meant by this aphorism: that realization is real religion, and all the rest is only preparation—hearing lectures, reading books, or reasoning is merely preparing the ground; it is not religion. Intellectual assent and intellectual dissent are not religion. 

The central idea of the *<term>Yogīs</term>* is that just as we come in direct contact with the objects of the senses, so religion can be directly perceived in a far more intense sense. The truths of religion, such as God and the Soul, cannot be perceived by the external senses. I cannot see God with my eyes, nor can I touch Him with my hands, and we also know that neither can we reason beyond the senses. Reason leaves us at a point that is quite indecisive; we may reason all our lives, as the world has been doing for thousands of years, and the result is that we find we are unable to prove or disprove the facts of religion. 

What we perceive directly we take as the basis, and upon that basis we reason. So it is obvious that reasoning has to run within these bounds of perception. It can never go beyond; the whole scope of realization, therefore, is beyond sense perception. The *<term>Yogīs</term>* say that man can go beyond his direct sense perception, and beyond his reason also. Man has in him the faculty, the power, of transcending his intellect even, and that power is in every being, every creature. By the practice of *<term>Yoga</term>* that power is aroused, and then man transcends the ordinary limits of reason, and directly perceives things which are beyond all reason.

<summary>This page argues that true religion consists of direct spiritual realization rather than mere intellectual study or scriptural knowledge. It explains that while reason is limited by sense perception, the practice of Yoga awakens a higher faculty that allows one to transcend the intellect and perceive truths beyond the reach of the senses.</summary>

<keywords>realization, religion, Vedas, Yogīs, Yoga, sense perception, reason, transcendence, spiritual experience</keywords>

# 50. The resulting impression from this *<term>Samādhi</term>* obstructs all other impressions.

We have seen in the previous aphorism that the only way to attain that <term>super-consciousness</term> is through <term>concentration</term>, and we have also seen that what hinders the mind from <term>concentration</term> are the past *<term>Samskāras</term>*, or impressions. You have all observed that when you try to concentrate your mind, your thoughts wander. When you try to think of God, that is the exact moment all these *<term>Samskāras</term>* choose to appear. At other times they are not as active, but when you want them to stop, they are certain to be there, doing their best to crowd into your mind. Why is that? Why are they so much more powerful during <term>concentration</term>? It is because you are suppressing them, and they react with all their strength. At other times they do not react. How countless these old past impressions must be; they are all lodged somewhere in the *<term>Chitta</term>*, ready and waiting like tigers to pounce. These must be suppressed so that the single idea we choose may arise, to the exclusion of all others. Instead, they are all struggling to come up at the same time. These are the various ways *<term>Samskāras</term>* hinder the mind's <term>concentration</term>; therefore, the *<term>Samādhi</term>* just described is the best to practice because of its power to suppress these *<term>Samskāras</term>*. The *<term>Samskāra</term>* created by this type of <term>concentration</term> will be so powerful that it will obstruct the actions of the others and hold them in check.

# 51. By the restraint of even this (impression, which obstructs all other impressions), once all are restrained, comes the “<term>seedless Samādhi</term>.”

<summary>This page explains how the practice of Samādhi creates a powerful mental impression that suppresses all other past impressions (Samskāras), eventually leading to a state of complete mental restraint known as seedless Samādhi.</summary>
<keywords>Samādhi, Samskāras, Chitta, concentration, super-consciousness, seedless Samādhi, mental restraint, impressions</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of how concentration suppresses past impressions, leading to the ultimate realization of the Soul's true nature.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>RAJA YOGA</header>

You remember that our goal is to perceive the Soul itself. We cannot perceive the Soul because it has become mingled with nature, with the mind, and with the body. The most ignorant person thinks their body is the Soul. The more learned person thinks their mind is the Soul, but both are mistaken. What causes the Soul to get mingled with all of this? These different waves in the *<term>Chitta</term>* rise and cover the Soul, and we are only a small reflection of the Soul through these waves. So, if the wave is one of anger, we see the Soul as angry: "I am angry," we say. If the wave is a wave of love, we see ourselves reflected in that wave and say we are loving. If that wave is one of weakness and the Soul is reflected in it, we think we are weak. 

These various ideas come from these impressions—these *<term>Samskāras</term>*—covering the Soul. The real nature of the Soul is not perceived until all the waves have subsided. Therefore, first, *<term>Patanjali</term>* teaches us the meaning of these waves; secondly, the best way to suppress them; and thirdly, how to make one wave so strong as to suppress all other waves, like fire eating fire, as it were. When only one remains, it will be easy to suppress that also. When that is gone, this *<term>Samādhi</term>* of concentration is called seedless; it leaves nothing, and the Soul is manifested exactly as It is, in Its own glory. Then alone we know that the Soul is not a compound; It is the only eternal simple in the universe, and, as such, It cannot be born, It cannot die, It is immortal, indestructible, and the ever-living essence of intelligence.

<summary>The author explains that the Soul is obscured by mental waves and past impressions, which cause us to misidentify with the body or mind. By practicing concentration to suppress these waves, one reaches a "seedless" Samādhi, revealing the Soul as an eternal, immortal, and simple essence.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Chitta, Samskāras, Patanjali, Samādhi, concentration, seedless, immortality, mental waves</keywords>

<page-num>123</page-num>

# CHAPTER II.

## CONCENTRATION—ITS PRACTICE

### 1. Mortification, study, and surrendering the fruits of work to God are called *Kriya Yoga*.

The levels of *Samādhi* with which we ended our last chapter are very difficult to attain; therefore, we must approach them gradually. The first step, the preliminary step, is called *Kriya Yoga*. Literally, this means work—working toward *Yoga*. The senses are the horses, the mind is the reins, the intellect is the charioteer, the soul is the rider, and this body is the chariot. The master of the household, the King, the Self of man, is sitting in this chariot. If the horses are very strong and do not obey the reins, and if the charioteer, the intellect, does not know how to control the horses, then this chariot will come to grief. But if the senses—the horses—are well controlled, and if the reins—the mind—are held firmly in the hands of the charioteer—the intellect—the chariot reaches the goal. 

What is meant, therefore, by mortification? It means holding the reins firmly while guiding this body and mind: not letting the body do anything it likes, but keeping them both under proper control. Next is study. What is meant by study in this case? Not the study of novels, fiction, or storybooks, but the study of those books which teach the liberation of the soul. Furthermore, this study does not mean controversial studies at all. The *Yogi* is supposed to have finished his period of controversy. He has had enough of all that and has become satisfied. He only studies to intensify his convictions. *Vāda* and *Siddhānta*. These are the two sorts of Scriptural knowledge: *Vāda* (the argumentative) and *Siddhānta* (the

<summary>The author introduces Kriya Yoga as the preliminary practice for attaining Samādhi, consisting of self-discipline, spiritual study, and devotion. Using the analogy of a chariot, he explains that the intellect must control the mind and senses to guide the soul toward liberation.</summary>

<keywords>Kriya Yoga, Samādhi, mortification, self-control, spiritual study, Vāda, Siddhānta, Yoga practice, chariot analogy</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Scriptural knowledge, moving from the distinction between argumentative and decisive study to the practical application of essential truths.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>

<header>RAJA YOGA</header>

decisive). When a person is entirely ignorant, they take up the first part of this: argumentative fighting and reasoning *pro* and *con*; and when they have finished that, they take up the *Siddhānta*, the decisive stage, and arrive at a conclusion. Simply arriving at this conclusion is not enough. It must be intensified. Books are infinite in number and time is short; therefore, the secret of knowledge is to take what is essential. Take that out, and then try to live up to it. There is an old simile in India that if you place a cup of milk mixed with plenty of water before a *Rāja Hamsa* (swan), it will take all the milk and leave the water. In that way, we should take what is of value in knowledge and leave the dross behind. All these intellectual gymnastics are necessary at first. We must not go blindly into anything. The *Yogī* has passed the argumentative stage and has reached a conclusion that is as immovable as rock. The only thing he now seeks to do is to intensify that conclusion. "Do not argue," he says; if someone forces arguments upon you, be silent. Do not answer any argument, but go away free, because arguments only disturb the mind. The goal is to train the intellect, so what is the use of disturbing it any more? The intellect is but a weak instrument and can give only knowledge limited by the senses; the *Yogī* wants to go beyond the senses; therefore, the intellect is of no use to him. He is certain of this, and therefore is silent and does not argue. Every argument throws his mind out of balance and creates a disturbance in the *Chitta*, and this disturbance is a drawback. These arguments and searches of the reason are only steps along the way. There are much higher things behind them. The whole of life is not for schoolboy fights and debating societies. By "surrendering the fruits of work to God," we are to take to ourselves neither credit nor blame, but to give both up to the Lord and be at peace.

<summary>The author explains that once a seeker moves beyond intellectual debate to a firm conclusion, they must focus on essential truths and avoid further arguments to maintain mental peace. True spiritual progress requires going beyond the limited intellect and surrendering the results of one's actions to God.</summary>

<keywords>Siddhānta, Rāja Hamsa, Yogī, Chitta, Raja Yoga, intellectual gymnastics, surrender to God, essential knowledge</keywords>

2. **(They are for) the practice of *Samādhi* and minimizing the pain-bearing obstructions.**

Most of us treat our minds like spoiled children, allowing them to do whatever they want. Therefore, it is necessary that there should be constant practice of the previously mentioned disciplines in order to gain control of the mind and bring it under subjection. The obstructions to *Yoga* arise from a lack of this control and cause us pain. They can only be removed by denying the mind and holding it in check through these various means.

3. **The pain-bearing obstructions are—ignorance, egoism, attachment, aversion, and clinging to life.**

These are the five pains, the fivefold tie that binds us down. Of course, ignorance is the mother of all the rest. It is the only cause of all our misery. What else can make us miserable? The nature of the Soul is eternal bliss. What can make it sorrowful except ignorance, hallucination, or delusion? All this pain of the soul is simply delusion.

4. **Ignorance is the productive field of all those that follow, whether they are dormant, attenuated, overpowered, or expanded.**

Impressions are the cause of these, and these impressions exist in different degrees. There are the dormant ones. You often hear the expression “innocent as a baby,” yet within the baby may be the state of a demon or of a god, which will come out eventually. In the *Yogī*, these impressions—the *Saṃskāras* left by past actions—are attenuated; that is, they are in a very fine state, and he can control them and not allow them to become

->5. Ignorance is mistaking that which is non-eternal, impure, painful, and non-Self for the eternal, pure, happy <term>Ātman</term> (the Self).<-

All these various types of impressions have one source: ignorance. We must first learn what ignorance is. All of us think, "I am the body," rather than the Self—the pure, the radiant, the ever-blissful—and that is ignorance. We think of a person and see them only as a body. This is the great delusion.

->6. Egoism is the identification of the seer with the instrument of seeing.<-

The seer is truly the Self: the pure one, the ever-holy, the infinite, the immortal. That is the Self of man. And what are the instruments? The <term>*Chitta*</term>, or mind-stuff; the <term>*Buddhi*</term>, or determinative faculty; the <term>*Manas*</term>, or mind; and the <term>*Indriyāni*</term>, or sense organs. These are the instruments for the Self to perceive the external world, and the identification of the Self with these instruments is what is called the ignorance of egoism. We say, "I am the mind," "I am thought," "I am angry," or "I am happy." How can we be angry, and how can we hate? We should identify ourselves with the Self, which cannot change. If it is unchangeable, how can it be happy one moment and unhappy the next? It is formless, infinite, and omnipresent. What can change it? It is beyond all law. What can affect it? Nothing in the universe can produce an effect on it; yet,

### 7. Attachment is that which dwells on pleasure.

We find pleasure in certain things, and the mind flows toward them like a current. This movement, following the pleasure center as it were, is attachment. We are never attached to anyone in whom we do not find pleasure. We find pleasure in very strange things sometimes, but the definition remains the same: wherever we find pleasure, we are attached.

### 8. Aversion is that which dwells on pain.

We immediately seek to get away from whatever gives us pain.

### 9. Flowing through its own nature, and established even in the learned, is the clinging to life.

You see this clinging to life manifested in every animal. Many attempts have been made to build a theory of a future life upon this instinct, because people love their lives so much that they desire a future life as well. Of course, it goes without saying that this argument has little value. However, the most curious part is that, in Western countries, the idea that this clinging to life indicates the possibility of a future life is applied only to humans and does not include animals. In India, this clinging to life has been one of the arguments used to prove past experience and existence. For instance, if it is true that all our knowledge comes from experience, then it is certain that we cannot imagine or understand what we have never experienced. As soon as chickens are hatched, they begin to

pick up food. Many times it has been seen that when ducks have been hatched by hens, as soon as they come out of the eggs, they fly to the water, and the mother thinks they will be drowned. If experience is the only source of knowledge, where did these chickens learn to pick up food, or the ducklings learn that the water was their natural element? If you say it is instinct, it means nothing—it is simply giving it a name, but it is no explanation. What is this instinct? We have many instincts in ourselves. For instance, most of you ladies play the piano, and remember, when you first learned, how carefully you had to put your fingers on the black and the white keys, one after the other; but now, after long years of practice, you can talk with your friends, and your hand goes on just the same. It has become instinct; it becomes automatic. But so far as we know, all the cases which we now regard as automatic are degenerated reason. In the language of the <term>Yogī</term>, instinct is involved reason. Discrimination becomes involved, and becomes automatic <term>*Samskāras*</term>. Therefore, it is perfectly logical to think that all we call instinct in this world is simply involved reason. As reason cannot come without experience, all instinct is, therefore, the result of past experience. Chickens fear the hawk, and ducklings love the water, and these are both the result of past experience. Then the question is whether that experience belongs to a particular soul, or simply to the body—whether this experience which comes to the duck is the duck's forefather's experience, or the duck's own experience. Modern scientists hold that it belongs to the body, but the <term>Yogīs</term> hold that it is the experience of the soul, transmitted through the body. This is called the theory of reincarnation. We have seen that all of our knowledge, whether we call it perception or reason, or instinct, must

These *<term>Chitta Vrttis</term>*, these mind-waves which are tangible, we can recognize and feel; they can be more easily controlled. But what about these subtle instincts? are acting on us. To the vast majority of humanity, the subtle states of these passions are not even known—the state when they are slowly rising from beneath the level of consciousness. When a bubble rises from the bottom of a lake, we do not see it, or even when it has nearly reached the surface; it is only when it bursts and creates a ripple that we know it is there. We shall only be successful in grappling with the waves when we can catch them in their subtle causes; until you can grasp them and subdue them before they become manifest, there is no hope of perfectly conquering any passion. To control our passions, we must control them at their very roots; only then shall we be able to burn out their very seeds. Just as roasted seeds planted in the ground will never sprout, so these passions will never arise.

->**10. These subtle states are to be rejected by opposing modifications.**<-

How are these subtle *Samskāras* to be controlled? We must begin with the large waves and work our way down. For instance, when a large wave of anger has entered the mind, how are we to control that? Simply by raising a large opposing wave. Think of love. Sometimes a mother is very angry with her husband, and while in that state, her baby enters; she kisses the baby, the old wave dies out, and a new wave arises—love for the child. That suppresses the other one. Love is the opposite of anger. So we find that by raising opposing waves, we can conquer those we wish to reject. Then, if we can raise these subtle opposing waves in our subtle nature, they will check the subtle workings of anger beneath the conscious surface. We have seen now that all these instinctive actions first began as conscious actions and became subtler and subtler. So, if good waves in the conscious

<summary>The author explains how to conquer passions by addressing them in their subtle, subconscious states before they become obvious. Using the principle of "opposing modifications," one can suppress negative waves like anger by intentionally raising positive waves like love.</summary>

->11. By meditation, their modifications are to be<-
->rejected.<-

Meditation is one of the great means of controlling the rising of these large waves. Through meditation, you can make the mind subdue these waves; and if you continue practicing meditation for days, months, and years, until it has become a habit—until it arises even without conscious effort—anger and hatred will be controlled and checked.

->12. The receptacle of works has its root in these<-
->pain-bearing obstructions, and their<-
->experience is in this visible life, or in the<-
->unseen life.<-

By the "receptacle of works" is meant the sum total of these *Samskāras*. Whatever work we do, the mind is thrown into a wave; after the work is finished, we think the wave is gone. It is not. It has only become subtle, but it is still there. When we try to remember the thing, it rises again and becomes a wave. Therefore, it was there; if it had not been there, there would be no memory. So, every action and every thought, good or bad, simply goes down and becomes subtle, and is stored up there. They are called "pain-bearing obstructions"—including both happy and unhappy thoughts—because, according to the *Yogīs*, both ultimately bring pain. All happiness that comes from the senses will eventually bring pain. All enjoyment will make us thirst for more, and that results in pain. There is no limit to human desires; one continues desiring, and when a point is reached where a desire cannot be fulfilled, the result is pain. Therefore, the *Yogīs* regard the

In exceptional cases, when these *Samskāras* are very strong, they bear fruit quickly; exceptional acts of wickedness or of goodness bring their fruits in this life. The *Yogīs* even hold that those who are able to acquire a tremendous power of good *Samskāras* do not have to die, but, even in this life, can change their bodies into god-bodies.
