Why should this not be so? The physiological meaning of food is the assimilation of energy from the sun. This energy has reached the plant, the plant is eaten by an animal, and the animal is eaten by us. The science of it is that we take a certain amount of energy from the sun and make it part of ourselves. That being the case, why should there be only one way of assimilating energy? The plant’s way is not the same as ours; the earth’s process of assimilating energy differs from our own. But all things assimilate energy in some form or another. The *Yogīs* say that they are able to assimilate energy by the power of the mind alone, and that they can draw in as much as they desire without resorting to ordinary methods. Just as a spider makes its web out of its own substance, becomes bound in its web, and cannot go anywhere except along the lines of that web, so we have projected out of our own substance this network called the nerves, and we cannot work except through the channels of those nerves. ->13. While the root exists, its fruition appears in the form of species, lifespan, and the experience of pleasure and pain.<-

When the roots, the causes, and the *<term>Samskāras</term>* exist, they manifest again and create effects. As the cause subsides, it becomes the effect, and that effect becomes more subtle, eventually becoming the cause of the subsequent effect. A tree bears a seed and becomes the cause of the next tree, and so the cycle continues. All our current actions are the effects of past *<term>Samskāras</term>*. In turn, these *<term>Samskāras</term>* become the cause of future actions, and so we go on. This aphorism states that while the cause remains, the fruit must arrive in the form of species; one person will be a human, another an angel, another an animal, and another a demon. Then there are different effects regarding lifespan; one person lives fifty years, another a hundred, and another dies at two years old and never reaches maturity; all these variations in life are regulated by these past actions. One person is born, so to speak, for pleasure; even if he hides himself in a forest, pleasure will follow him there. Another person, wherever he goes, is followed by pain; everything becomes painful. It is all the result of their own past. According to the philosophy of the *<term>Yogis</term>*, all virtuous actions bring pleasure, and all wicked actions bring pain. Anyone who performs wicked deeds is sure to reap their fruit in the form of pain.

->14. They bear fruit as pleasure or pain, caused by virtue or vice.<-

->15. To those who possess discrimination, everything is, in a sense, painful because everything brings—<-

The *Yogis* say that the person who has powers of discrimination—the person of good sense—sees through all these various things called pleasure and pain. He knows that they are always equally distributed, that one follows the other, and that they melt into one another. He sees that people follow an *ignis fatuus* all their lives and never succeed in fulfilling their desires. There has never been a love in this world that did not experience decay. The great king *Yudisthira* once said that the most wonderful thing in life is that we see people dying around us every moment, yet we think we shall never die. Surrounded by fools on every side, we think we are the only exceptions, the only learned people. Surrounded by all sorts of experiences of fickleness, we think our love is the only lasting love. How can that be? Even love is selfish, and the *Yogi* says that, in the end, we shall find that even the love of husbands and wives, and children and friends, slowly decays. Decay seizes everything in this life. It is only when everything—even love—fails, that a person suddenly discovers how vain and dreamlike this world is. Then he catches a glimpse of *Vairagyam* (renunciation); he catches a glimpse of the beyond. It is only by giving up this world that the other comes—never through building onto this one. There has never yet been a great soul who did not have to reject sensory pleasures and enjoyments to become so. The cause of misery is the clash between different forces of nature, one dragging one way and another dragging another, making permanent happiness impossible.

### 16. The misery that has not yet come is to be avoided.

Some *Karma* we have already worked out, some we are working out now in the present, and some is waiting to bear fruit in the future. That which we have already worked out is past and gone.

We must work through what we are experiencing now, and it is only what is waiting to bear fruit in the future that we can conquer and control. Therefore, all our forces should be directed toward the control of that *Karma* which has not yet borne fruit. That is what is meant in the previous aphorism when *Patanjali* says that these various *Samskāras* are to be controlled by counteracting waves.

### 17. The cause of what is to be avoided is the junction of the seer and the seen.

Who is the seer? The Self of Man, the *Puruṣa*. What is the seen? The whole of nature, beginning with the mind and down to gross matter. All this pleasure and pain arises from the junction between this *Puruṣa* and the mind. You must remember that according to this philosophy, the *Puruṣa* is pure; it is when it is joined to nature, and through reflection, that it appears to feel either pleasure or pain.

### 18. The experienced is composed of elements and organs, has the nature of illumination, action, and inertia, and exists for the purpose of experience and release (of the experiencer).

The experienced, meaning nature, is composed of elements and organs—the gross and fine elements that compose the whole of nature, and the organs of the senses, mind, etc., and

There is a story that *Indra*, the king of the gods, once became a pig wallowing in the mud; he had a sow and many piglets, and he was very happy. Then some other gods saw his situation, came to him, and said, "You are the king of the gods; you have everything that the gods command. Why are you here?" But *Indra* said, "Leave me alone; I am fine here. I do not care for the heavens as long as I have this sow and these little pigs." The poor gods were at their wits' end. After a while, they decided to come and slowly kill one of the piglets, then another, until they had killed all the pigs and the sow too. When all were dead, *Indra* began to weep and mourn. Then the gods ripped his pig body open and he emerged from it. He began to laugh when he realized what a hideous dream he had experienced—that he, the king of the gods, had become a pig and thought that the pig-life was the only life! Not only that, but he had wanted the whole universe to enter that pig life! The *Puruṣa*, when it identifies itself with nature, forgets that it is pure and infinite. The *Puruṣa* does not merely live; it is life itself. It does not just exist; it is existence itself. The Soul does not simply know; it is knowledge itself. It is a complete mistake to say that the Soul lives, or knows, or loves. Love and existence are not the qualities of the *Puruṣa*, but its essence. When they are reflected upon something else, you may call them the qualities of that thing. But they are not the qualities of the *Puruṣa*; they are the essence of this great *Ātman*, this Infinite Being without birth or death, who is established in His own glory, but appears as if He has become

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Yoga philosophy, transitioning into a detailed explanation of Sānkhya philosophy and the three qualities (gunas) that constitute nature and the human mind.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>YOGA APHORISMS: CONCENTRATION II</header>

---

*Sānkhya* philosophy. According to the *Sānkhyas*, nature is both the material and efficient cause of this universe. In this nature, there are three sorts of materials: *Sattva*, *Rajas*, and *Tamas*. The *Tamas* material is everything that is dark, everything that is ignorant and heavy; *Rajas* is activity. *Sattva* is calmness and light. When nature is in the state before creation, they call it *Avyaktam*—undefined or undifferentiated; that is, a state in which there is no distinction of form or name, a state in which these three materials are held in perfect balance. Then the balance is disturbed, these different materials begin to mingle in various fashions, and the result is this universe. In every person, these three materials also exist. When the *Sattva* material prevails, knowledge comes. When the *Rajas* material prevails, activity comes, and when the *Tamas* material prevails, darkness comes along with lassitude, idleness, and ignorance. According to the *Sānkhya* theory, the highest manifestation of this nature, consisting of these three materials, is what they call *Mahat*, or intelligence—universal intelligence—and each human mind is a part of that cosmic intelligence. Then, out of *Mahat* comes the mind. In *Sānkhya* Psychology, there is a sharp distinction between *Manas*, the mind function, and the function of the *Buddhi* (intellect). The mind's function is simply to collect and carry impressions and present them to the *Buddhi* (the individual *Mahat*), and the *Buddhi* decides upon them. So, out of *Mahat* comes mind, and out of mind comes fine material, and this fine material combines and becomes the gross material outside—the external universe. The claim of the *Sānkhya* philosophy is that, beginning with the intellect and coming down to a block of stone, everything has come out of the same thing, only as finer or grosser states of existence. The *Buddhi* is the finest state of existence of the materials, and then comes *Ahamkāra*,

These <term>Tanmātras</term> combine and become denser, and finally produce this universe. The finer is the cause, and the denser is the effect. It begins with the <term>Buddhi</term>, which is the finest material, and continues becoming increasingly dense until it becomes this universe. That which is not the result of combination cannot die; these <term>Puruṣas</term>, or Souls, are infinite in number.

By the "undefined," he means the very fine materials, the <term>Tanmātras</term>, which cannot be sensed by ordinary people. Every day of our lives, we are throwing out a mass of good or evil, and everywhere we go the atmosphere is full of these materials. The difficulty with humanity is that they forget the original meaning and put the cart before the horse. It was people who made these places holy, and then the effect became the cause and made people holy. If only the wicked were to go there, it would become as bad as any other place. It is not the building but the people that make a church, and that is what we always forget. That is why sages and holy persons, who have so much of this <term>*Sattva*</term> quality, are emanating so much of it around them and exerting a tremendous influence day and night on their surroundings. A person may become so pure that their purity will become tangible, so to speak. The body has become pure, and in an intensely physical sense—not as a figurative idea or poetic language—it emanates that purity wherever it goes. Whoever comes in contact with that person becomes pure. They say that unintelligent things slowly evolve into animals, and from animals slowly evolve into humans. They claim that instead of everything coming out of intelligence, intelligence is itself the last to arrive. Both the religious and the scientific statements, though seemingly directly opposed to each other, are true. Take an infinite series A—B—A—B—A—B, etc. The question is which is first, A or B. If you take the series as A—, you will say that A is first, but if you take it as B—A you will say that B is first. It depends on the way you are looking at it. Intelligence evolves and becomes gross matter, and this again evolves as intelligence, and again evolves as matter once more. The *Sānkhyas* and all religious thinkers put intelligence first, and the series becomes intelligence then matter, intelligence then matter. The scientist points to matter and says matter then intelligence, matter then intelligence. But they are both indicating the same chain. Indian philosophy, however, goes beyond both intelligence and matter and finds a <term>Puruṣa</term>, or Self, which is beyond all intelligence, and of which intelligence is but the borrowed light.

> 20. The seer is intelligence only, and though pure, is seen through the coloring of the intellect.

This is again *Sānkhya* philosophy. We have seen from this philosophy that from the lowest form up to intelligence, all is nature, but beyond nature are <term>Puruṣas</term> (souls), and these have no qualities. Then how does the soul appear to be

<summary>This page explains that the debate between religion and science regarding whether intelligence or matter came first is a matter of perspective in an infinite cycle. It introduces the Puruṣa as the ultimate Self that exists beyond both intelligence and nature.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Sānkhya, Puruṣa, intelligence, matter, evolution, seer, intellect, souls, Indian philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Sānkhya philosophy, specifically addressing how the pure soul appears to take on the qualities of the intellect and the paradox of human freedom versus bondage.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

happy or unhappy? By reflection. Just as if a piece of pure crystal is placed on a table and a red flower is put near it, the crystal appears to be red, so all these appearances of happiness or unhappiness are but reflections; the soul itself has no kind of coloring. The soul is separate from nature; nature is one thing, soul another, eternally separate. The *Sānkhyas* say that intelligence is a compound, that it grows and wanes, that it changes, just as the body changes, and that its nature is nearly the same as that of the body. As a fingernail is to the body, so is the body to intelligence. The nail is a part of the body, but it can be trimmed off hundreds of times, and the body will still last. Similarly, the intelligence lasts for eons, while this body can be trimmed off, thrown off. Yet intelligence cannot be immortal, because it changes—growing and waning. Anything that changes cannot be immortal. Certainly intelligence is manufactured, and that very fact shows us that there must be something beyond that, because it cannot be free. Everything connected with matter is in nature, and therefore bound forever. Who is free? That free one must certainly be beyond cause and effect. If you say that the idea of freedom is a delusion, I will say that the idea of bondage is also a delusion. Two facts come into our consciousness and stand or fall by each other. One is that we are bound. If we want to go through a wall, and our head bumps against that wall, we are limited by that wall. At the same time we find will, and think we can direct our will everywhere. At every step these contradictory ideas are coming to us. We have to believe that we are free, yet at every moment we find we are not free. If one idea is a delusion, the other is also a delusion, because both stand upon the same basis—consciousness. The *Yogi* says both are true; that we are bound so far as intelligence goes, that we are free as far as the soul is concerned. It is the real

<summary>This page explains that the soul is eternally separate from nature and only appears happy or unhappy through reflection, much like a crystal reflects the color of a nearby flower. nature of man, the Soul, the <term>*Puruṣa*</term>, which is beyond all laws of causation. Its freedom is filtering through layers and layers of matter, in various forms of intelligence, and mind, and all these things. It is its light that is shining through all. Intelligence has no light of its own. Each organ has a particular center in the brain; it is not that all the organs have one center; each organ is separate. Why do all these perceptions harmonize, and where do they get their unity? If it were in the brain there would be only one center for the eyes, the nose, and the ears, while we know for certain that there are different centers for each. But a person can see and hear at the same time, so a unity must exist behind intelligence. Intelligence is eternally connected with the brain, but behind even intelligence stands the <term>*Puruṣa*</term>, the unit, where all these different sensations and perceptions join and become one. The Soul itself is the center where all the different organs converge and become unified, and that Soul is free, and it is its freedom that tells you every moment that you are free. But you mistake and mix that freedom every moment with intelligence and mind. You try to attribute that freedom to the intelligence, and immediately find that intelligence is not free; you attribute that freedom to the body, and immediately nature tells you that you are again mistaken. That is why there is this mixed sense of freedom and bondage at the same time. The Yogi analyzes both what is free and what is bound, and his ignorance vanishes. He finds that the <term>*Puruṣa*</term> is free, and is the essence of that knowledge which, coming through the <term>*Buddhi*</term>, becomes intelligence, and, as such, is bound.

->22. Though destroyed for the one whose goal has been gained, it is still not destroyed, being common to others.<-

The whole idea of this nature is to make the Soul know that it is entirely separate from nature, and when the Soul knows this, nature has no more attractions for it. But the whole of nature vanishes only for that person who has become free. There will always remain an infinite number of others, for whom nature will go on working.

->23. Junction is the cause of the realization of the nature of both the powers, the experienced and its Lord.<-

According to this aphorism, when this Soul comes into conjunction with nature, both the power of the Soul and the power of nature become manifest in this conjunction, and all these manifestations are thrown out. Ignorance is the cause of this conjunction. We see every day that the cause of our pain or pleasure is always our joining ourselves with the body. If I were perfectly certain that I am not this body, I should take no notice of heat and cold, or anything of the kind. This body is a combination. It is only a fiction to say that I have one body, you another, and the sun another. The whole universe is one ocean of matter, and you are the name of a little particle, and I of another, and the sun of another. We know that this matter is continuously changing; what is

### 24. Ignorance is its cause.

Through ignorance, we have joined ourselves with a particular body and thus opened ourselves to misery. This idea of the body is a simple superstition. It is superstition that makes us happy or unhappy. It is superstition caused by ignorance that makes us feel heat and cold, pain and pleasure. It is our task to rise above this superstition, and the *Yogī* shows us how we can do this. It has been demonstrated that, under certain mental conditions, a person may be burned, yet, while that condition lasts, they will feel no pain. The difficulty is that this sudden upheaval of the mind comes like a whirlwind one minute and vanishes the next. If, however, we attain it scientifically through Yoga, we shall permanently achieve 그 separation of the Self from the body.

### 25. When that (ignorance) is absent, the junction is also absent, which is the state to be avoided; that is the independence of the seer.

According to this Yoga philosophy, it is through ignorance that the Soul has been joined with nature, and the objective is to get rid of nature’s control over us. That is the goal of all religions. Each Soul is potentially divine. The goal is to manifest this Divinity within by controlling nature, both external and internal. Do this either by work, or worship, or psychic control, or by philosophy—by one, or more, or all of these—and be free. This is the whole of religion. Doctrines, dogmas, rituals, books, temples, or forms are but

The *Yogī* claims that whoever controls the mind controls matter also. The internal nature is much higher than the external, and much more difficult to grapple with and much more difficult to control; therefore, whoever has conquered the internal nature controls the whole universe; it becomes their servant. *Rāja Yoga* proposes the methods of gaining this control. Higher forces than we know in physical nature will have to be subdued. This body is just the external crust of the mind. They are not two different things; they are just like the oyster and its shell. They are but two aspects of one thing; the internal substance of the oyster takes up matter from the outside and manufactures the shell. In the same way, these fine internal forces which are called the mind take up gross matter from the outside, and from that, manufacture this external shell, or body. If, then, we have control of the internal, it is very easy to have control of the external. Furthermore, these forces are not different. It is not that some forces are physical and some are mental; the physical forces are but the gross manifestations of the fine forces, just as the physical world is but the gross manifestation of the fine world.

It is only nature that changes, combining and recombining, dissolving continually. When through constant practice we begin to discriminate,

## 27. His knowledge is of the sevenfold highest stage.

When this knowledge comes, it will arrive, as it were, in seven stages, one after the other; and when one of these has begun, we may know that we are gaining knowledge. The first to appear will be the realization that we have known what is to be known. The mind will cease to be dissatisfied. While we are aware of thirsting for knowledge, we begin to seek here and there, wherever we think we can find some truth; and, failing to find it, we become dissatisfied and seek in a new direction. All searching is in vain until we begin to perceive that knowledge is within ourselves, that no one can help us, and that we must help ourselves. When we begin to practice the power of discrimination, the first sign that we are getting near the truth will be that the dissatisfied state will vanish. We shall feel quite sure that we have found the truth, and that it cannot be anything else but the truth. Then we may know that the sun is rising, that the morning is breaking for us, and, taking courage, we must persevere until the goal is reached. The second stage will be that all pain will be gone. It will be impossible for anything in the universe—physical, mental, or spiritual—to give us pain. The third will be that we shall obtain full knowledge, and that omniscience will be ours. Next will come what is called the freedom of the *Chitta*. We shall realize that all these difficulties and struggles have fallen away from us. All these vacillations of the mind, occurring when the mind cannot be controlled, have fallen away, just as a stone falls from the mountain top into the valley and never comes up again. The next will be that this *Chitta* itself will

<summary>This page describes the seven stages of enlightenment that occur as ignorance vanishes, beginning with internal satisfaction and the end of suffering. It emphasizes that true knowledge is found within oneself through the practice of discrimination.</summary>

They were working their own way, and we, through ignorance, joined ourselves to them. But we have been alone—omnipotent, omnipresent, ever-blessed. Our own Self was so pure and perfect that we required no one else. We required no one else to make us happy, for we are happiness itself. We shall find that this knowledge does not depend on anything else; throughout the universe, there can be nothing that will not become radiant before our knowledge. This will be the final state, and the *Yogi* will become peaceful and calm, never to feel any more pain, never to be deluded again, never to touch misery. 30. Non-killing, truthfulness, non-stealing, continence, and non-receiving are called *Yama*.

A man who wants to be a perfect *Yogī* must give up the idea of sex. The Soul has no sex; why should it degrade itself with sexual ideas? Later we shall understand better why these ideas must be abandoned. Receiving is just as bad as stealing—specifically, receiving gifts from others. Whenever someone receives gifts, their mind is acted upon by the mind of the giver, so that the person who receives the gifts becomes diminished. Receiving gifts destroys the independence of the mind and makes us mere slaves. Therefore, receive nothing.

31. These, unbroken by time, place, purpose, and caste, are (universal) great vows.

These practices—non-killing, non-stealing, chastity, and non-receiving—are to be practiced by every man, woman, and child; by every soul, irrespective of nation, country, or position.

32. Internal and external purification, contentment, mortification, study, and worship of God are the *Niyamas*.

External purification is keeping the body pure; an unclean man will never become a *Yogī*. There must be internal purification also. That is obtained by the virtues named previously. Of course, internal purity is of greater value than external, but both are necessary; external purity without internal purity is useless.

33. To obstruct thoughts which are hostile to *Yoga*, opposing thoughts should be introduced.

This is the way to practice all these virtues that have been stated: by holding thoughts of an opposite character in the mind. When the idea of stealing comes, non-stealing should be thought of. When the idea of receiving gifts comes, replace it with a contrary thought.

> 34. The obstructions to *Yoga* are killing, etc., whether committed, caused, or approved; whether through greed, anger, or ignorance; whether slight, moderate, or great; and the result is innumerable instances of ignorance and misery. This is the method of thinking the opposite.

If I tell a lie, or cause another to tell a lie, or approve of another doing so, it is equally sinful. If it is a very mild lie, it is still a lie. Every vicious thought will rebound; every thought of hatred which you have thought, even in a cave, is stored up and will one day come back to you with tremendous power in the form of some misery here. If you project all sorts of hatred and jealousy, they will rebound on you with compound interest. No power can avert them; once you have put them in motion, you will have to bear them. Remembering this will prevent you from doing wicked things.

> 35. Once non-killing is established, in his presence all hostilities cease in others.

If a man masters the idea of not injuring others, even animals which are by their nature ferocious will become peaceful before him. The tiger and the lamb will play together before that *Yogī* and will not hurt each other. When you have come to that state, then alone you will understand that you have become firmly established in non-injury.

### 36. By the establishment of truthfulness the *Yogī* gets the power of attaining for himself and others the fruits of work without the works.

When this power of truth is established in you, then even in a dream you will never tell a lie, in thought, word, or deed; whatever you say will be the truth. You may say to a man, "Be blessed," and that man will be blessed. If a man is diseased, and you say to him, "Be cured," he will be cured immediately.

### 37. By the establishment of non-stealing all wealth comes to the *Yogī*.

The more you flee from nature, the more she follows you, and if you do not care for her at all, she becomes your slave.

### 38. By the establishment of continence energy is gained.

The chaste brain has tremendous energy and gigantic willpower; without that, there can be no mental strength. All people with gigantic brains are very continent. It gives wonderful control over humanity. Leaders of people have been very continent, and this is what gave them power. Therefore, the *Yogī* must be continent.

### 39. When he is fixed in non-receiving he gets the memory of past life.

When the *Yogī* does not receive presents from others, he does not become indebted to others, but becomes independent and free. His mind becomes pure, because with every gift he receives, he also receives all the evils of the giver, and they come and lay layer after layer on his mind, until it is hidden under all

That is the sign of a virtuous man. Misery is caused by sin and by no other cause. What business have you with clouded faces? It is terrible. If you have a clouded face, do not go out that day; shut yourself up in your room. What right have you to carry this disease out into the world? When your mind has become controlled, you will have control over the whole body; instead of being a slave to the machine, the machine will be your slave. 44. By repetition of the *mantram* comes the realization of the intended deity.

The higher the beings that you want to reach, the harder the practice.

45. By sacrificing all to *Iśvara* comes *Samādhi*.

By resignation to the Lord, *Samādhi* becomes perfect.

->46. Posture is that which is firm and pleasant.<-

Now comes *Āsana*, or posture. Until you can achieve a firm seat, you cannot practice breathing and other exercises. A firm seat means that you do not feel the body at all; only then has it truly become firm. Normally, you will find that as soon as you sit for a few minutes, all sorts of disturbances arise in the body. But when you have moved beyond the idea of a physical body, you will lose all sense of it. You will feel neither pleasure nor pain. And when you become aware of your body again, it will feel so rested; it is the only perfect rest that you can give to the body. When you have succeeded in mastering the body and keeping it steady, your practice will remain stable, but while you are distracted by the body, your nerves become agitated, and you cannot concentrate the mind. We can make the seat firm by thinking of the infinite. We cannot think of the Absolute Infinite, but we can think of the infinite sky.

->47. By relaxing effort and meditating on the unlimited, posture becomes firm and pleasant.<-

Light and darkness, pleasure and pain, will not then disturb you.

->48. Once the seat is mastered, dualities do not obstruct.<-

The dualities are good and bad, heat and cold, and all such pairs of opposites.

->49. Controlling the motion of exhalation and inhalation follows after this.<-

When the posture has been mastered, then this motion is to be regulated and controlled; thus, we arrive at *Prāṇāyāma*.

<vocab>Yoga Aphorisms, Āsana, posture, Prāṇāyāma, exhalation, inhalation, dualities, concentration</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the practice of Āsana (posture) as a state of physical stability where the body is no longer felt, allowing for concentration. It also introduces Prāṇāyāma (breath control) as the next step after one has mastered a steady posture and overcome the influence of physical dualities.</summary>
<keywords>Āsana, posture, Prāṇāyāma, breath control, dualities, infinite meditation, concentration, Raja Yoga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Prāṇāyāma (breath control), moving from the general definition of the practice to a specific discussion of Prāṇa as cosmic energy and the various methods and stages of its regulation.</meta>

<header>RAJA YOGA</header>
<page-num>156</page-num>

the controlling of the vital forces of the body. *Prāṇa* is not breath, though it is usually so translated. It is the sum-total of cosmic energy. It is the energy that is in each body, and its most apparent manifestation is the motion of the lungs. This motion is caused by *Prāṇa* drawing in the breath, and it is what we seek to control in *Prāṇāyāma*. We begin by controlling the breath as the easiest way of getting control of the *Prāṇa*.

->50. Its modifications are either external, internal, or motionless; they are regulated by place, time, and number, and are either long or short.<-

The three sorts of motion of this *Prāṇāyāma* are: one by which we draw the breath in, another by which we exhale it, and the third action is when the breath is held in the lungs or stopped from entering the lungs. These, again, are varied by place and time. By place is meant that the *Prāṇa* is held in some particular part of the body. By time is meant how long the *Prāṇa* should be confined to a certain place, and so we are told how many seconds to maintain one motion and how many seconds to maintain another. The result of this *Prāṇāyāma* is *Udghāta*, the awakening of the *Kuṇḍalinī*.

->51. The fourth is restraining the *Prāṇa* by directing it either to the external or internal objects.<-

This is the fourth sort of *Prāṇāyāma*. *Prāṇa* can be directed either inside or outside.

->52. From that, the covering to the light of the *Chitta* is attenuated.<-

<vocab>Raja Yoga, Prāṇa, Prāṇāyāma, Udghāta, Kuṇḍalinī, Chitta</vocab>

<summary>This page defines Prāṇa as the cosmic energy manifested through the lungs and explains Prāṇāyāma as the regulation of this energy through inhalation, exhalation, and retention. These practices are measured by time and location within the body to awaken the Kuṇḍalinī and clarify the mind.</summary>

# YOGA APHORISMS: CONCENTRATION II 157

The <term>Chitta</term> has all knowledge by its very nature. It is made of <term>Sattva</term> particles but is covered by <term>Rajas</term> and <term>Tamas</term> particles, and through <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>, this covering is removed.

### 53. The mind becomes fit for <term>Dhāraṇā</term>.

After this covering has been removed, we are able to concentrate the mind.

### 54. The drawing in of the organs occurs when they give up their own objects and take the form of the mind-stuff.

These organs are separate states of the mind-stuff. For example, I see a book; the form is not in the book, it is in the mind. Something exists outside that calls that form up, but the real form is in the <term>Chitta</term>. These organs identify themselves with and take the forms of whatever comes to them. If you can restrain the mind-stuff from taking these forms, the mind will remain calm. This is called <term>Pratyāhāra</term>. From this arises supreme control of the organs.

When the <term>Yogī</term> has succeeded in preventing the organs from taking the forms of external objects, and in making them remain one with the mind-stuff, then comes perfect control of the organs. When the organs are perfectly under control, every muscle and nerve will be under control, because the organs are the centers of all sensations and of all actions. These organs are divided into organs of work and organs of sensation. When the organs are controlled, the <term>Yogī</term> can control all feeling and doing; the whole of the body will be under his control. Only then does one begin to feel joy in being born; then one can truthfully say, “Blessed am I that I was born.” When that control of the organs is obtained, we feel how wonderful this body really is.

Yoga, Aphorisms, <term>Chitta</term>, <term>Sattva</term>, <term>Rajas</term>, <term>Tamas</term>, <term>Prāṇāyāma</term>, <term>Dhāraṇā</term>, mind-stuff, <term>Pratyāhāra</term>, <term>Yogī</term>

<summary>This page explains that Prāṇāyāma removes the impurities covering the mind, making it fit for concentration and enabling Pratyāhāra, or the withdrawal of the senses. Mastering the senses allows the Yogī to control all bodily sensations and actions, leading to a state of profound gratitude and joy.</summary>

<keywords>Chitta, Sattva, Rajas, Tamas, Prāṇāyāma, Dhāraṇā, Pratyāhāra, sense withdrawal, self-control, mind-stuff</keywords>

# CHAPTER III.
## THE CHAPTER OF POWERS

We have now come to the chapter called the Chapter of Powers.

->1. *Dhāraṇā* is holding the mind onto some particular object.<-

*Dhāraṇā* (concentration) occurs when the mind holds onto some object, either inside the body or outside the body, and keeps itself in that state.

->2. An unbroken flow of knowledge toward that object is *Dhyāna*.<-

The mind tries to think of one object, to hold itself to one particular spot, such as the top of the head, the heart, etc. If the mind succeeds in receiving sensations only through that part of the body, and through no other part, that would be *Dhāraṇā*. When the mind succeeds in keeping itself in that state for some time, it is called *Dhyāna* (meditation).

->3. When that, giving up all forms, reflects only the meaning, it is *Samādhi*.<-

That is, when in meditation all forms are given up. Suppose I were meditating on a book, and I have gradually succeeded in concentrating the mind on it and perceiving only the internal sensations—the meaning, unexpressed in any form—that state of *Dhyāna* is called *Samādhi*.

->4. These three, when practiced in regard to one object, are *Samyama*.<-

<vocab>Dhāraṇā, Dhyāna, Samādhi, Samyama, concentration, meditation, Yoga</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the three internal stages of Yoga: Dhāraṇā (concentration), Dhyāna (meditation), and Samādhi (absorption). When these three are practiced together on a single object, the state is called Samyama.</summary>
<keywords>Dhāraṇā, Dhyāna, Samādhi, Samyama, concentration, meditation, Yoga Sutras, mental discipline</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Samyama and moves through the fifth, sixth, and seventh aphorisms regarding the development of spiritual powers and the stages of mental focus.</meta>

When a person can direct their mind to any particular object and fix it there, and then keep it there for a long time, separating the object from its internal part, this is *Samyama*; it is *Dhāraṇā*, *Dhyāna*, and *Samādhi* following one another and becoming one. The form of the thing has vanished, and only its meaning remains in the mind.

### 5. By the mastery of that comes the light of knowledge.

When one has succeeded in achieving this *Samyama*, all powers come under their control. This is the great instrument of the *Yogi*. The objects of knowledge are infinite, and they are divided into the gross, grosser, and grossest, and the fine, finer, and finest, and so on. This *Samyama* should first be applied to gross things; when you begin to gain knowledge of the gross, it should be brought slowly, by stages, to finer things.

### 6. That should be employed in stages.

This is a note of warning not to attempt to go too fast.

### 7. These three are closer than those that precede.

Before these, we had *Prāṇāyāma*, *Āsana*, *Yama*, and *Niyama*; these are external parts compared to these three—*Dhāraṇā*, *Dhyāna*, and *Samādhi*. Yet even these latter are external to the "seedless" *Samādhi*. When a person has attained them, they may attain omniscience and omnipresence, but that would not be salvation. These three would not make the mind *Nirvikalpa* (changeless), but would leave the seeds for acquiring bodies again; only when the seeds are, as the *Yogi* says, “fried,” do they lose the possibility of producing further plants. These powers cannot fry the seed.

<vocab>Samyama, Dhāraṇā, Dhyāna, Samādhi, Yogī, Prāṇāyāma, Āsana, Yama, Niyama, Nirvikalpa, omniscience, seedless Samādhi</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that mastering Samyama leads to great spiritual knowledge and powers, though it should be practiced in stages from gross to fine objects. While the internal practices of concentration and meditation are more advanced than physical disciplines, they are still considered external to the highest state of seedless Samādhi.</summary>
<keywords>Samyama, meditation stages, spiritual knowledge, Yogi, Samādhi, Nirvikalpa, seedless Samādhi, mental discipline</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the stages of Samādhi, specifically contrasting the lower states of mental control with the ultimate "seedless" state.</meta>

-># RAJA YOGA<-

### 8. But even they are external to the seedless (*Samādhi*).

Compared with that seedless *Samādhi*, therefore, even these are external. We have not yet reached the real *Samādhi*, the highest state, but only a lower stage in which this universe still exists as we see it, and in which all these powers reside.

### 9. By the suppression of the disturbed modifications of the mind, and by the rise of modifications of control, the mind is said to attain the controlling modifications—following the controlling powers of the mind.

That is to say, in this first state of *Samādhi*, the modifications of the mind have been controlled, but not perfectly. If they were perfectly controlled, there would be no modifications at all. If there is a modification which impels the mind to rush out through the senses, and the *Yogi* tries to control it, that very control itself will be a modification. One wave will be checked by another wave, so it will not be real *Samādhi*—where all the waves have subsided—as control itself will be a wave. Yet this lower *Samādhi* is very much nearer to the higher *Samādhi* than when the mind comes bubbling out.

### 10. Its flow becomes steady by habit.

The flow of this continuous control of the mind becomes steady when practiced day after day, and the mind obtains the faculty of constant concentration.

### 11. Taking in all sorts of objects and concentrating upon one object, these two powers being destroyed and manifested—

<summary>This page explains that lower stages of Samādhi, while involving mental control, are still external to the highest "seedless" state because the act of control is itself a mental modification. It emphasizes that constant practice leads to a steady flow of concentration.</summary>

<keywords>Samādhi, seedless Samādhi, mental modifications, Yogi, concentration, Raja Yoga, mental control</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the stages of concentration and the internal states of the mind during Samādhi, following the explanation that constant practice leads to a steady flow of mental control.</meta>

respectively, the *Chitta* gets the modification called *Samādhi*.

The mind is taking up various objects, running into all sorts of things, and then there is a higher state of the mind when it takes up one object and excludes all others. *Samādhi* is the result of that.

### 12. The one-pointedness of the *Chitta* is when it grasps the past and present as one.

How are we to know that the mind has become concentrated? It is because time will vanish. The more time vanishes, the more concentrated we are. In common life, we see that when we are interested in a book, we do not notice the time at all, and when we leave the book, we are often surprised to find how many hours have passed. All time will have the tendency to come and stand in the single present. So the definition is given: the more concentrated the mind, the more the past and present come and stand as one.

### 13. By this is explained the threefold transformations of form, time, and state, in subtle or gross matter, and in the organs.

By this, the threefold changes in the mind-stuff as to form, time, and state are explained. The mind-stuff is changing into *Vṛttis*; this is a change as to form. To be able to hold the changes to the present time is a change as to time. To be able to make the mind-stuff go back to past forms, giving up even the present, is a change as to state. The concentrations taught in the preceding aphorisms were to give the *Yogī* a voluntary control over the transformations of his mind-stuff, which alone will enable him to perform the *Samyama* mentioned before.

<summary>This page defines Samādhi as the state where the mind focuses on a single object, causing the perception of time to vanish as past and present merge into one. It also explains the threefold transformations of mind-stuff—form, time, and state—which a Yogi must control to achieve Samyama.</summary>

<keywords>Chitta, Samādhi, Yoga Aphorisms, Vṛttis, Yogī, Samyama, concentration, transformations of mind-stuff, one-pointedness</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformations of the mind-stuff, moving from the definitions of Samādhi and one-pointedness to the practical application of Samyama on these transformations to gain extraordinary knowledge.</meta>

### 14. That which is acted upon by transformations, either past, present or yet to be manifested, is the qualified.

That is to say, the qualified is the substance which is being acted upon by time and by the *Samskāras* <note>latent impressions or tendencies</note>, and is constantly changing and being manifested.

### 15. The succession of changes is the cause of manifold evolution.

### 16. By making *Samyama* on the three sorts of changes comes the knowledge of past and future.

We must not lose sight of the first definition of *Samyama*. When the mind has reached that state where it identifies itself with the internal impression of the object, leaving the external, and when, through long practice, that is retained by the mind, and the mind can enter into that state in a moment, that is *Samyama*. If a person in that state wants to know the past and future, they have to perform *Samyama* on the changes in the *Samskāras*. Some are active now at present, some have finished their work, and some are waiting to act; so by performing *Samyama* on these, one knows the past and future.

### 17. By making *Samyama* on word, meaning, and knowledge, which are ordinarily confused, comes the knowledge of all animal sounds.

The word represents the external cause, the meaning represents the internal vibration that travels to the brain through the channels of the *Indriyas* <note>the sense organs or powers of perception</note>, conveying the external impression to the mind, and knowledge represents the

<summary>This page explains that substance is "qualified" by its transformations over time and that performing Samyama on these changes allows a practitioner to know the past and future. It also introduces how Samyama on the relationship between words, meanings, and mental impressions grants the ability to understand all animal sounds.</summary>
<keywords>Samyama, Samskāras, evolution, transformations, Indriyas, past and future, animal sounds, Chitta</keywords>

reaction of the mind, from which perception arises. These three, when confused, create our sensory objects. Suppose I hear a word: first, there is the external vibration; next, the internal sensation carried to the mind by the organ of hearing; then, the mind reacts, and I understand the word. The word I know is a mixture of these three: vibration, sensation, and reaction. Ordinarily, these three are inseparable, but through practice, the *Yogi* can separate them. When a person has attained this, if they perform *Samyama* on any sound, they understand the meaning that sound was intended to express, whether it was made by a human or by any other animal.

### 18. By perceiving the impressions, knowledge of past lives is gained.

Each experience we have comes in the form of a wave in the *Chitta*; this wave subsides and becomes finer and finer, but it is never lost. It remains there in a minute form, and if we can bring this wave up again, it becomes memory. So, if the *Yogī* can perform *Samyama* on these past impressions in the mind, he will begin to remember all his past lives.

### 19. By performing *Samyama* on the signs in another’s body, knowledge of that mind is gained.

Suppose every person has particular signs on their body which differentiate them from others; when the *Yogī* performs *Samyama* on these signs peculiar to a certain person, he knows the nature of that person's mind.

### 20. But not its contents, as those were not the object of the *Samyama*.

<summary>This page describes how Samyama allows a Yogi to distinguish between the components of sound to understand all beings, recall past lives by accessing mental impressions, and perceive the nature of another's mind through physical signs.</summary>

# RAJA YOGA

He would not know the contents of the mind simply by performing *Samyama* on the body. A twofold *Samyama* would be required: first on the signs in the body, and then on the mind itself. The *Yogi* would then know everything that is in that mind—past, present, and future.

### 21. By performing *Samyama* on the form of the body, the power of perceiving forms is obstructed. When the power of light in the eye is separated from the object, the *Yogi's* body becomes invisible.

A *Yogi* standing in the middle of this room can apparently vanish. He does not actually disappear, but he will not be seen by anyone. The form and the body are, in a sense, separated. You must remember that this can only be done when the *Yogi* has attained that power of concentration where form and the thing formed have been separated. Then he performs *Samyama* on that, and the power to perceive forms is obstructed, because the power of perceiving forms arises from the connection between the form and the thing formed.

### 22. This also explains the disappearance or concealment of words that are being spoken.

### 23. *Karma* is of two kinds: that which will soon bear fruit, and that which will bear fruit later. By performing *Samyama* on that, or by the signs called *Aristha* <note>portents or omens of death</note>, the *Yogis* know the exact time they will leave their bodies.

When the *Yogi* performs *Samyama* on his own *Karma*—on those impressions in his mind which are currently active, and

36. Enjoyment comes from the lack of discrimination between the soul and *Sattva*, which are very different from each other. The actions of the *Sattva* are for another’s sake; *Samyama* on this provides knowledge of the *Puruśa*.

This power of non-attachment gained through purity gives the *Yogī* the enlightenment called *Pratibhā*.

37. From that arises the knowledge of hearing, touching, seeing, tasting, and smelling that belongs to *Pratibhā*.

38. These are obstacles to *Samādhi*, but they are powers in the worldly state.

If the *Yogī* experiences all these enjoyments of the world, they come from the junction of the *Puruśa* and the mind. If he wishes to perform *Samyama* on the fact that nature and the soul are two different things, he gains knowledge of the *Puruśa*. From that arises discrimination. When he has attained 그 discrimination, he receives *Pratibhā*, the light of supreme genius. These powers, however, are obstacles to the attainment of the highest goal—the knowledge of the pure Self and freedom. These are, as it were, encountered along the way; if the *Yogī* rejects them, he attains the highest. If he is tempted to acquire them, his further progress is barred.

39. When the cause of bondage has been loosened, the *Yogī*, through his knowledge of how the mind manifests through the organs, enters another’s body.

The *Yogī* can enter a dead body and make it get up and move, even while he himself is functioning in another body. Or he can enter a living body and take control of that man’s mind and

It is one part of the universal mind. Now, however, it can only work through the nerve currents in this body, but when the *Yogī* has loosened himself from these nerve currents, he will be able to work through other things.

## 40. By conquering the current called *Udāna*, the *Yogī* does not sink in water or in swamps, and he can walk on thorns.

*Udāna* is the name of the nerve current that governs the lungs and all the upper parts of the body, and when he is master of it, he becomes light in weight. He cannot sink in water; he can walk on thorns and sword blades, stand in fire, and so on.

## 41. By the conquest of the current *Samāna*, he is surrounded by a blaze.

Whenever he likes, light flashes from his body.

## 42. By making *Samyama* on the relation between the ear and the *Ākāśa* comes divine hearing.

There is the *Ākāśa*, the ether, and the instrument, the ear. By performing *Samyama* on them, the *Yogī* gains divine hearing; he hears everything. Anything spoken or sounded miles away, he can hear.

---

43. By performing *Samyama* on the relationship between the *Ākāśa* and the body, the *Yogī*, becoming as light as cotton wool, travels through the skies.

This *Ākāśa* is the material of this body; it is only *Ākāśa* in a certain form that has become the body. If the *Yogī* performs *Samyama* on this *Ākāśa* material of his body, it acquires the lightness of *Ākāśa* and can travel anywhere through the air.

44. By performing *Samyama* on the actual modifications of the mind which exist outside—called "great disembodiness"—comes the disappearance of the covering to light.

In its foolishness, the mind thinks that it is working only within this body. Why should I be bound by one system of nerves and place the Ego only in one body if the mind is omnipresent? There is no reason why I should. The *Yogī* wants to feel the Ego wherever he chooses. When he has succeeded in that, all covering to the light goes away, and all darkness and ignorance vanish. Everything appears to him to be full of knowledge.

45. By performing *Samyama* on the elements, beginning with the gross and ending with the superfine, comes mastery of the elements.

The *Yogī* performs *Samyama* on the elements, first on their gross states and then on their finer states. This *Samyama* is practiced more by a sect of the Buddhists. They take a lump of clay and perform *Samyama* on it, and gradually they begin to see the fine materials of which it is composed; when they have understood all the fine materials within it, they gain power over that element.

<vocab>Samyama, Ākāśa, Yogī, Ego, Buddhists, disembodiness</vocab>

<summary>This page describes how a Yogī attains the ability to fly by mastering the relationship between the body and ether and how they overcome ignorance by realizing the mind's omnipresence. It also explains the process of mastering physical elements by concentrating on their composition from gross to subtle states.</summary>

<keywords>Samyama, Ākāśa, levitation, disembodiness, Ego, elements, Buddhists, mastery, Raja Yoga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of mastering the elements through Samyama and transitions into the resulting physical powers and the mastery of the sensory organs.</meta>

# RAJA YOGA

element. So with all the elements, the *Yogī* can conquer them all.

### 46. From that comes minuteness and the rest of the powers, "glorification of the body," and the indestructibility of bodily qualities.

This means that the *Yogī* has attained the eight powers. He can make himself as small as a particle, or make himself huge, as heavy as the earth, or as light as the air; he will rule everything he wants, he will conquer everything he wants, a lion will sit at his feet like a lamb, and all his desires will be fulfilled at will.

### 47. The glorification of the body includes beauty, complexion, strength, and adamantine hardness.

The body becomes indestructible; fire cannot injure it. Nothing can injure it. Nothing can destroy it until the *Yogī* wishes. “Breaking the rod of time, he lives in this universe with his body.” In the Vedas, it is written that for such a person there is no more disease, death, or pain.

### 48. By performing *Samyama* on the objectivity, knowledge, and egoism of the organs, the conquest of the organs comes gradually.

In the perception of external objects, the organs leave their place in the mind and go toward the object; that is followed by knowledge and egoism. When the *Yogī* performs *Samyama* on these in stages, he conquers the organs. Take anything that you see or feel—a book, for instance—and first concentrate the mind on the object itself. Then concentrate on the knowledge that it is in the form of a book, and then on the Ego that sees the book. By that practice, all the organs will be conquered.

<summary>This page outlines the supernatural physical powers a Yogī attains through mastering the elements, including bodily indestructibility and the ability to conquer the sensory organs through Samyama.</summary>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the powers attained through Samyama, specifically transitioning from the conquest of the physical elements and organs to the mastery of the mind and the ultimate state of Kaivalya.</meta>

# YOGA APHORISMS: POWERS

### 49. From that comes a glorified mind, power of the organs independent of the body, and the conquest of nature.

Just as the conquest of the elements results in a glorified body, so the conquest of the mind will result in a glorified mind.

### 50. By performing *Samyama* on the *Sattva*, the person who has distinguished between the intellect and the *Puruṣa* attains omnipresence and omniscience.

When we have conquered nature and realized the difference between the *Puruṣa* and nature—that the *Puruṣa* is indestructible, pure, and perfect—when the Yogi has realized this, then omnipotence and omniscience follow.

### 51. By giving up even these, the very seed of evil is destroyed; he attains *Kaivalya*.

He attains aloneness and independence. Then that man is free. When he gives up even the ideas of omnipotence and omniscience, there will be an entire rejection of enjoyment and of the temptations from celestial beings. When the *Yogī* has seen all these wonderful powers and rejected them, he reaches the goal. What are all these powers? Simply manifestations. They are no better than dreams. Even omnipotence is a dream. It depends on the mind. As long as there is a mind, it can be understood, but the goal is beyond even the mind.

### 52. The *Yogī* should not feel allured or flattered by the invitations of celestial beings, for fear of falling into evil again.

<vocab>Samyama, Sattva, Puruṣa, Yogi, Kaivalya, omniscience, omnipotence, Yoga Aphorisms</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that mastering the mind leads to omniscience and omnipotence, but the ultimate goal of Kaivalya (liberation) is only reached by rejecting even these powers and celestial temptations. The text emphasizes that all powers are mere manifestations of the mind, while the true self lies beyond the mind.</summary>

<keywords>Samyama, Sattva, Puruṣa, Kaivalya, omniscience, omnipotence, spiritual liberation, celestial beings, conquest of nature, mental powers</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the warnings against the temptations of celestial beings and explains how to achieve higher discrimination through Samyama on time to overcome ignorance.</meta>

# RAJA YOGA

There are other dangers as well; gods and other beings come to tempt the *Yogī*. They do not want anyone to be perfectly free. They are jealous, just as we are, and sometimes even worse than us. They are very much afraid of losing their positions. Those *Yogīs* who do not reach perfection die and become gods; leaving the direct road, they turn into side paths and gain these powers. Then they must be born again; but he who is strong enough to withstand these temptations and go straight to the goal becomes free.

->53. By performing *Samyama* on a moment of time and its succession, discrimination is gained.<-

How are we to avoid all these things—these *Devas*, and heavens, and powers? By discrimination; by knowing good from evil. Therefore, a *Samyama* is provided by which the power of discrimination can be strengthened. This is done by performing *Samyama* on a moment of time.

->54. Even those things which cannot be differentiated by species, sign, or place will be distinguished by the above *Samyama*.<-

The misery we suffer comes from ignorance—from a lack of discrimination between the real and the unreal. We all take the bad for the good and the dream for reality. The Soul is the only reality, and we have forgotten it. The body is an unreal dream, and we think we are all bodies. This lack of discrimination is the cause of misery, and it is caused by ignorance. When discrimination arrives, it brings strength, and only then can we avoid all these various ideas of the body, heavens, gods, and *Devas*. This ignorance arises through differentiating by species, sign, or place. For instance, take a cow. A cow is differentiated from a dog by its species. Even among cows

<summary>This page warns that gods and celestial beings may tempt the Yogī to stray from the path of liberation, and it explains that true freedom is found through discrimination. By performing Samyama on moments of time, one gains the ability to distinguish the real Soul from the unreal body and avoid the misery caused by ignorance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the power of discrimination (Viveka) gained through Samyama, specifically how it allows the Yogi to distinguish between the composite nature of the universe and the pure, simple essence of the Soul (Puruṣa).</meta>

<header>YOGA APHORISMS: POWERS</header>

alone, how do we make the distinction between one cow and another? By signs. If two objects are exactly similar, they can be distinguished if they are in different places. When objects are so mixed up that even these *differentiæ* <note>distinguishing characteristics</note> will not help us, the power of discrimination acquired by the above-mentioned practice will give us the ability to distinguish them. The highest philosophy of the *Yogī* is based upon this fact: that the *Puruṣa* is pure and perfect, and is the only "simple" <note>an indivisible, non-composite entity</note> that exists in this universe. The body and mind are compounds, and yet we are always identifying ourselves with them. That is the great mistake—that the distinction has been lost. When this power of discrimination has been attained, one sees that everything in this world, mental and physical, is a compound, and, as such, cannot be the *Puruṣa*.

->55. The saving knowledge is that knowledge of<-
->discrimination which covers all objects and all<-
->means.<-

Saving, because the knowledge takes the *Yogi* across the ocean of birth and death. The whole of *Prakriti* in all its states, subtle and gross, is within the grasp of this knowledge. There is no sequence in perfection by this knowledge: it takes in all things simultaneously, at a single glance.

->55. By the similarity of purity between the<-
->*Sattva* and the *Puruṣa* comes *Kaivalya*.<-

When the soul realizes that it depends on nothing in the universe, from gods to the lowest atom, that state is called *Kaivalya* (isolation) and perfection. It is attained when this mixture of purity and impurity called the mind has been made as pure as the *Puruṣa* Itself; then the *Sattva*, the mind, reflects only the unqualified essence of purity, which is the *Puruṣa*.

<meta>This page begins Chapter IV, moving from the conclusion of the previous discussion on the state of Kaivalya (isolation) and the purity of the mind to the various methods by which spiritual powers (Siddhis) are attained.</meta>

## CHAPTER IV.
### INDEPENDENCE

1. **The *Siddhis* (powers) are attained by birth, chemical means, power of words, austerity, or concentration.**

Sometimes a person is born with the *Siddhis* (powers), which result from the exercise of powers in a previous birth. In this life, they are born, as it were, to enjoy the fruits of those previous efforts. It is said of *Kapila*, the great father of the *Sānkhya Philosophy*, that he was a born *Siddha*, which literally means a person who has attained success.

The *Yogīs* claim that these powers can be gained by chemical means. All of you know that chemistry originally began as alchemy; people searched for the philosopher’s stone, elixirs of life, and so forth. In India, there was a sect called the *Rāṣāyanas*. Their idea was that ideals, knowledge, spirituality, and religion were all perfectly fine, but the body was the only instrument by which to attain them. If the body broke down from time to time, it would take much more time to reach the goal. For instance, a person wants to practice *Yoga* or wants to become spiritual. Before they have advanced very far, they die. Then they take another body and begin again, then die, and so on; in this way, much time will be lost in the process of dying and being reborn. If the body could be made strong and perfect so that it could escape birth and death, we would have much more time to become spiritual. So these *Rāṣāyanas* say to first make the body very strong, and they claim that this body can be made immortal. The idea is that if the mind is
